- (sv) mr president, the report we are dealing with does not in itself imply major changes. most of the amendments are purely technical. nevertheless, it is worth pointing out that whenever we take a decision of this kind, it is good for the environment, in this case because it will create better conditions for the functioning of the internal market. large quantities of dangerous goods are transported via the roads, railway network and ports of the european union. it is therefore necessary to adopt appropriate regulations to govern such transport. we are gradually, sector by sector, getting member states to apply common minimum rules. this is a very positive development and we have every reason to thank the rapporteur, mr koch, for the work he has done on this subject. it is also an important issue in the context of the conditions under which the internal market operates. if we are to make the common transport market really work at union level, it is important not only that there are rules, but that, as far as possible, we have common rules. finally, i would like to comment on a third point which i think is also essential, namely an amendment tabled by mr ari vatanen. the context in our various member states is in many ways different. the purpose of this amendment is to take account of the extreme cold weather which can be experienced in the northern regions of the union. this is a special circumstance which requires that the effect of these very low temperatures on the goods and their packaging be taken into account. mr president, it is a good thing that this regulation is being made more flexible. i hope the commission will accept this amendment.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial interests are protected. - (sv) mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the internal market is not complete. subsidies, monopolies and barriers to competition continue to hamper markets and economic development itself. national governments give new subsidies, promising to give the last, and it is invariably the same. subsidies distort the intended effect of our financial efforts, both within and between countries. the gradual dismantling of state aid is necessary, and more and more markets must be opened up to competition. this principle must be applied to both private and public monopolies. it is the public monopolies that most often resist. increased competition and business creation are expected to have positive effects on well-being - including in the areas of training, healthcare and social services. public monopolies must be replaced by competitive structures. europe must be modernised, adapted to the laws of entrepreneurship, to a more competitive environment, for the benefit of consumers and businesses alike. effective competition puts pressure on prices and raises living standards. our consumption policy is far too little concerned with the price level. competition policy and consumer policy must go hand in hand. the internal market is the basis of our work. the legislation governing it must be the same for all, for small countries as for large ones. a systematic review of national regulations is necessary to be able to eliminate obstacles to competition. the european regulations themselves may well merit an analysis in this respect. the new model currently being tested by the commission should not result in a pure process of nationalisation, which would nullify the effects of our competition policy. to be effective, it must be deeply rooted in the minds of the national authorities. it might be useful to analyse the results towards the middle of the year, but also to examine more closely the impact of the new situation on the role of the commission. the question of how we should proceed next was, until further notice, answered by the idea of organising a global institutional congress, where there would be an unconditional debate with the widest perspectives between representatives of the various interests involved. this will give us the opportunity to develop new principles or to review the changes we have discussed in greater depth. it will also be possible to find new common solutions and analyse the proposed amendments which have been the subject of debate in the parliamentary committee. the adaptation of competition law must be satisfactory. a poorly adapted competition policy can be responsible for a setback from the legal point of view and undermine the right to property, a fundamental principle which we must safeguard. we are faced here with a rather exciting debate. a conference where the various issues are reviewed helps to clear up misunderstandings and, at the same time, perhaps also to obtain improvements on certain points. parliament and the commission can jointly step up their efforts towards an effective competition policy, and thus create new opportunities and new resources for citizens. in my constituency in stockholm we have many good local examples of increased supply and improved quality which have been achieved by competition in areas which were previously entirely in the hands of monopolies. we call for the continuation of the open debate which was intensified during the discussion of the von wogau and rapkay reports. we hope that legal considerations will be given the importance they should in a state governed by the rule of law.
- (sv) mr president, the eu is now under strict surveillance. trust in the european union has deteriorated considerably. strong action is needed to remedy this. we therefore welcome the increased ourselves, however, is to find the right balance. the result of the fraud and negligence which are taking place must not be to lock us into a hierarchical and outdated bureaucracy, where excessive control over details prevents progress towards a modern administration. we therefore welcome the offensive perspective which characterizes the van hulten report. the aim of the approach is to give each administration responsibility for its own control. our experience of modern administrative functioning shows that openness, decentralisation of responsibilities and a truly competent evaluation are just as effective as bureaucratic and fastidious control. the major challenge here is to create a modern and efficient administration without losing anything in terms of legal certainty or our capacity for control. this requires specific training, more modern recruitment methods and, above all, transparency and openness in the system. the best protection against irregularities is the possibility of effective supervision of the administration.

- (sv) mr president, the motion for a resolution under discussion today asks, as before, that the agenda for the next intergovernmental conference be extended to include profound reforms of the institutions. the next enlargement of the union is cited as justification for this. i'm surprised because it's a misinterpretation. a federal and centralistic eu that increasingly governs the member states is actually less likely to expand. a flexible eu that focuses on fewer but essential areas and respects national differences and national democracies is more likely to encompass many more countries. the resolution calls for a more efficient and transparent economic policy at eu level. but it is impossible to talk about this without talking about monetary union. the current text does not mention the major democratic and political problems of monetary union and the central bank - that is indefensible. if we want a more democratic eu, we must rethink the whole design of emu. the central bank must be placed under political control so that monetary policy can be directed by a political objective such as employment growth or welfare; to this end, the transparency of the central bank must be improved. the stagnant and failed monetarism of the stability pact must be reviewed and rejected if we are to have a homogeneous policy which puts the objective of welfare first. article 56 of the treaty, which prohibits all interference with the free movement of capital, currency speculation, must be abolished so that dangerous currency speculation is curbed by political control. most eu countries now have governments dominated by the social democrats. it is remarkable that none of them demand a change in the direction of monetary union now that they have the opportunity to do so. the declaration that monetary union could be a counterweight to the globalisation of capital also undermines credibility. the resolution calls for the european parliament to be given greater power over the intergovernmental conference. it is important to emphasize, however, that the intergovernmental conference is and must be a conference between member states. it is the parliaments of the member states, or the people in the popular vote, which must lead the development of the treaty. the european parliament is therefore not given any formal influence in the negotiation or ratification process.

- (sv) mr president, the proposal for a council directive has two fundamental questions. the first concerns the absolute responsibility of manufacturers to take back end-of-life vehicles. this could create a monopoly situation in the dismantling business. this is particularly true in regions of the eu where distances are long and where many small firms are involved in dismantling. i think that the eu directive should not, whatever the subject, be disadvantageous to small businesses in the union. unlimited manufacturers' liability may even lead to the disappearance of the market for used car parts. a manufacturer has a vested interest in selling new parts. but the trade is vital, especially for those who collect and refurbish old vehicles. this principle is also arbitrary outside the principles of the market economy. companies must be able to be transformed, sold and liquidated. they must be able to establish themselves in new markets, but also to leave old ones. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. the other principle in the council's common position is the retroactivity feature in the proposal for a directive. the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter, but it is not yet clear whether the commission intends to take a decision on the matter. it also changes the owner's liability retroactively. a consumer may have changed product over the years in many ways. at present, all eu countries have laws regulating the destruction of cars. among these, there can be good and bad countries. until this eu directive is interpreted, eu countries must, each on their own, be responsible for the destruction of vehicles as best as possible, so that the retroactivity in eu legislation is not applied. this is not in contradiction with a funding model with fund-raising.

the rapporteur. - (sv) mr president, the question of the social protection system is of paramount importance. this is a natural consequence of cooperation within the eu. it is based on monetary cooperation, which has since been followed by macroeconomic cooperation and cooperation on employment policy. the logical next step is to develop the social protection system. these areas are interdependent. economic stability is a basis for progress and leads to a high employment rate. it also offers the opportunity to develop well-being. however, the reverse is also true: a well-developed social protection policy can contribute to higher employment and growth. in other words, social protection is a productive factor. there are differences in the social protection system within the eu. many of them are historical. however, we now face a number of common challenges. this is particularly the case with demographic change, which is not only related to the fact that the number of elderly people is increasing, but also to the fact that the number of births is decreasing. the labour market is also changing. women are largely in the labour market. another challenge is technological progress. we must face these challenges together. the commission communication recommends a greater exchange of experience, as well as the setting up of a high-level group and benchmarking. in addition, the social protection report must be published annually and examined together with the employment report. that's good, but it's not enough. we propose a procedure similar to the luxembourg process. clear guidelines and indicators are needed, while member states should develop methods in national action plans. we are building a common model, but we are maintaining subsidiarity. this social convergence is a process which will continue for a long time. so parliament must be involved in the work - not by entering the high-level group, but in such a way that the representative of parliament can follow the work in this group and come up with proposals on how to continue the process. the commission proposal, like cooperation for employment, is based on four main objectives. the aim is to make the system more employment-friendly, to make the pension system sustainable in the long term, to promote social integration and to guarantee a high and sustainable level of health protection. all of these points are important. in our report we have particularly stressed the importance of fighting poverty in the eu and of finding indicators as soon as possible in order to make progress in this area. equality between men and women does not have its own pillar. it is mainstreaming that is being discussed. the four pillars must therefore include aspects of equality between men and women. we have noted this. we are putting forward a whole range of proposals, for example, for a shift to individualised social protection systems, for parental leave to give entitlement to social security benefits, for better opportunities to reconcile family and working life and for basic old age pensions to ensure a decent standard of living. these proposals are primarily in favour of women. in parallel with this european social convergence strategy, a concrete plan is needed for legislative work in the social field, as well as proposals for a framework agreement in the context of the social dialogue with a concrete timetable. we expect parliament to put this into its proposal for a social action programme. social convergence is also necessary in the context of enlargement, on the one hand to strengthen the social model and on the other hand to eliminate the risks of social dumping. the commission should consider the development of a specific action programme relating to social policy in the context of enlargement. we had a constructive discussion at the committee. we had many amendments, but we were able to agree on one report. the number of amendments for the vote in parliament is now reduced to 15. this is because we have a significant convergence. some of these amendments have been read out to committee and i shall reject them. i shall also reject the amendments tabled by the tdi group, because i do not think they add anything to the report. on the other hand, my group will vote for amendment no 14 by the liberal group. i think it's a good offer. it is similar to amendment no 1, but it is better. in conclusion, i would like to say that the eu is not only concerned with trade cooperation. the eu is also a union for employment and social justice. social policy plays a vital role in creating a people's europe. with this report, parliament wants to contribute to strengthening the social dimension in european society. we hope that the council and the commission will take this contribution seriously and incorporate it into future social policy. the lisbon summit is a unique opportunity to present such a strategy.

the idea of a social europe is a product of study, far removed from the real world. social policy is basically a national process. in my country we have learned that much of social protection has to development of almost all social services. community democracy is the framework for social protection. now that we have economic coordination, i certainly think that greater coordination of social concerned by social problems.

the rapporteur. - (sv) mr president, we must now take a position on an essential issue, an international treaty, a subject which could influence the development of prosperity in many member states. in my native sweden, this is one of the most important export sectors. it is about music and phonograms. it's about artists' copyright, to protect their artistic work. the treaties on copyright and on interpretation and performance and on phonograms are discussed. without intellectual property rights protection, economic motivation and creativity are curbed. the fakes are big business. in europe alone, piracy is estimated to cost €4.5 billion. the problem is that many jobs in the entertainment industry are in the red. this is a historic treaty, since the eu is a contracting party, even though the individual member states, not the eu as such, are members of the organisation. there is a specific provision requiring parliament's assent in two cases. in the case of this treaty, both conditions are met. it creates a new institution, an assembly, which is then responsible for monitoring the treaty and its consequences. the treaty also covers matters which are dealt with in accordance with the codecision procedure. parliament must therefore give its assent, since this is within its competence. the future will tell us whether it is appropriate, through this new parliament, to reduce the commission's powers in this area. at the moment, parliament cannot do anything. as regards literary works, the treaty complements the berne convention. international legal protection is developed for distribution, sale, rental, public performance and making available to the public. in addition, we are creating protection for software and databases. however, there is a problem with the concept of "author". for example, this is not defined. the storage issue also poses a certain problem. this one's not solved either. it is dealt with by a general clause, which has no definition. if we do not clarify these issues, it could lead to a different approach to them around the world, without homogeneity. that would not be a good solution. the question of arbitration also needs to be resolved. parliament cannot change these particularities. we can only say yes or no to the treaty as it stands. yes or no to a treaty with loopholes. as rapporteur, i think, and the committee too, that we must say yes. we have here a treaty which, despite its shortcomings, is an important step forward for copyright. we have had a long discussion about whether parliament and the eu should have powers in the area of patents in the area of intellectual property law. i think you do. however, there is still no community legislation in the field of patent law and protection of designs and models, but it is necessary. a european patent is on its way and that is a good thing. the union and the member states are now taking the first steps together in the field of intellectual property law. it's a good thing for us to have the power to do that, even if we're not fully satisfied. we can thus serve as an example to the applicant countries. we have a direct economic interest in these questions being settled before enlargement. i would like to thank the commission for its efforts in this area and for the cooperation with the commission officials, which has been absolutely outstanding. finally, i would also like to thank parliament for having the kindness to listen to these few words at this late hour.

in writing. -{sv) we believe that the report contains many valid proposals, and we support them, but we are very critical of the prospect of convergence which is implied in the whole text. we are particularly opposed to the paragraphs dealing with tax harmonization and the idea that the commission should guide the approach taken by the member states (see for example paragraph 7). we would like to emphasize that social protection policy is essentially a matter for the member states, even if the enlargement of the eu requires more coordination than was the case before.
in writing. - (sv) we abstained in the vote on mr jan andersson's report on the implementation of a concerted strategy for the modernisation of social protection. our position towards the european union is, in principle, positive. as members of the swedish liberal party, we see in european integration the possibility of finding solutions to problems that transcend national borders, such as the environment, trade issues, movement between countries, human rights and conflict resolution. on these issues, european democracies have an opportunity to prove to the world that cooperation leads to peace and prosperity. we also adhere to the principle of subsidiarity, which means that decisions should be taken as closely as possible to those who are affected. that is why we are actively working towards the introduction of a european constitution which makes the division of responsibilities clear and unambiguous for all to see. it must be absolutely clear to all citizens that the eu must confine itself to the issues it knows best - those that go beyond the borders of each country. all other issues must be dealt with at local, regional or national level. social policy is one example of an area where the eu should have only limited competence, except when it comes to the free movement of people within the union. each member state must be fully responsible for decisions concerning its social protection system. social security, social measures for children and the elderly, for example, are obviously subjects which are not directly intended to cross national borders. the same applies to employment policy. we do not believe that european legislation in these areas is a solution to the problems of our social protection systems. we believe it is important that the eu should concentrate its efforts on a small number of issues where its intervention can really be useful. this does not, however, prevent member states from cooperating and exchanging ideas in the social field. issues relating to social protection and gender equality have traditionally been among the topics considered as priorities by the liberal group. at national level, we wholeheartedly support many of the ideas put forward by mr andersson in his report.
in writing. -{v) the fundamental values common to our member states, on which the european union is based, are democracy, respect for human rights and freedoms, and the choice of a market economy with a social character. these values must be expressed in solidarity with the most vulnerable groups in our society and in the world around us. in accordance with the principle of subsidiarity, it is up to the member states themselves to decide on their social protection systems. however, people living in the european union must be able to benefit from a minimum social protection network, regardless of the country in which they reside. the report expresses the wish for "efficient and ambitious fiscal coordination" but does not specify the scope of this. we believe that such a position is an excessive interference in national tax policy and we cannot therefore approve it. we shall therefore vote against paragraph 7 of the report. the report emphasizes the social dimension of european cooperation, which we welcome. nevertheless, it is important to clarify the role of the eu in social protection. we regret that this clarification is not in the text of the report.

the rapporteur. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, you are all the more welcome here, since you are so few in number. one might think that a case handled at such a late hour is of little importance, but that is not the case. this is a practical and practical matter, since it is our common currency that is being lost. how many people know that this currency is counterfeited and that we are in an absolutely unacceptable situation in this respect? we cannot allow our common currency to lose its value because it is counterfeited. this is why the ecb, ecofin, europol, olaf and the commissioner responsible for legal affairs have all recognised the need to take swift and joint action to close the gaps in our legislation. it is easy to forget that the old values can be exchanged twenty years after the introduction of the new currency. it is therefore important to include these values in the criminal law part of this action programme. this addition was requested by parliament. the council should have given parliament the three months it was supposed to give, including the last document that was adopted on this subject, but we all know how difficult it is to implement these measures. since the matter will be discussed by the council in march, it is good that parliament now gives its opinion on the proposal so that the decision to be taken in march will also reflect parliament's views to the member states. if one of our countries were to start creating complications, it would have to take responsibility both morally and economically for the delay that this might cause. that would be very ill-intentioned. on 1 january 2002 the euro will be in circulation. the whole of the union must be afforded the same protection, one which makes it impossible to counterfeit not only the securities in circulation but also those which will be put into circulation at a later date. it is important that we have it in time, in all countries with similar conditions. the commission has proposed information and prevention measures, exchanges of experience and penal measures. this requires a certain - necessary - approximation of our national provisions, the minimum maximum penalty for counterfeiting being eight years. the sale and possession of counterfeiting equipment will also be considered as criminal acts. we will simply set a minimum level for the most severe sentences. the respective values of the euro are well known. modern computer equipment can cause serious harm if we do not take precautions. parliament has tabled a number of amendments to this proposal, notably with regard to the competent courts, a text stipulating that for the same criminal case, the indictment and trial must take place in one country. we have also taken steps against companies involved in counterfeiting. the new candidate countries are asked to adapt their legislation to these rules. it took the council and the commission two years to develop this proposal. it is absolutely necessary that the decision be taken in march. it would be irresponsible to delay it, or even risk the euro deteriorating in the meantime. it would also be deceiving our fellow citizens, who will soon have to handle these new banknotes that they do not know. it is thought that the fake currency will particularly harm countries outside the euro area, where there is less awareness of the value of the new currency. these countries are also covered by the protection measures and their views have been taken into account. the proposal to take responsibility for our common interests is therefore marked by a spirit of solidarity and reciprocity which binds us. the council is due to meet in march to take this decision. thank you, colleagues, for listening to me. i also thank those who have helped to ensure that this matter can be dealt with quickly - i am thinking in particular of the pse group.
the rapporteur. - (sv) mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i hope that parliament is ready today to decide on the extension and strengthening of the european financial market. as a new member of parliament, i found it very interesting and useful to be entrusted with this important task. i would like to thank my colleagues in the committee on economic and monetary affairs for their willingness to find compromises, for the attentive reception given by the president and the bureau to the complexity of the matter, for the positive contacts we have had with the commission, in particular with the commissioner responsible for the dossier, and with the council, and for the disinterested work done by the secretariat of the committee on economic and monetary affairs. we have a great deal of agreement in our committee, with the exception of one point for which i hope a solution will be found. the issue of ucits may appear difficult, technical and politically quite risky. in my opinion, not everyone knows what ucits are. however, let me emphasize forcefully that this is a question that really concerns european citizens, because it is a question of how their savings will be managed, what are the existing choices, how inter-community competition will work with regard to the different types of savings, whether savers are entitled to some security as investors, and what opportunities are available to european citizens to prepare for old age through savings advice. it is also a question of weighing the demand for liberalisation to make the market more efficient and increase profits against the need to protect investors. during the 1980s most markets were radically deregulated, the main consequence of which was the very rapid development of national financial and bond markets in a few years. collective investment funds have emerged as an alternative to individual bank savings. sixty percent of swedes save through various investment funds. this type of saving provides security to the investor because of the diversity of investments. the capital market and the bond market continued to expand. the current ucits directive has become obsolete. new types of investment funds have been introduced in the different countries, but the ucits concerned are not allowed to market their units in other member states. this restriction of course hampers competition and innovation and limits the public's saving choices. the commission proposal under discussion today has two parts. the first concerns products, i.e. investment funds. the second part deals with investment agencies. the first proposal is essentially aimed at extending investment opportunities for these bodies by allowing them to invest in assets owned by other investment firms, for example, units of other ucits, financial futures and standardised options, deposits with credit institutions and certain types of money market instruments. special rules for the allocation of risks, based on both quantitative and qualitative criteria, are provided for these investments. the proposal also calls for the introduction of specific rules for the allocation of risk for bodies whose purpose is to reproduce stock market indices, index funds. the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy is strengthening the requirements in relation to the commission's proposal. in order to ensure transparent operation and sound financial management, investments in non-harmonised funds should only be allowed if qualitative criteria, such as transparency and investor protection, are implemented. in addition, quantitative criteria (risk exposure) should be applied. let me turn, mr president, to the point on which there is some disagreement, and which i hope will be overcome in the future, namely, otc derivatives. the commission distinguishes in its proposal between derivatives which are traded on a regulated market and those which are not. the latter are called otc derivatives. according to the commission proposal, otc derivatives do not fall within the investment categories envisaged for ucits; they must be used only for the purpose of sound portfolio management and the reduction of exchange rate risks. the market for financial derivatives has grown very rapidly in almost all member states in recent years. it is therefore important that the rules applicable to derivatives are flexible in order to avoid a closed market in the future. the definitions of 'standardised financial futures' and 'standardised options' should therefore be brought together in a new definition of 'financial derivatives', which also covers over-the-counter derivatives. investments in over-the-counter derivatives should, however, be permitted only if both quantitative (risk exposure to certain counterparties and total risk exposure) and qualitative criteria are applied. these criteria are particularly vital in this case. they should be put in place to promote investor protection. the second commission proposal is aimed at those who manage ucits, namely management companies. the provisions concerning them, in particular the conditions required for them to be able to start and continue their activity, are adapted to the regulations in force. it is essential to be able to put in place rules on so-called simplified prospectuses. anyone considering acquiring units in a fund should be offered relevant information in a language that is understandable to investors in the country concerned. the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy wants to go further than the commission in this respect. we also discussed in committee the criteria for the capital injection required of management companies. there are two sides to every story here. i think it is important to ensure that common rules do not exclude smaller companies. in the months i have been working on this report, a very clear demand has been made, both by the financial sector and by the organisations which represent consumers. the current situation is not healthy. savers and the financial industry need modernised rules across europe. i believe that today's proposal will strike a good balance between the desire to liberalise the market and to provide effective protection for investors. in conclusion, i would like to say that some of our colleagues think that this proposal goes too far, while others regret that i am not sufficiently liberal. i'll just tell them: you can't have it all.
in writing. - (sv) mr olle schmidt's report is an improvement on the commission's proposal. i am not opposed to an update of the common minimum rules, which would allow investment firms to be given the so-called european passport. however, i do not think it would be reasonable to do so until all the member states have adopted commissioner monti's proposal on minimum taxes on capital. the report, as it stands, will only have the effect of increasing the liberalization of capital markets, whereas the most important thing is to control them. in these circumstances, like all the members of the green group, i voted against the proposal to amend directive 1 and abstained on the proposal to amend directive 2.

- (sv) mr president, the postal sector is still an area where outdated monopolies are still allowed to persist, in defiance of all the rules of competition. the commission has not been able to act with sufficient vigour on this matter and this situation has persisted for far too long. in any other area, the community competition authority has intervened, but it has not done so in this one. we have waited in vain for 18 months for the commission to submit a proposal. the question i would now like to put to the commission is this: when will we have a new, modern postal system capable of operating throughout the union? one of our founding principles is that of the internal market. we are currently working intensively to extend it to an increasing number of member states. this objective requires efficiency, and efficiency requires competition. in all sectors, one after the other, deregulation and liberalization are now a necessity. the eu is often the first to fight for this objective. the commission is making this progress in an exemplary way. and if it does, it is because we know from experience that the old monopolies do not meet the expectations of a modern society for a good quality service. it also does so in order to keep up with technological developments and, finally, to be able to bring down prices, knowing that all this is more easily obtained on a free market, open to competition. this is how things work in a whole range of different sectors - but unfortunately the postal business is a major exception. this cannot be attributed to customer satisfaction. it is well known that the increase in rates and the delay in the delivery of mail are constantly fuelling the debate. if we really want to have a functioning internal market, we cannot continue to grant derogations to postal activities. several countries have taken the lead in allowing more free competition in the postal sector. the netherlands, finland and sweden are the most advanced countries in this field. during the 1990's they demonstrated that this approach has many advantages. in sweden, this gradual development has been the subject of a broad political consensus. it is important to continue along this path and to take the necessary incentive measures. experience shows that the old monopolies do not live up to the standards we as customers are all entitled to in terms of efficient service. we have received a letter from supporters of the preservation of this monopoly before the start of this debate. they make sweden a deterrent example. it is possible that this is justified in a number of contexts, but not on this particular point. a circular stated that the price of the stamp had increased by 59 percent since privatization in 1993. this is true if we consider the question in the light of the 20% of the total postal services market where the monopoly has in practice been maintained, i.e. where the state is the sole actor. for the 80 per cent of the market which operates under free competition, prices have fallen. this shows that competition really works. my conclusion is that we must ensure that competition extends to the remaining 20% of the market. it is in this situation that an increasing number of people wish to... - (sv) mr president, at the second international conference of socialist women held in copenhagen in 1910, it was decided that the main demand of the time, namely the right to vote, should be linked to the question of women's rights as a whole, and that it was appropriate to 'give an international character and to prepare well for women's day'. it is interesting to note that this women's day was subsequently banned during the war in all countries where fascism and racism had come to power. in 1910, women's suffrage was on the agenda. today, the focus is on the number of women who can participate in decision-making processes. women are not a homogeneous community. they belong to different social groups. they have different individual needs and interests. however, from a structural and general point of view, it appears that women, wherever they may be, whatever social category, ethnicity or religion they may belong to, whether at home or at work, and in any area of society, are subordinate to men - i might add: with a few individual exceptions. in my country, sweden, several political parties have in recent years voluntarily introduced quotas for the participation of women and men in political assemblies. the consequence has been an increase in the number of women in policy-making bodies, which in turn has opened up new possibilities for studying the issue. what happens when more women take an active part, on an equal footing with men, in political decision-making? what we have seen so far is that the agenda changes - and when the agenda changes, so obviously do the types of decision. as soon as the question of quotas is raised, the word 'competence' is automatically mentioned. but it is interesting to note that the criteria for competence are rarely defined. it often seems that men, as in the past, have the least clearly defined competence. so being a man would be synonymous with being more competent. this attitude, in my view, shows how widespread the incompetence is among today's decision-makers. we expressed our desire to make 8 march an international holiday before this house at the beginning of the year. until this demand is heard, i would like to wish all socialist women much success with the women's world marches, which will begin this spring on the occasion of international women's day. this is an action that will be carried out worldwide, on the themes of violence against women and poverty. i would like to end by repeating the point made by commissioner patten at the end of the previous debate: the ideal police force would be a grandmother of colour. there's no such thing as a black-lettered grandmother, but there are black grandmothers.

- (sv) mr president, like many other members, i should like to thank mr katiforis for this interesting report. reading it and discovering the picture of the european economy as it is presented, i have the feeling that the situation is rather worrying. the level of investment is too low, unemployment remains too high and growth is insufficient. it is therefore not surprising that the euro continues to lose ground to other currencies, even though the european central bank, according to some rumors, is secretly buying euros to support its currency. the rapporteur notes that stability is not an end in itself, and i agree with him. there is far too much talk about stability, convergence and harmonization, and far too little about the factors of innovation and development. i think that cooperation within the framework of emu is a colossal undertaking which is having a hard time keeping up with the pace of change in an economy which is highly mobile. however, my conclusion is totally different from that of the previous speaker. i think we must rely on human resources, on the one hand, through a macro-political approach that stimulates demand and creates new job opportunities, and on the other hand - and perhaps above all - by relying on measures that affect the working environment, such as training, the democratization of enterprises and the improvement of social relations. in this respect, the weakness of the emu project seems to me to be glaring. we cannot use the baron de münchhausen's method of rebuilding the european economies from the ground up. on the contrary, it is necessary to mobilize the workers and the populations of all the member states at the grassroots level.

in writing. - (sv) we decided to abstain in the vote on amendment no 4 to the katiforis report on the economy of the union (1999). our position in principle is that competition between different national tax systems is not always a bad thing and that member states should, as far as possible, retain their right to decide in this area. at national level, we believe it is urgent that taxation of labour and business should be reduced to levels equivalent to those in our competitors, but this is a matter for the swedish parliament, not the european parliament. the fact that several countries are looking for different solutions in the field of taxation and social protection policy gives the opportunity to adapt these to national specificities and to test different social protection systems. tax harmonization at european level should only be possible in exceptional cases, for example for certain environmental or capital taxes. we agree that a certain degree of harmonization is necessary for the internal market to function, but we believe that amendment no 4 goes far too far and is lacking in too many nuances.

the rapporteur. - (sv) mr president, since the brundtland report in 1987, it is clear that a policy which focuses solely on economic growth cannot be a long-term policy. the challenge for all of us is to integrate social and environmental perspectives. this means that the environmental perspective cannot be considered as a sectoral issue, but that any negative consequences of an activity in the field of production, transport, construction, etc., must be identified during the planning phase and minimized through various measures. following the rio conference, an action programme was drawn up which highlights, for each sector, the most important problems in this respect and indicates how the environment and natural resources should be protected. although the document is several hundred pages long, it is generally consistent. to further this necessary integration of ecological thinking into our politics, new methods must be developed. it is also necessary to select indicators which enable us to know at all times what actions are being carried out, what programmes of dissemination of knowledge are being carried out, what pilot projects are being carried out, etc. this type of work has already been carried out for many years in many oecd countries. it could be given more priority, but it is in the process. this is rarely the case in most developing countries. in these countries, the knowledge and resources to integrate the environmental perspective are lacking, as is the spirit of prevention that is the essence of the rio decision. in such a context, union aid can play an important role. we have a great responsibility in general to pass on knowledge and techniques which can be used over the long term, that is to say, which do not damage the environment or natural resources. the purpose of the proposed regulation under discussion today is to ensure that the eu fully integrates the environmental perspective into its aid. the aim, of course, is that this type of integration or mainstreaming, to use the term, should be done automatically, that is to say, without subsidies or special programmes. however, there is still a lot to be done in terms of methods to be developed. in particular, knowledge and skills need to be improved, both within the commission and in the countries receiving aid. i was responsible for preparing this second reading. i have, of course, relied largely on the experience we had and on the work done before the first reading. parliament had tabled a large number of amendments at that time, about a third of which had been accepted by the council before the second reading. in analysing the situation, i have identified three or four points on which it seems to me that parliament should try to work out amendments to the council's proposal. the first is the size of the budget, the second is the way in which we are conducting our work and the third is the way in which the regulations are to be interpreted. the commission's proposal is to apply the principle of subsidiarity to the activities of the developing countries and to those of the eu member states. this list does not mean that the other questions raised in the first reading are not important, but we should concentrate on those which are most important. in addition, in order to reach agreement with the council, we must try to ignore, as far as possible, the less important issues. i am pleased to note that informal discussions have led to a considerable increase in the budget, from the proposed 50 million euro to about 90 million euro. i think we must support such a development, as amendment no 11 calls for. as regards the duration of the regulation, i do not see any reason why we should make a definitive commitment on this point. the council's proposal seems acceptable to me, since it provides for an evaluation to be carried out in four or five years. whatever the outcome, the matter will then be referred back to parliament. finally, i would like to mention one point on which there is still a difference of opinion. this is what is known as comitology, that is to say, the way in which the actual implementation of these regulations is to be carried out. i think we should try to move away from a system where the member states themselves are responsible for implementing this in every detail. member states should be involved in the development of a framework and rules, but leave it to the commission to carry out the project itself. to this end, we have proposed in this reading the choice of what has been called split comitology, an approach which should serve an excellent purpose. i hope that this will also be the proposal of parliament as it will be expressed in the vote. i just want to add one thing. it turns out we're gonna need some additional consultations. i would therefore like to propose that the vote be taken on wednesday.

- (sv) mr president, i represent a minority of the gue/ngl group on this issue. i do not think we need this directive, it is in fact useless. i think that the present situation is perfectly well functioning, with the fact that the manufacture of chocolate is subject to different regulations in different countries. there is no need for harmonisation at union level. we could totally keep the current system. but if we are to harmonize the rules, as the council of ministers seems to have decided, i think it would be better to allow a vegetable fat content other than cocoa butter of up to 5%. the two types of chocolate will then continue to be available, and the choice of purchase will be based on personal taste or national traditions. the alternative solution, which would be to ban other vegetable fats, would result in the banning of many products that consumers like and are used to. i think that would be absolutely unnecessary in this case. on the other hand, it is important that chocolate be clearly labelled, which i believe is reflected in the proposal, so that the consumer can really choose what he wants to buy and eat. nor is it clear that developing countries, as a whole, would benefit from a ban on the addition of other vegetable fats. on the one hand, vegetable fats such as shea butter would be disadvantaged, which would be detrimental to some countries. on the other hand, it is likely that the chocolate market would decline, and thus also the consumption of cocoa butter. my conclusion is that i would prefer to see the old system where different national regulations coexist. since that cannot be done, i think the common position can be accepted.

- (sv) mr president, we in the european parliament cannot say that one type of chocolate is better than another. nor can we say that there is one that tastes better. it is up to the consumer to decide which chocolate is the best in quality and taste. consumer choice is the principle on which the market economy is based, and the political class must not assume the burden of oversight. the previous prime minister of malta, dom mintoff, tried in the 1970s to reduce chocolate imports and to change the way maltese and tourists alike consumed chocolate. with the help of china, he had a chocolate factory built on the island. but the local population did not appreciate this local production, and the consumption of chocolate fell. if we align the evolution of chocolate production with a single eu-wide manufacturing tradition, the cocoa-producing countries risk being the losers in the transaction, as there is a strong chance that the total consumption of chocolate in the union will decrease significantly. so let's not make the same mistake as dom mintoff. the proposal to indicate the contents of certain types of chocolate on the front of the packaging is discriminatory and totally unnecessary. this would have almost the same effect as the warning on cigarette packs. mr president, the chocolate war has been raging in the european union for 25 years. it has ridiculed us as a political class, and it has ridiculed the eu as an institution. the eu will have to make room for two different chocolate-making traditions in a society marked by diversity. we have the opportunity to bring to an end, in a way that is honorable for all parties, this european war over chocolate that has lasted for 25 years. the common position must therefore be followed to the nearest comma.
the rapporteur, mr president, regrets that i have caused this slight complication with the agenda of the plenary. after consulting the other members of the committee on development and cooperation, i would ask you to postpone the vote on this regulation until tomorrow. there are two main reasons for my request. one is that a request for separate votes on the amendments should have been tabled before 7 p.m. yesterday. this request could not be made because of a misunderstanding. i think it would be regrettable to continue the vote as a group vote. informal consultations with the council have led me to believe that it might be possible to reconcile the differences between the council and parliament, but if we vote as a bloc we could squander this chance. the other reason refers to comitology. we would benefit from further discussions within the political groups on certain details of some of the amendments. i therefore ask you, as rapporteur, to take account of my recommendation to postpone the vote.
the committee on legal affairs and citizens' rights has adopted a number of amendments to the proposal. - (sv) madam president, ladies and gentlemen, europe is built on fundamental rights. the same is true of the american constitution, which is also based on our european rights, on the philosophy of john locke and on the ideas of the french revolution. the american people can invoke our fundamental rights in their courts, which is a source of pride, a source of national conscience and a means of action for them. fundamental rights can be the basis of individual identity and citizenship. they can also help each citizen to make use of the opportunities which are now available to him in europe, thanks to free movement, the single currency and information technology - in other words, the opportunities created by the new economy. what constitutes our resources in europe, in this case our cultural differences, will be easier to protect and accept if citizens know that they can demand respect for their rights wherever they are in the union. the free movement of persons simply requires that fundamental rights be guaranteed. without this guarantee, eu enlargement could even be a failure and ruin our economic development. a binding charter is an imperative necessity in an equally indispensable constitution. i would like to warn against accession to the council of europe convention, especially if such accession were to take place without a binding charter. it is necessary to examine these complications more closely. i hope that we will get a modernised and reformulated version of article 6 of the council of europe convention, which summarises and condenses the list of existing rights, to which the subject of modern biotechnologies and data protection have been added. the charter should be easily accessible to citizens and sufficiently clear. it should not be divided into two parts or contain too many references. the charter will have to be binding and apply to the european institutions themselves. we cannot accept that citizens should be left unprotected against the attacks on them by institutions such as olaf or europol, for example. the project will strengthen individual rights in europe.

present, there are already several labels of this type which coexist relatively well and complement each other. we also want environmental ngos to have a decisive influence on the award of the label and the definition of the criteria. experience shows that this should give labels more credibility. this system works perfectly where it has been implemented. we are therefore particularly pleased that the idea of a more commercial organization has been abandoned. i also welcome the fact that the principle of a gradual labelling has been abandoned. better stick to one flower. a gradual system of the kind we have used since the beginning would probably have the effect of giving consumers more worries than information. we are also pleased that the proposal also covers the services sector. in summary, we think that the common position and the amendments which have been tabled, including those of the gue/ngl group, make this a very good report.

- (sv) mr president, mr blokland has done a tremendous job. the subject was more than complex. in addition, lobbyists have exerted unrealistic pressure. i never thought there were so many people in europe who cared about garbage. you'd think it was gold, not garbage. it may be a good omen that we are witnessing the emergence of such a powerful reprocessing industry and one that relies on such financial and personal skills. of course, the eu must put in place a sustainable system for the protection of air, land and water. this is a commitment and a responsibility that we all share. it is also clear that this question must be settled at community level. but is it really the community level that requires such detailed technical descriptions as those which are being attempted in this directive? would it not be more sensible to take decisions on the permitted release thresholds for the various substances, on the measurement system, on how these measures should be taken, and then apply the principle of subsidiarity? there are indeed huge differences in the way waste is treated. i would like to mention the case of northern scandinavia, where transport distances are long, i.e. the incineration of waste along with large quantities of biofuels, and the case of the highly populated areas of central europe, where it is more of a cleaning problem, the aim being more to burn waste than to generate, for example, heat for energy purposes. i would like to see us having the opportunity one day - and we do not have it today - to discuss the possibilities of creating a framework legislation in this area.
- (sv) mr president, i too would like to thank mr davies for his report. it is a very important text, especially in the health field. from an ecological point of view, it is also closely linked to the next issue we are going to deal with, the one which is the subject of the myller report. i am convinced that these two reports together constitute a good basis for the future of the environment and the development of public health in europe. i should like to use the short time i have to speak to try to dispel at least some of the myths that have been flourishing about the ozone problem. i am thinking in particular of the situation in southern europe. the climate of these regions is often used as a pretext or explanation for the high ozone levels recorded in the summer. it is true that a climate with long periods of high pressure increases ozone levels. but at the same time, studies show that the causes of this ozone layer are not to be found elsewhere, and that is why it is damaging cities such as barcelona, athens, milan and many coastal tourist towns. these causes are rooted in traffic, industrial processes and energy production. these are the sectors of activity which can be affected by these directives. it is therefore a pity that the committee on industry, foreign trade, research and energy is taking such a detached attitude to this text and that it is only looking at it from a perspective which is intended to promote industry. it would be more sensible to consider what happens when the who's guidelines are constantly exceeded and people, whether locals or tourists, are exposed to health hazards. we know that the greatest risks are to people who spend long periods outside, especially children. we know that exceeding the ozone threshold has a direct effect on their respiratory capacity. these are facts which the committee on industry, foreign trade, research and energy should consider tomorrow when this directive is put to the vote.
- (sv) mr president, i should like to begin by thanking mrs myller for her report and for the conscientious approach she has taken to this directive proposed by the commission. even if the committee on industry, foreign trade, research and energy seems to have barely woken up and is still surprised, it can be said that the acidification problem is not really new, since it has existed for as long as the industrial age - that is, for a hundred years. research on this question has been going on for thirty years. none of us should have any doubt, when it comes to acidification, about what is to blame and how to remedy it. some people accuse the commission's proposal, and the ideas in the report, of being excessive and far too costly, and say that the estimates in question are utopian. such statements are not correct, since we know that the member states themselves contributed to the preparation of the reports which formed the basis of the whole directive. the criticism should be that we have not included the scenario which is usually emphasized in energy, for example in the kyoto protocol, and which states that it will be necessary to reduce the consumption of fossil fuels in order to solve the climate problems we are facing. i am far from denigrating the gothenburg protocol, but - even though it bears the name of my home town - i must say that it is, in fact, impossible to compare this protocol with the directive on which we will have to take a decision tomorrow. it may be asked why the directive comes a month after the united nations directive, as if it were a reply to it, but that is only because we are dealing with this matter so slowly. if we had been quicker, the directive would have been ready long before the famous gothenburg protocol. the implementation of this directive is called impossible, but i would rather say that the situation we are in today is impossible. the difficulties are not with the directive but with the present context.
- (sv) mr president, the main environmental problem in my country of origin is the acidification of agricultural land, forests, lakes and waterways. acidification leads to forest death, reduced forest growth, reduced flora and fish death. acidified water also releases heavy metals that are a health hazard. nature is particularly sensitive to acidification in the nordic countries, where the filtration capacity is often insufficient. acidification is caused by acid rain, which itself comes from atmospheric emissions from the use of fossil fuels. in sweden and finland, we have taken large-scale measures to reduce the manifestations of air pollution at the national level. we are investing in large-scale operations to add lime to the water of lakes and marshes. we are largely satisfying the objectives proposed in this directive. however, more than 80 percent of the acid rain that falls in our country is caused by air pollution in other western european countries. that's why we're asking for your help. many northern europeans believe that only environmental cooperation with other eu countries can solve the acidification problem. i would like to say this to the members of the committee on industry, foreign trade, research and energy: the resolution adopted by the united nations in the form of the gothenburg protocol is far from sufficient. i am convinced that the eu can do better than the united nations in dealing with the environmental problem facing europe. the european directive on air pollution which is being proposed to us can indeed represent an important step in the fight against acidification of natural areas, which affects the northern regions of the union in particular. in addition, it must at the same time achieve a significant reduction in the amount of ground-level ozone which is harmful to the health of the population, especially in the large cities of the southern eu. it is therefore in our common interest to welcome this proposal for a directive.
the rapporteur, mr president, has already said that the european union is not a party to the convention on human rights. after the horrors of world war ii, many held out great hope for a better world and a more humane future. unfortunately, in many ways, these hopes have been dashed. many of the world's inhabitants continue to live under oppression, many of their basic rights being denied. every day we read about violence committed in the name of the victims' religion, gender, ethnicity and political views, sexual orientation and so on. the european community was set up to prevent the atrocities of the second world war from happening again. it was not intended to be merely an economic association but a community of essential moral values based on respect for human rights. our internal cooperation and our external policy are both based on this basis. the european parliament has always considered the promotion of human rights to be one of its main tasks. over the years, the importance of this work has increased. no one today can be mistaken about the commitment of this house in this area. in many respects the eu has a good human rights policy, but unfortunately it is far too fragmented and fragmented. the union therefore has a great need for a harmonized, strategic and coherent policy in this area. the council report which was presented to us in october last year was a first step in this direction. this report was welcome, we have been waiting for it for a long time, and we hope that we will have to deal with this subject again. this is an important text, but it reveals several gaps that need to be filled. i believe that some of these failures are linked to structural obstacles and the fact that the eu has not developed a common human rights strategy. harmonisation is necessary, not only between the european institutions, but also between the union and its member states. human rights must be integrated naturally into community activity: aid, trade, economic, cultural and political cooperation. we need a policy which is coherent, reliable and predictable. the eu must speak with one voice and send the same messages to those who violate human rights. the eu's views on this matter must be beyond doubt. some human rights are universal in nature. those who violate them cannot use religion, culture, or tradition as an excuse. the most serious acts, which are specified by international conventions, cannot - and should not - be considered as internal affairs. i would therefore urge the council to develop a strategy to improve our work, to make it more targeted and hopefully to achieve more results. proposals that could be included in such a strategy include, for example, the idea that the council should define its own indicators to measure respect for human rights - these indicators should be used when concluding trade agreements. these clauses can be applied constructively, but they require clarification as to the adaptations, implementation and possible sanctions. we could also set up an advisory group with representatives of the european institutions. they could meet regularly to discuss the promotion of human rights in the short and long term. experts will need to be present at union delegations in third countries. we must work more systematically to collect data, information and documentation on the various contexts and to assess the respective situations. we need a system whereby we can be alerted at an early stage to human rights violations. we should also set up a website to present our work on human rights, explaining, for example, the tasks of each body and the results achieved. this site may also contain links to other organisations' websites. i would also like to say a few words about women's rights, which i have chosen to deal with specifically in my report. the commission has made 1999 the year of the fight against violence against women. this was a commendable initiative, but the campaign was carried out in a discreet way. we need a long-term job, not one-off campaigns. women's rights are systematically violated all over the world. we're hearing about domestic violence, rape, ritual abuse, and assault in prison. in the 1990s, rape was used systematically as an act of war in several armed conflicts. these acts can now be examined by the international criminal court. rape is now the most common crime in the world. according to united nations data, the number of women who die from domestic violence is increasing every year, compared to the number of women who die from road accidents, malaria, war or cancer. the trafficking of women and children is increasing at an alarming rate. in some countries, the regime practices a policy of pure apartheid against women. thousands of girls are sexually mutilated every day. violence against women is a global issue, which should be placed high on the agenda and which must become a central concern in european policy, particularly in relation to relations with countries that violate women's rights. this question must be included in diplomatic relations and formulated in the course of the negotiations on the enlargement of the union. the eu member states must ratify the statute of the international criminal court as soon as possible so that it can start work. the eu can play an important role in urging different countries to ratify international conventions designed to protect human rights. let me conclude by saying that it is clear from the reports which will be discussed after mine that human rights violations can also occur within the eu. we must of course also deal with it. if we want to be credible to the outside world, we must also have good internal politics.

in writing. - (sv) it is entirely desirable to tighten up the rules applicable to incineration plants. however, it may be doubted whether it is appropriate to have the same type of rules for installations dealing with unsorted waste and those which only have to treat certain types of waste after sorting. the facilities for unseparated waste require more sophisticated equipment. if the same requirements are applied to all types of incineration plants, there is a risk that the sorting of waste will be hindered and that recycling or reuse, including composting of organic waste, will be hindered. the swedish christian democrats therefore ask the commission to take these views into account in the further work on the issues relating to the treatment of waste and household waste.

in writing. - (da) the danish and swedish social democrats voted today in favour of the report by mr andrew nicholas duff and mr johannes voggenhuber on the drawing up of a charter of fundamental rights of the european union. we are in favour of the drawing up of a charter of fundamental rights in the form of a political document. in this period of reforms within the eu, including its enlargement, it is important to show the world that the eu is committed to protecting people. a charter is a good way of doing this. the drafting of a charter of fundamental rights always involves a certain risk, namely that citizens will regard it as a nice document to use at solemn events, but that it will disappoint their expectations. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environment from harmful substances. in the context of previous votes on the same subject, we had already made it clear that we did not want to put too many questions on the agenda of the conference so that it could be closed at the nice summit in december. we also stressed earlier that no new obstacles should be placed in the way of enlargement. the development of a set of binding fundamental rights raises a number of questions of principle which are very difficult to resolve, both in terms of their content and in relation to other fundamental rights such as those covered by the european convention on human rights. it would therefore be unrealistic to expect the conference to be closed in december. we therefore believe that it is not appropriate to include fundamental rights in the agenda of the current intergovernmental conference. we have expressed this view on every point in the report which has been put to the vote. we have decided to vote for the report as a whole, however, because we believe that fundamental rights are one of the foundations of the european construction and because we want a charter to be drawn up in the form of a political document. whether it will be subsequently incorporated into the treaty in a binding manner will depend on its content; in other words, will it offer citizens better protection than they currently enjoy?

in writing. - (sv) at the cologne european council, it was decided to draw up a charter of fundamental rights for the eu. the aim of this would be to gather and confirm the rights existing in the treaty and to make them clearer and more familiar. we warmly welcome such a charter of fundamental rights, which can of course also encompass the rights enshrined in existing international conventions signed by the member states. at the same time, human rights must be seen as an international and global issue, not just a matter of concern to european citizens. we are reluctant to make such a charter legally binding, however, before we have fully clarified what this would entail in practice. the relationship between the court of justice of the eu and the european court of human rights, for example, appears to be legally complicated and could undermine the european convention on human rights. we believe that a number of points need to be clarified before we can lock ourselves into a model, which is why we abstain in the final vote.
in writing. - (sv) we warmly welcome the work now under way to draw up a charter of fundamental rights for the eu. such a charter has a very high symbolic value and will strengthen the confidence of the union's citizens in the eu and in european integration. the charter of fundamental rights must be seen as a complement to the european convention on human rights, which we believe should be incorporated into the treaty. the charter must focus on the relationship between citizens and the european institutions. the main objective of the union's foreign policy must be to ensure that the union is governed in an open way. it must be legally binding and even incorporated into the eu treaty. the charter should not be just a declaration, without any commitment on the part of the eu institutions and member states. we want to insist on the principle of subsidiarity and for the charter to focus on the fundamental freedoms and rights and the rights of citizens as they appear in the common constitutional traditions of the member states. we want to stress the importance of the social dimension in the eu's activities, but we believe that social policy is basically a matter for each individual member state to decide.
