in writing. - (sv) we voted against a number of points in the haug report on the guidelines for the budgetary procedure for the financial year 2001. the major challenge facing the eu in the coming years is the enlargement of the union to include the countries of central and eastern europe. enlargement is going to cost a lot of money, for many reasons. it is therefore essential that the eu budget provides for substantial amounts for this purpose, which are not limited to accession aid. the haug report does not mention the enlargement of the union. the text prefers to define the 'social and cultural dimension of europe' as the main element of the 2001 budgetary guidelines. we believe that the eu must focus on problems which the member states cannot solve on their own - that is, on issues which transcend national boundaries. cultural and social policies are very important areas, but for the most part they should not be the subject of community legislation. aid for the reconstruction of kosovo is considered in the haug report as another priority, which we fully support. on the other hand, we believe that the funds needed for kosovo should be taken from the budget for aid for tobacco and other crops which are currently covered by the common agricultural policy. as liberals, we believe that the common agricultural policy must be adapted to the market and 'de-bureaucratised'. if parliament is serious about eastward enlargement, it must work for substantial cuts in agricultural spending. all direct aid to agricultural production must be phased out. the eu plays an important role in the field of employment, in particular through the internal market. however, we would like to point out that unemployment cannot be reduced by political decisions at european level alone. we are convinced that measures taken in the member states, such as reducing labour taxation, introducing more flexible labour law and a more favourable environment for entrepreneurship, are the factors which can increase employment and bring about a dynamic development for the union as a whole.
in writing. - (sv) we would like to make the following points clear in relation to points 11 to 16 concerning aid for the development of renewable energy sources. we are in favour of promoting these sources of energy, but we believe that it must be done, rather than through direct subsidies, through the use of various forms of economic incentives, for example by higher taxation of fossil fuels, the introduction of environmental taxes and a stricter application of the polluter pays principle. in addition, aid should be granted for research and projects on the development of renewable energy sources.
in writing. - (sv) on 8 december 1999 the european patent office granted patent no 695 351 'by mistake' for the genetic manipulation of human cells and embryos. as a christian democrat member of the european parliament, i have approved the resolution drawn up today, in which we firmly distance ourselves from this decision of the european patent office, which is contrary to the moral concepts of our society, as well as to european legislation and the national laws in force throughout europe, in the field of patents. i would like to express my anger and deep disappointment that the european patent office has treated a matter which concerns life itself in such an irresponsible, careless and careless way. at the same time, i hope that the european patent office as a collective authority, and its representatives as individuals, will draw the appropriate conclusions from this episode. it is therefore necessary to reverse this totally unacceptable decision on the genetic manipulation of human cells and embryos as soon as possible - in order to protect life, the most precious and sacred possession we have.
the report by mr van miert, on behalf of the committee on legal affairs and citizens' rights, is a very good report. it calls for action across borders, particularly as the internet is being denationalized. i would like to congratulate the rapporteur, mr kirkhope, on the remarkable work he has done. in a context such as this, it is perhaps especially welcome that mr kirkhope is british. it is good that he has established a definition of child pornography, and i think we have reached a satisfactory compromise. i particularly welcome the inclusion of anything resembling child pornography in this definition. the proposal provides for the establishment of contact points and special units in the member states to deal with this problem. it is hoped that this will lead to more reliable investigations. in this area, the quality of the investigations requires more expertise. europol will be involved, as will the candidate countries. it is good that such criminal acts committed in third countries are punished by national legislation. the definition of 'child' - that is, age - has been formulated to create the most effective level of protection against child pornography. our reasoning was to be flexible in seeking an age limit that would strike as hard as possible on child pornography. because it is about protecting children with maximum efficiency, to prevent the childhood they are entitled to being stolen from them. it's a right that should be every child's right! it must be one of the most basic of human rights.
- (sv) mr president, i welcome the fact that this debate on child pornography on the internet is taking place. we have heard a lot of intelligent things, apart from a number of rather primitive and ignorant remarks made by my opponent. i would like to remind you that many pedophiles actually victimize the children around them. divorce, alcoholism, or any other phenomenon is not the cause of such behavior. it is well known that sexual arousal from the presence of a child is not a common occurrence, and we can be thankful for that. the internet facilitates the exchange of photographs, and that's what we need to talk about. i agree with mrs maes that we have very competent police forces working on these issues. however, given the scale of the problem, their staff is too small to be able to analyse the situation as a whole. it is therefore a pity that we are turning to europol immediately. we have spoken to europol officials about trafficking in women and children, and they have told us that they have two reports - but these are not official reports, so only europol knows what tasks they are assigned to. this is a problem. the police agencies with whom i have had contact in sweden, and who are working on these issues through interpol, need more support, of course - as do their colleagues in other countries. because this is not just a european union issue. there is also much to do in thailand, the philippines, and many other places around the world where the police are on the job. westerners do not have the exclusivity of work in this field. there is one aspect which is not mentioned very often in this report and which i would like to bring into the debate, namely the question of the reintegration of victims. the problem with the internet is the clarity of the images. children, when they are not very young, are constantly at risk of being followed on the network and constantly recognized. we need to think about how our respective countries are dealing with children who have been exposed to this crime and whose photos are circulating on the internet. in sweden, we also found that there could be people who applied for jobs in the school world or the childcare sector, because they were pedophiles. i regret that we have not discussed the enormous budgetary restrictions and the large-scale privatizations that are being undertaken in these areas of activity. of course, it may also be necessary to take preventive measures to ensure that no one works alone with children in private or public schools, childcare facilities or similar jobs. i think this debate is very welcome, but i would like to reiterate that paedophilia is not a common phenomenon, nor is it widespread, and as i said before, we can only welcome it.
- (sv) mr president, i share the rapporteur's position in principle that we should not authorize new food additives unless there is a real need for them. i would say, however, that we must be attentive to any local or national traditions that may exist and, if necessary, give reasons for such authorization. the additive i would like to mention is ethylhydroxyethylcellulose. this is not a delicacy, and it cannot be abused. however, it has been used for more than 20 years in sweden, finland and, i believe, norway as a binder in gluten-free bread and pastries. the purpose of this is to prevent the gluten intolerant from becoming crushed, so that the relatively small group of people who are gluten intolerant can consume bread and pastries in the same form as those intended for healthy people. ethylhydroxyethylcellulose is of particular interest in the baking traditions of the northern countries of the union. it is therefore not a perfect substitute for other types of celluloses already authorised. the rapporteur states that there is a risk of contamination by ethyl hydroxyethyl cellulose, which could have a carcinogenic effect, and he also refers to a possible laxative effect if large doses are ingested. i would like to say that the reality may be just the opposite. the fact that this bread is compact and not spoon-fed is especially important for children with gluten intolerance. these children find it difficult to understand why they are forced to eat crumbs, while others have the right to bread that does not fall into small pieces. if the child shows a lack of interest in what he is fed, the risk of malnutrition increases. when gluten intolerant individuals refuse to eat bread crumbs and eat ordinary bread, they may develop abdominal pain, possibly diarrhea, and in the long run, increased risk of stomach and intestinal cancer. many families with gluten-intolerant children have a heavy lifestyle, with the preparation of meals being a major added burden. they cannot buy their own bread, but they have to make it, using this additive themselves. why should we make the situation of these families even more difficult? why can't they continue to use a product they're used to? not authorising ethylhydroxyethylcellulose would create difficulties for adults with gluten intolerance and for the families of children with this condition. in such a case, the precautionary principle cannot be invoked to justify a ban. on the contrary, the precautionary principle argues in favour of the authorisation of ethylhydroxyethylcellulose.
in writing. - (sv) we would like to say the following about amendments nos 22 to 28, which will be voted on jointly: we take the problem of child pornography very seriously, whether on the internet or in other media, and we certainly agree that the member states, as well as the candidate countries, must do everything in their power to combat it. it is also important to take effective joint action and parliament must be kept informed of the progress of work in this area. we believe that, for the same reasons as those which led us to reject the proposal to create special registers for convicted sex offenders (amendments nos 21, 30 and 32), that proposal no 24 is politically undesirable. the idea of recording the identity of people who have an e-mail address, if technically feasible, would be an excessive restriction of personal integrity and would also risk hindering the legitimate use of the internet, which should not be encouraged.
commission (de) mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i should first like to draw the attention of the honourable member, mr arne arvidsson, to the community legal provisions on the coordination of national social security systems. under these provisions, persons who are temporarily staying in a member state other than the one in which they are insured are entitled to care services if their situation so requires. the persons concerned must obtain a form e111 from their insurance institution and present it to the institution of the member state in which they are temporarily staying to prove that they are insured. in order to facilitate the situation of patients on dialysis in the event of a temporary stay and to avoid any potential uncertainty as to the concept of an indispensable benefit, the member states have agreed on specific regulations laid down in decision no 163 of the administrative commission of the european communities on social security for migrant workers of 31 may 1996. under these regulations, dialysis is an essential service provided that the stay is not for medical purposes. however, as the availability of dialysis treatment may differ from one member state to another, the patients concerned are naturally encouraged to make the necessary prior arrangements with the hospital providing this service to ensure that they will be able to benefit from dialysis treatment in the event of a temporary stay in that member state. as to whether the commission will take any initiative to increase the availability of dialysis treatment during the holiday period, i would say that community law does not provide for any uniform system of social security and that it is for each member state to organise its own social security system and to decide what benefits are maintained, who is entitled to them and to what extent they are provided. it is therefore not for the commission to intervene in this area and to influence national regulations on the way in which clinics and hospitals operate in the member states.
- (sv) many hospitals do not consider chronic dialysis as an emergency treatment and for this reason may deny people dependent on dialysis the opportunity to be treated in their specialist departments during a journey. children, parents or young people in families where a person is on dialysis often have a strong desire to travel. they work, they have holidays, but they can't go on trips in the usual way. the fact that they can travel abroad is an international issue for them, and when national authorities do nothing, they turn with hope to european organizations. even if it is not possible to intervene on a purely legal level, perhaps other ways could be found to draw attention to the need to increase dialysis activity during holiday periods in the member states. for example, hospitals that have made efforts in this direction could be honoured, and perhaps european awards of considerable value could be organised for those who have taken action.
- thank you for your answer. i am glad that the commission has really looked into the problem and intends to do everything possible to speed up the payments. mr patten told the committee on budgets a while ago that there were commitments in category 4 which were eight and a half years old and had not yet been paid. this shows either that we have serious efficiency problems in implementing the budget or that we should have had some kind of deadline or cut-off that would have been much more effective in paying off long-standing commitments. these may be two problems which the commission needs to address.
- mr president, anyone who has recently been to turkey will realise that the situation is very complex. the first impression is that there is a certain euphoria in the media about the union. there's a very lively debate going on in the country. that's a very important point. secondly, the unions, the grassroots movements, etc., all say the same thing. they see turkey's candidacy in a positive light. even akin birdal, on the eve of his imprisonment, said that a new turkey has emerged in the last five months. we must expect to see many fluctuations in our relations with turkey. we'll be recording progress as well as setbacks. we may be witnessing the birth of something. but the old legal system is still in place. this is what stands in opposition to the work of democracy advocates such as akin birdal. some reformers are well aware of the implications of the copenhagen criteria. a party leader told us that at least 65 articles of the constitution and the penal code needed to be changed. in our discussions with the people of this country during the visit of the socialist group, we have noted three different approaches to the turkish candidacy. firstly, there are the anti-reformists, the fundamentalists, etc., who are opposed to turkey's membership. then there are the reformers - in government, the administration, human rights groups and other sectors of civil society - who are calling for it with all their might. finally, there is a third group, which supports the candidacy, but on turkish terms. it is in this context that the copenhagen criteria and the monitoring by parliament and the commission are of particular importance. they will have to be effective if turkey is to join the union. there are two impressions and messages that emerge from the talks we have had with the turkish citizens during the two or three days we have been here. firstly, nothing can be achieved in turkey without a strong civil society. the civil society we met said they appreciated the european aid but deplored the slowness with which it is sometimes delivered. we are aware of this and we ask the commission to look into the matter. the last message we received was this: we have the route, but it needs to be specified. we are not going to be able to do that, because we are not going to be able to do that.
- (sv) mr president, the precautionary principle should be a decisive factor in the dissemination of gmos. this should now be reflected in a moratorium on the dissemination of new modified species. the remaining scientific uncertainties - particularly as regards the risks of spread - as well as the shortcomings in legislation and the application of international agreements justify such a moratorium. we also believe that each eu member state should have the right to apply a stricter policy than the union, and that is why we will vote against amendment no 6 on a common european procedure for dissemination. the proposals which will be adopted tomorrow go much further than we would have liked. we shall therefore vote in such a way as to make all these measures as valid as possible. that means that we will vote in favour of most of the bowe report's proposals, but not all of them. we shall vote in favour of the full civil liability of those who disseminate gmos. we want to see an end to the use of antibiotic resistance markers. we want to see the labelling of foodstuffs clearly and we want each application to be dealt with separately without simplified procedures. we are also in favour of the obligation for producers to provide clear information and, in the case of exports to a third country, to obtain the authorization of the third country.
- (sv) mr president, mr rapporteur, ladies and gentlemen, it is important to restore market confidence. but it is even more important to restore the confidence of our citizens in the quality of the food they buy and consume - in fact, this is perhaps the way we can strengthen the market. the decisive factor here is the safe traceability of food production. amendment no 29 is very important for producers, and perhaps especially for small producers in crisis-affected areas. it allows the addition of additional information to the indication of the country of origin for the labelling of products. the fact that a meat can be mentioned, for example, as coming from a particular region, from a specific animal breed or as having been produced by well-defined farming methods is indeed a right. however, it is not desirable to legislate on this point, except to ensure that false labelling is prohibited. i am very pleased that mr whitehead has drawn attention to the issue of minced meat. i fully support amendment no 30 tabled by the rapporteur. the most important thing in all this is that no exception should be made for cheap food products. we in this house, who are mostly middle-aged and well-off, know full well that it is the young people and families with small children who buy and consume the cheapest food products - sausages, hamburgers, etc. in all systems of society and in all periods, it has always been the weakest social groups that have had the least purchasing power. it is our absolute moral duty to ensure the quality of cheap food products before the quality of beef.
- (sv) mr president, the eu is facing enormous challenges. within a few years, the union will have expanded to include almost twice as many member states as it does today. the euro will have been put into circulation. we will have put in place a security policy worthy of the name, implemented administrative reforms and, i hope, regained the citizens' faith in the european project, which has now been shattered. the intergovernmental conference must prepare the eu for these changes. it gives us the opportunity to adapt our work to the new member states and to the new tasks that we have. the question is, on the one hand, to find the balance between democracy and efficiency, and on the other hand, to safeguard the balance between small and large countries. these are the questions which we have to deal with after the amsterdam treaty has been concluded. in these discussions, it is important not to get lost in the technical details, nor to lose sight of our own goal - to make the eu work more openly, more efficiently and more understandably. to achieve these goals, we all have to make sacrifices. unfortunately, governments seem to have forgotten about them. the intergovernmental conference is making only limited progress, and this is not the fault of the portuguese presidency. it is likely that things will go on as usual: on the last day, at the last minute, we will agree on a compromise that will not really satisfy anyone. we are already talking about the next intergovernmental conference, which is supposed to resolve the major issues at a later date. we've heard that before. there is beginning to be a certain inflation of unfinished intergovernmental conferences. but our citizens expect more from the eu, and we do not have much time. that is why we must seize the opportunity that is open to us today. the eu must change in the direction the liberals are calling for, i.e. towards greater openness, more transparency, clearer accountability criteria, a clearer division of powers and a common constitution. the union simply needs to be liberalized. many of our amendments are included in the report, and i support them with a few exceptions. the challenges facing the eu are exciting and historically significant. they should inspire us all to roll up our sleeves. we have the opportunity to create a work of value that is unique: democratic, competitive and peaceful collaboration across the continent. but instead of inspiring hope and enthusiasm, this development seems to be a cause of anxiety for our governments. the question is where is the political will and where are the prospects for the future?
- (sv) mr president, if the treaty amendments proposed in the resolution we are debating were to be implemented, the european union would take a decisive step towards the evolution towards a federal state, a federal state in which national parliaments would lose control of almost all the key policy areas. in addition to the massive increase in supranational decision-making processes, the proposal contains several qualitative changes that move in the direction of federalism. i am thinking in particular of the proposal to abolish the right of national veto for amendments to important parts of the present treaty. i am also thinking of the request for the creation of a common electoral district for the election of members of the european parliament. i am convinced that this evolution of the eu towards a federal state is leading to a dead end in every way. the main problem with the eu today is not the lack of centralized power, but the absence of a genuine democracy. what the union needs is not an extra supranationality, but a democratic reform which decisively reduces the political power of the commission, makes the legislative work done in the council public and gives the national parliaments a decisive influence. the amendments to the treaty proposed in the resolution would place the eu under the total domination of the largest member states. their power would be radically increased by the introduction of a double majority system in the council of ministers. it would also be increased by increasing the representation of these states in the european parliament. in a european union extended to twelve additional countries, and where the seven hundred seats in parliament would be distributed in the way proposed in the resolution, only thirteen seats would go to sweden, seven to finland and five to ireland. this implies that political currents playing an important role in many member states would not be represented at european level. this again reflects the weakness of the democratic anchorage of which such a parliament could boast. compared with other federal states, a "european state" built on this model would be unusually centralised and would offer little protection to the smaller states it encompasses. the comparison can be made with the united states, where the bicameral system operates, one of the two chambers being elected according to the principle of proportional representation, while the second one is the same for all states. in the european union as conceived in the proposal we are discussing, the largest countries would have the upper hand in both assemblies. for those who want to reform monetary union, the emu, the treaty reform offers an opportunity to act. many supporters of emu from left-wing parties have argued for changes that would place the central bank under democratic control, so that the objectives of economic policy are not solely monetary. it is strange that they are silent today, and that only the gue/ngl group is calling for the democratisation of emu. as you may have gathered, i intend to vote against this resolution... (the president cut the speaker off)
in writing. - (sv) the production of genetically modified organisms, the gmos, means that industry, that is to say man, is committed in the name of health, medicine, the concern to increase crops, to create insect-resistant plants and other advances that we imagine could improve human existence in the future, on a path whose end is unknown. it may one day turn out to be the very opposite of the promises made at the beginning of the journey. i want to make that warning today. the european union needs new rules, stricter than the first ones, which were enacted by the 1991 gmo directive and are still in force. the commission's proposal has been under consideration since 1998. any further delay would be a mistake. as a swedish christian democrat member of the european parliament, i would like to say that the gmo industry and economic interests should never guide our legislative action or dictate the regulations. their representatives have repeatedly stated that europe is "lagging behind" on gmos, mainly in comparison with the us and china, that the gmo industry cannot "develop" as "freely" in europe, and that the 15 eu member states are "losing" business creation and new jobs. our political position must be inspired by a sense of our administrative responsibility, not by the manipulation of the genetic code. our mission is to leave future generations a world and an environment in as good a condition as possible. we must be able to demonstrate that humanity, based on administrative responsibility and the precautionary principle, is willing to give up what might appear to be short-term "profits" and to invest in higher values which are much more relevant in the long term, such as respect for creation and genetic diversity. from a purely practical point of view, this implies regulations that ensure maximum risk reduction of the spread of gmos, the cessation of the use of antibiotic resistance markers, the development of independent research on the risks induced by gmos, civil liability based on the principle of strict liability of persons who have disseminated gmos whose effects on health or the environment have been found to be harmful, environmental impact assessments and long-term socio-economic studies of the use of gmos, permanent labelling of products containing gmos, compliance with all existing regulations, for example the biosafety protocol, a transparent application and decision-making procedure, which leaves the right of public opinion to express itself, which deals with each request separately and does not tolerate any simplification of procedures.
- (sv) mr president, enlargement to the east is the most important issue for the future of the eu. the swedish christian democrats believe that the european parliament should amend a number of points in the proposal which has just been approved by the constitutional committee. i shall concentrate on these amendments. 1) one commissioner from each member state: this is a principle which affects the legitimacy, the democratic anchoring and the future of the commission. if it should happen that there is no commissioner for each member state, the council will take over the role which the commission plays today. 2) six meps per member state, reflecting the diversity and political spectrum of each of these countries. this is particularly important as countries such as estonia, latvia, lithuania and slovenia are preparing to join the european union after being unwillingly part of the soviet union and yugoslavia. 3) no lists of candidates at european level. the distance between candidates and voters would be excessive. 4) more flexibility as regards the maximum number of 700 seats in the european parliament. 5) no eu funding for political parties active at european level. that would be a misuse of public money, and would leave too much room for arbitrariness. the funding of political parties can be abused. this is a fact that we know exists in europe today. (6) the the qualified majority in the european council, which is referred to in paragraph 8.1 of the resolution, must be defined as a two-thirds majority - not a simple majority. 7) exclusion of a member state must be decided unanimously, with the exception of the country concerned. disfellowshipping is a particularly serious measure. 8) eu funding will have to remain based on member state contributions in the future. 9) the resolution contains no wording on the principle of subsidiarity and the distribution of powers. there is a need for a wording on this.

exert their influence. we need to find a balance. it is a necessity if our citizens are to have confidence in european cooperation. when the composition of the european parliament is adjusted, i allocating votes which does not have to be changed every time new member states join us. that is why i am in favour of a system which takes account of demographic differences. it is essential that each state has a member of the commission. commissioners work for the whole of the union, and they really play an important role in reaching consensus among member states. the commission must therefore bring together the knowledge and experience of all the member states. european cooperation is first and foremost a civil project which must be dealt with separately from military matters. our countries do indeed have different historical contexts on the question of alliances and non-alignment. we have made important progress in cologne and helsinki in our preparation for crisis and conflict prevention actions. it is therefore important that the treaty reflects the difference between civil and military cooperation. the creation of a european military alliance should therefore not be carried out within the framework of the european institutions, but between the states. there is no place in the treaty on european union for compulsory mutual military assistance.

- (sv) mr president, commissioner, if europe is to grow, attract capital and create jobs, it must create an internal market for financial services. so far we have had only words and very little concrete action. at present, the financial market and thus europe's capacity for growth are hampered by a host of national regulations and administrative hassles. this is not only harmful to businesses, but also to european citizens. the development of the internet is of great importance, and will continue to increase, for the provision of cross-border financial services. the main beneficiaries of this development will be consumers, who will have access to a wider choice and lower prices. the liberals do not consider tax harmonization to be one of the prerequisites for the completion of the internal market in financial services, although some degree of harmonization may be necessary to avoid harmful tax competition. the euro has stabilised economic development and opened up better opportunities to lower capital costs, which benefits both businesses and households. i am absolutely convinced that if we want the eu to offer its citizens growth and economic power, all member states must participate fully in the european project, that is to say, they must be members of emu. the statement by mr romano prodi, which is published today in a swedish newspaper, that sweden could remain outside emu seems to me to be as surprising from an economic point of view as it is doubtful in the spirit of the treaty. we welcome the commission's intention to publish a green paper on the liberalization of supplementary pensions. however, it is important to emphasise that the pension systems which exist in europe are characterised by their diversity from one state to another and that there is no need to harmonise these different systems in order to create the internal market. it is essential to ensure that a large number of different systems can coexist, which increases the freedom of choice and the diversity of the offer. on the other hand, it is desirable to implement a minimum harmonisation of the way in which member states treat supplementary pensions from a tax point of view, in order to avoid double taxation. i also believe that the member states themselves must decide what form the system should take, for example whether pensions should take into account dependants. it is more sensible to define the answer to such a question at national level. the question of biometric risks was the subject of a lively discussion in the committee on economic and monetary affairs. some have argued that the directive should cover only those schemes which cover these risks. we do not think that is desirable, especially when we consider the possibilities for small and mediumsized enterprises to operate. free will is indeed a good thing. in conclusion, i would like to congratulate mr garcía-margallo y marfil and mr kuckelkorn on their two important reports, which help to put us on the path to a competitive europe.
- (sv) mr president, when we talk about supplementary pensions, it is important to know whether we want to ensure that pensioners are better protected in their old age, or whether we want to strengthen the european capital markets. these are not only two totally different objectives, but they are also difficult to reconcile within the same system. the commission proposal and the two reports under discussion today give priority to the needs of the capital market. and secondly, it is hoped that these market-based pensions will also be beneficial for the people concerned in the next 30 or 40 years. but we don't know what will happen. if we consider the evolution of europe over the last 30 to 40 years, and if we accept that circumstances may change in the future, we can see how uncertain such forecasts are. in the field of pensions, the level of protection and solidarity is seriously deficient, because the risks are very high and there can never be too much protection or solidarity. the additional insurance is therefore a problem. they are much less secure than the basic pension system, which is financed on the principle of solidarity. i also think that supplementary insurance and pensions may be necessary, but they should be used to invest in activities that help to strengthen the economy in the long term. this would be wiser than letting stock market prices and global financial capitalism dictate the day-to-day destiny of pensioners. i also think it would have been good if these complementary systems could have been regulated within the different sectors of activity as pension funds based on the principle of solidarity, as is the case in some member states. even though these systems are also market-based, they offer much greater stability and security than private pension schemes. it seems that we are now moving towards a liberal organization of pensions in europe, and i will therefore advise my constituents to put their surplus financial resources into less random objects than pension insurance. i would say to the younger generation to focus on training and skills development, and to the older generation to acquire satisfactory and affordable - (sv) mr president, like many other members of the green group, i voted against this report for a number of reasons, three of which are mainly due to the fact that the report is not a report on the environment. firstly, it is not federalism, as many people think, that emerges from the position expressed by parliament, but rather centralism. it is the larger states that get more power, as do the central institutions. there is no sign of any desire for decentralization, for the distribution of powers, no hint of the need to give substance to the principle of subsidiarity. secondly, the text does not provide for any adaptation for the enlargement of the eu, which is perhaps the most serious error. the successful completion of a union of 28 member states obviously requires a flexible structure. clear definitions of competence must be used to determine what is to be decided by the central institutions, what is the responsibility of the member states or the regions. but the report does not say anything about that. if we really want such a large union to work, it is clear that a good deal of the decisions currently taken at european level should be decentralised to lower levels, otherwise the whole system will implode. finally, what we are witnessing here is not a democratic constitutional process but a power play behind closed doors. that is why i voted no.
in writing. -{v) enlargement is one of the most important issues for the future of europe. the current intergovernmental conference aims to prepare the eu for this enlargement. in order to achieve this, a number of institutional changes are necessary. the eu must become more efficient and more democratic; the balance between the smallest and largest countries must be maintained; it is important that the intergovernmental conference reaches a conclusion before the end of the year, so that the enlargement process which has already begun will not be delayed. the report on the intergovernmental conference is the european parliament's contribution to the conference. it also mentions the necessary institutional reforms, which have not been dealt with by the amsterdam treaty, and a number of additional issues which need to be addressed before enlargement. the swedish members of parliament, mr schori, mr andersson, mr färm and myself, mrs hedkvist petersen, support the report in its main lines, but we would like to emphasize the following points on which we differ partially, and which led us to abstain in the final vote. the role of the european parliament is to represent the people of the union. the enlargement will mean that more states will be represented in the assembly. in order to maintain the effectiveness of the decision-making process, it is necessary to change the allocation of seats. we are advocating a ceiling of 700 members in the european parliament. the number of members per country should be a function of the size of the population, according to the principle of degressive proportionality. if a european-wide electoral district is established, the number of members should not be increased. a new weighting of votes in the council is one of the conditions for successful eu enlargement. it is important that the solution chosen is permanent and that we do not have to go back to it in future enlargement processes. it must be based on a balance between the two principles of 'one country, one vote' and 'one citizen, one vote'. one possibility would be to calculate this weighting of votes in the council according to the size of the population of the various countries. we believe that each member state should be represented by a member of the commission. commissioners work for the union as a whole and play an important role in reaching consensus among member states. it must therefore have the knowledge and experience that are specific to each of the countries of the union. the commission's legitimacy will also be strengthened. the protection of the union's interests against fraud must be the concern of all the institutions of the member states, not just of a supranational authority. it is however important and desirable that all member states have european prosecutors and that all cooperate. unanimity in the council must be required for constitutional and fundamental questions. the same must be true of questions relating to the common security and defence policy. close cooperation between at least one third of the member states cannot apply to defence matters. european cooperation is first and foremost a civil project which must be dealt with separately from military matters. our countries do indeed have different historical contexts on the question of alliances and non-alignment. we have made important progress in cologne and helsinki in our preparation for crisis and conflict prevention actions. it is therefore important that the treaty should reflect the difference between civil and military cooperation. there is no place in the treaty on european union for compulsory mutual military assistance. we are not prepared to abolish the right of veto in matters of military cooperation.
in writing. -{sv) the intergovernmental conference is a historic opportunity to make the eu a democratic, transparent and effective organisation, and we therefore regret that the governments of the member states do not have the will to reform the union in depth. we therefore welcome the fact that parliament is taking up the challenge of reform in this respect, but we would like to make the following clarifications regarding our position: a union of 25 to 30 member states will not be able to function in the same way as a union of 15. it is therefore necessary to review the way the european institutions work and to allocate tasks more clearly between the member states and the union. the eu can only function effectively and democratically if it deals only with issues that transcend borders, while the rest are essentially resolved at national level. the problem for the intergovernmental conference is to find a real balance between, on the one hand, the desire to create an effective and democratic union and, on the other hand, the desire to reflect the historical balance of power between small and large countries, that is to say, to take account of the fact that the eu is both a union of peoples and a union of states. on the basis of this principle, we believe it is important that the intergovernmental conference should set the composition of the commission at one commissioner per member state. as regards the distribution of powers in the council, the commission has proposed a double majority system for certain matters, i.e. that a decision will be adopted only if it is approved by a majority of the member states and if these countries represent a majority of the citizens of the union. this is important from the point of view of democracy, since the vote will thus clearly be based on the people it represents. the people's party supports the commission's proposal but stresses that small countries must not be disadvantaged. parliament will have 700 seats representing the eu population according to the principle of degressive proportionality. we are in favour of a new system of european electoral lists being tried out, in this case a limited number of members of parliament, out of the 700 that will be in the parliament, being able to be elected in a common electoral constituency. we are aware that european parties are currently only weakly anchored in public opinion, but we believe that a complementary system of european lists can eventually help to strengthen european identity. in an eu of 25 to 30 countries, more and more countries will want to make use of the possibility that some members can go further than others in one or another policy area (so-called close cooperation). we do not think that this can be seen as a permanent solution to the problem of eu enlargement. this 'europe à la carte' could well jeopardise european cooperation as a whole and ruin the very idea on which it is based. the cohesion between the cooperation partners is at risk of being eroded, which could have very negative consequences for the union as such. instead, flexibility should be seen as a temporary alternative for countries that are not mature enough, or prepared enough, to be able to engage in deeper cooperation. the wording of the treaty of amsterdam on closer cooperation is therefore an acceptable and sufficient solution. the accumulation of concentric circles eventually makes the system difficult to understand. the objectives common to the union as a whole must be promoted in this cooperation, and no member state must be excluded. we welcome the fact that the eu is now given more scope to implement a credible foreign and security policy and to assume a greater share of responsibility in resolving conflicts in its neighbourhood. but at the same time it must be stressed that this development must not lead to a situation where two parallel conflict resolution organisations are competing with each other. it is therefore important to emphasize that this development must take place in the context of close cooperation between nato and the eu, and that the representatives of these organizations must agree as soon as possible on a satisfactory division of tasks.
in writing. -{sv) as a representative of the swedish christian democrats, who consider the enlargement of the eu to the east to be one of the most obviously important issues, i would like to make the following remarks in view of the work that our governments and heads of state are called upon to carry out, within the so-called intergovernmental conference, on the competences and decision-making processes of the european union. 1) each member state must be represented by a commissioner (point 10). the commission's legitimacy, its democratic foundation and its future are at stake. 2) the european parliament must have at least six members from each member state and then apply the system of degressive proportionality which is currently in operation (point 3.2), rather than, as proposed by the committee on constitutional affairs, the principle of only four members from each country, followed by a system of strict proportionality. this is necessary if the political spectrum of each member state is to be reflected in its diversity. 3) the the creation of single european lists or of an eu-wide electoral constituency is to be banned (point 4). to avoid further widening the gap between citizens and their elected representatives, the member states must be the electoral districts. (4) the the number of 700 seats in the european parliament should not be adopted as a ceiling (point 3): a certain degree of flexibility should be possible, depending on the number of member states that the eu brings together. 5) we must not introduce eu funding for parties active at european level, nor an arbitrary exclusion procedure, which would be dangerous for democracy (point 6). 6) council decisions to which the principle of qualified majority applies must be taken not by an absolute majority of the member states but by a two-thirds majority (paragraph 8) - this will increase their legitimacy. 7) a decision as serious as the exclusion of a member state from the eu must require the unanimity of the other countries, and not, as proposed by the committee on constitutional affairs, a four-fifths majority (paragraph 29), 8) the social protection must not be regarded as a matter of community interest (paragraph 42), but must remain a matter for national legislation, in accordance with the principle of subsidiarity. 9) eu funding must not be based entirely on contributions from the member states (paragraph 44.4). 10) respect for the defence solutions chosen by the non-aligned countries and the right of each state to decide in this area must be established within the framework of the cfsp (paragraph 34). 11) the principle of subsidiarity (to which there is unfortunately no reference in this report) must be integrated into the treaty in such a way that the eu's competences are clearly limited to core international issues, so that citizens can more easily know what the responsibilities of each political body are. national parliaments and local authorities have an important role to play in ensuring respect for the principle of subsidiarity and in monitoring the action of governments within the council.

number of initiatives in this area, including the establishment of a european network of local authorities. the present proposal also contains a new budget heading, estimated at eur 27 million. this heading should be placed in category 4, which naturally also updates a proposal from yesterday's committee on a revision of the budgetary plan, intended to allow support, in the longer term, for the democratization and reconstruction of the balkans. a comisión dos orçamentos tem vários was asked about these figures. parliament is determined to come forward with the aid needed, and we are aware that the needs are very great. we have to be attentive to the needs of the balkan peoples, but the mission of the parliament and the other arms of the budgetary authority is also to find technical solutions to their financial needs. i do not think that the 27 million of us who are talking about reinforcement really need to be reviewed as the prospects are financed. i think it is a general opinion, with the possible exception of the council, that it should be considered impossible to use all this aid in the current category 4 budgetary framework. it is not fair that aid to other poor regions of the world should be affected by the problems of the balkans. in summary, i think we need to reflect on the quality of the actions. at the moment, when the commission is presenting, on the one hand, a financing framework and, on the other hand, a new form of aid management to be set up by the civil administration, which corresponds to the eu, it is important not to limit our attention merely to the overall figures or to the formal decision-making processes. at least as important is an evaluation of the action as its speed and effectiveness. currently, it seems that, in general, the work in kosovo is more effective than in bosnia, but there are still some questions that need to be clarified. all this argumentation reveals that a common foreign and security policy is intimately linked to the role of the parliament, notably with regard to the budgetary process. in these circumstances, we have to develop a model that allows us to guarantee that aid is attributed in a transparent and correct way, in accordance with our regulations, and that it can also be analysed and reviewed, as mr staes has said. but this should not result in an execution that helps to block and obscure the structures of the decision. it is in this context that we support the laschet report.

- (sv) mr president, i should like to begin by thanking the rapporteur, mr ferber, for his work. it is easy to agree with this text, many of which are the result of careful thought. i would like to speak on a matter which will be of great importance to parliament and the eu as a whole in the long term. this is a subject which many speakers have already raised today, namely the question of languages. there are few occasions when the linguistic diversity of europe is more fully appreciated than in a debate in this house, where speeches are constantly translated from one to the other of the eleven languages present. this is of course a costly and difficult task, but it has nevertheless always been clear throughout the history of the european union that anyone participating should be able to speak his own language, if possible. a significant part of the report we are dealing with is based on the principle that the eu will be enlarged to include six new member states by the year 2004. it may be that this perspective is an underestimation. a few days ago the commission expressed its own objective, which is to welcome ten new member states by 2005. i am one of those who believe that the greatest challenge facing the eu is enlargement, the ultimate aim of which is to reunite a europe that has always been divided by artificial borders. however, i think it would be more realistic to rely on the slightly lower figure in the report of the committee on budgets. that is still a lot, considering the number of additional languages. if we assume that the first round of negotiations should be on the basis of 'five plus one' - in writing. - (da) we have today voted in favour of the bösch report, which aims to improve our fight against fraud and irregularities in relation to community funds. although the scale of fraud and irregularities has decreased slightly from 1997 to 1998, we still lack more effective control mechanisms. mr kinnock's reform plan is, on the whole, visionary and realistic, but it has some imperfections. parliament had already proposed, in the context of the van hulten report, the creation of an external body attached to the court of auditors, which would be responsible in particular for disciplinary matters and would help to recover funds unduly paid. the creation of such a body would remove disciplinary matters from the commission's competence. we've had some very bad experiences with the internal disciplinary board. for the time being, it is colleagues who are required to act as judges against other officials who are summoned before the disciplinary council. this is totally unacceptable. the echo and med cases are all too well known. we must also criticise the proposal in the white paper to set up an advisory panel to look into irregularities. we have set up olaf to investigate suspected fraud and irregularities. the creation of a new panel would undermine the powers conferred on olaf. we voted against the creation of a european public prosecutor's office because it would be a giant we would have to create when the real problems lie elsewhere. we need more fundamental reform of personnel policy. in any case, only the most serious cases will have criminal repercussions. ninety percent of the cases will be disciplinary in nature and will involve cases of negligence and incompetence. we need a public body for internal control and better redundancy. as the report suggests, we should amend the staff regulations and the disciplinary procedure.

- (sv) mr president, commissioner, i recently visited the new bridge over the öresund strait, which links sweden to denmark. this bridge is part of the european transport network. it serves to permanently link scandinavia to the european continent. it should facilitate transport between sweden, finland and the rest of europe. however, the difference in voltage between national rail systems poses a problem. part of the track will have to be unelectrified to allow the train to operate between sweden and denmark. sweden and finland have different rail gauges. the only line that crosses the two countries is the one between haparanda and torneå. systems to adapt the train to the track spacing are being tested. but there are also very positive examples of cooperation. the ore convoys between northern sweden and northern norway are driven by a single locomotive driver who himself crosses the border. this is working satisfactorily, thanks to the cooperation between countries and also between the social partners. the eu currently has 15 separate rail systems, which are often not compatible. this means that in the railway sector we have neither free movement nor the single market. if we really want to create a competitive international railway network, we must also create a common railway structure throughout europe. the harmonization of rail systems is particularly important for countries and regions with a dispersed position in the european territory. to travel within the european union, swedish trains, for example, have to cross several borders and therefore change railway systems several times. this is now a clear handicap in the face of competition, hence the call for the countries at the centre of the eu to harmonise their systems. for the same reason, it is also important that countries which share a common border with the eu participate in this harmonisation work, so that traffic to or from third countries is not restricted. to achieve interoperability, a series of technical components must be harmonised. however, it is at least as important that the same work be done in terms of administration and operation, that the staff be prepared for this and that agreements be drawn up for the crossing of the frontiers. traffic on european roads is increasing year by year. this increase is causing problems, both in terms of environmental disturbance and noise, as well as congestion and accidents. to meet these needs, transport must become cleaner, quieter, more efficient and safer. the train is currently the means of transport which best meets these criteria. this is why rail transport must be made more attractive and more competitive. to achieve this, however, the train must compete on the same terms as other modes of transport. the savary report is therefore a very important text, which represents a first step in the right direction. it is now up to all member states to put it into practice.

going back to rail has been almost halved. it is impossible to force freight and passengers back on the train. the rail will only win again on the market if it becomes more efficient and if it has the opportunity to develop in this direction on its own. if this is not the case, there is a clear risk that the european rail network, at least as far as freight transport is concerned, will disappear within a few decades. the commission is therefore taking a step in the right direction when it seeks what it calls interoperability in this sector. the next step will be to ensure that the eu's internal market is open to the countries of central and eastern europe. common railway operating systems and competitive pricing would help the railways to operate, but if we want the train to survive in the long term, at least two additional measures are needed. first of all, the diversity of operators is essential. just as european roads are paved by many road transport companies, there should be a large number of competing rail transport companies. in addition, a distinction should be made between operators and owners of national railways. we are using this model in sweden, and i think it would be great if a similar system were to be introduced in all the eu member states. if we do not do this, the confusion between the state and the operators will be a handicap for the european rail network. in the long term, this means that there is still a lot of work to be done, even after the decisions which the european parliament is about to take as a result of this report, but these are important steps.

but the sky is not enough for us. women make up more than half the world's population, and they are still vastly underrepresented in most political, economic and social spheres. this is not acceptable. over the centuries, women have demanded the right to be considered full citizens, from the suffragettes once active in the north to the current movements against female genital mutilation in the south. at the beijing conference in 1995, a joint action programme was agreed on by these different movements - a milestone in the recognition of women's rights as an integral part of human rights. almost all the governments of the world have signed up to this programme of action of the beijing conference. the beijing declaration identifies 12 critical areas and sets out long-term goals and intentions for governments, the united nations, grassroots movements and private enterprises to adopt in order to improve conditions for women. five years later, discrimination against women is still as common and violence against women is also a global phenomenon. a glass ceiling keeps women from advancement in the areas of professional life, administration and politics. women represent the overwhelming majority of the estimated one billion human beings who live in extreme poverty and illiteracy, and the decisions that affect women's lives are made by men everywhere. it is high time, at the beijing +5 conference in june, to take concrete decisions which will actually result in the achievement of the objectives set out in the action programme. the report on which we will be voting tomorrow stresses the need for progress in each of the 12 areas mentioned, if human rights are to be applied to women. a three-pronged strategy is needed to ensure that the conference does not become just another rhetorical exercise. the world's governments must first adopt a concrete programme of action and recommit themselves to the beijing goals. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. these three poles have so far been missing. the fact that governments refuse to acknowledge that girls and women, from the cradle to the grave, are exploited and persecuted is, since the dawn of time, the most blatant and widespread violation of human rights in the world. this is something the committee on women's rights and equal opportunities wants to change. women should not only support half of the sky, but also have the right to take responsibility for decisions that affect the entire earth. isn't that right, commissioner?

reasoning is that we should try to limit as much as possible the consumption of cigarettes, including, of course, the consumption of nicotine. we need to reduce the harmful substances in cigarettes, the rapporteur. - (sv) mr president, commissioner, we know that this question, which is now being considered for third and final reading in parliament, is a serious problem for mankind. the ozone layer, which is vital to life on earth, is getting thinner by the day. perhaps this phenomenon is most evident in the northern hemisphere, but it nonetheless has effects on all living things on a planetary scale. we also know how we have to deal with this problem. the equation seems simple, but unfortunately it's more complicated than it looks. on an issue like this, there are so many parties involved. what we must definitely take a stand on in this house is a regulation which provides us with stricter rules, but also acceptable margins which allow the industry to reconvert. i believe that we have succeeded in producing a legislative proposal which is a good incentive for change and which also encourages environmental foresight. our task was to develop an acceptable plan for future evolution. with this regulation we want to show that we can beat the deadlines set by the montreal protocol, and we know that the union industry will succeed in its conversion, even though certain industrial sectors have done everything they could to prevent this directive from coming into being. parliament adopted the council's common position on second reading with 10 amendments. seven of these amendments were accepted by the council without any problems. the others, namely amendments nos 14, 15 and 21, have remained unresolved, and that is why we entered into informal negotiations with the council at the beginning of the year. both the council and parliament intended to reach an agreement without going through a formal conciliation procedure, in order to avoid any delay in the entry into force of the directive. the first amendment concerns hcfcs in certain air conditioning equipment which parliament had proposed to ban. during the negotiations it was decided to reintroduce them, but with a more stringent phasing-out schedule. the second amendment was a total ban on hcfcs in refrigeration and air conditioning equipment, which the council was less favourable to. the result of the negotiations was to delay their abolition until 2015, with the adoption of a revision clause. i consider this to be one of the most important amendments parliament has helped to obtain through its negotiations with the council. the third amendment provided for an extension of certain deadlines: on this point too parliament and the council were able to agree on an acceptable solution. it could therefore have been hoped that if we decided to adopt the conciliation committee's proposal, the case would be closed. but three problems remain. during the last stage, three errors were discovered in the proposed legislation. the export ban had inadvertently been imposed on inhalers of anti-asthmatic medicines and pumps of analgesic preparations for cancer patients. in addition, the year of departure indicated for imports of cfcs by some companies was incorrect. the commission will now put forward supplementary proposals which, according to our timetable, will be discussed at the july plenary session, and which will be aimed at correcting these errors. it was never intended to prohibit the exports in question, nor to set an unrealistic date for the application of the import quotas of these companies. however, i would like the commission to explain how this could have happened and whether it believes that this is the best way of solving the problem. i would like to conclude by thanking the commission and the portuguese presidency, as well as parliament's conciliation committee, for their excellent cooperation. all have done their best to develop a valid regulation, and have respected the importance of its entry into force as soon as possible. i think we have found a compromise that works for all parties. it does not repair the hole in the ozone layer, but it is a step in the right direction. i hope that parliament will accept this proposal today or tomorrow and that we will have better legislation on ozone-depleting substances in the european union by october.
