- (sv) madam president, this case concerns the abolition of the swedish exception to the internal market rules on the import of alcohol and tobacco. this may seem a minor detail in the multitude of regulations that govern the internal market, but swedish alcohol policy has proved effective in tackling the consequences of alcohol abuse. this policy is based in particular - but not exclusively - on high taxes, which lead to high consumer prices. it also includes a state-owned monopoly on alcohol distribution and active efforts to keep alcohol consumption out of certain areas of society, such as road traffic. although sweden is part of the so-called 'vodka belt' with its specific consumption patterns, and despite the existence of personal consumption distilleries and smuggling, the overall level of consumption is low. the overall result, if you add up all the accidents, including traffic accidents, alcohol-related illnesses and deaths, is less damage and less public costs. this policy is of course more difficult to apply without protection in the form of import restrictions, and that is why sweden wanted to obtain either a permanent exception or at least - a demand on which the swedish government and the commission have now agreed - a delay of a few more years before the exception is abolished. we simply need time to prepare new measures in the field of alcohol policy to reduce the risks that the removal of import restrictions may bring. in the long term, it would probably not be possible to isolate sweden from the rest of europe in this area. we already see that the quantities imported by private individuals from denmark and germany are large. the commission has already taken a number of measures to improve the quality of the products it produces, and has also proposed a number of measures to improve the quality of the products it produces. there are also alarming reports of private individuals manufacturing alcohol underground, smuggling it, and so on. even if taxes on alcohol were set at national level, as they should remain, it would be impossible in the long term to maintain the large differences between the excise duties of neighbouring countries without problems. the high level of excise duties in sweden creates difficulties for swedish producers and retailers of beer, wine and spirits, as well as for the more serious sector of the hotel and catering industry. in these circumstances, it seems inevitable that sweden will gradually reduce its taxes on alcohol. it is not certain that it will be necessary to bring them down to the levels prevailing on the european continent, but some steps in this direction will probably be necessary. several eu countries do not tax beer or wine, which is also questionable from a public health point of view. perhaps taxes should be imposed on these products in these countries. i am glad that the rapporteur, mr maaten, is taking the question of public health seriously, instead of seeing the alcohol problem as a single problem for the internal market, as many do. excessive alcohol consumption is a public health problem which must be brought to our attention, including at european level. for the nordic countries, the risk is that there will be a rapid change in consumption patterns, which will lead to a large number of people adding the old tradition of drinking water to the european tradition of beer and wine, with serious consequences for their health. the next two countries to hold the presidency, france and sweden, have indicated their intention to tackle this issue together and to propose joint efforts in the field of public health, including the role of alcohol. i'm glad to hear it. this is an important initiative. how will the swedish government deal with the problem, then, when the borders are gradually opened and the quantities of alcohol imported increase, while at the same time, in all likelihood, taxes are reduced? i believe in the effectiveness, in dealing with traffickers, of a long-term offensive, in the form of information, behavioural work and educational efforts in schools and care centres, and in particular popular education through associative movements, etc. all this is necessary and must be firmly established in the long term. in this context, i therefore agree with the rapporteur's conclusion that parliament must accept the agreement drawn up, at the request of the ecofin council ministers, by the swedish government and the commission, and which gives sweden until the end of 2003 to reduce import restrictions to the level that is current on the internal market. we shall then have achieved the twofold objective of adapting to the rules of the internal market and of having the time to take the swedish alcohol policy on the offensive in order to avoid the harmful consequences of this change.
- (sv) madam president, sweden has a different alcohol policy from the rest of europe. social policy in this area has its roots in a history marked by the heavy scourge of alcoholism. for a long time, this view of alcohol has managed to hold up against outside influences. that is no longer the case today. the internationalisation of lifestyles, the increasing mobility of the population within the eu and membership of the eu have changed the situation. the exemption for imports of alcohol and tobacco which has been in force until now was not permanent. the new agreement concluded between the swedish government and the commission provides for a gradual increase in import quotas. it is a good compromise which gives sweden the opportunity to adapt to these new conditions. mr maaten has written a good report. it does not advocate the idea that alcohol is simply an agricultural issue, as is claimed in some parts of europe. the maaten report, on the contrary, makes it clear that we must also begin to treat it as a public health issue. the swedish alcohol policy has kept both total alcohol consumption and alcohol-related diseases at a low level, while the system of monopoly distribution and high taxation in sweden is under heavy pressure from an open-border europe. a large proportion of alcohol consumed in sweden is now made up of contraband or clandestine spirits. trade between sweden and its neighbouring countries is flourishing. the increase in import quotas gives sweden the opportunity to gradually adjust its excise duty and alcohol policy along the lines followed in other european countries. we must reduce the excise duty in sweden, but perhaps also increase it in some eu countries. sweden can no longer pursue a policy based on border controls and higher taxes in neighbouring countries. it is time, now, to find new solutions. i think that cooperation with other countries is what we need, and an effective information strategy must dominate future alcohol policy. we need a sensible policy, marked by social responsibility and greater empowerment of individuals. we need more europe in sweden, but perhaps also more sweden in europe.
- (sv) madam president, it is rather curious to hear swedish alcohol policy being discussed in the european parliament. that is the subject of this report. the swedish government made a solemn promise before the referendum on membership that swedish policy in this area would remain intact. and now the social democratic government, in agreement with the left party and the moderate party, has just given an informal promise - that is the term used in the commission's text - that it would accept the commission's proposal to gradually reduce by the year 2003 the restrictions on the import of alcohol and tobacco, and then to abolish them. the abolition of the rules on import means, in short, the dismantling of swedish alcohol policy. it will put very strong pressure on a policy whose sole backbone is high consumer prices, in other words a high taxation of alcohol. four parties represented in the swedish parliament oppose the line taken by the commission and the government: the christian democrats, the liberal party, whose views we have just heard, the centre party and the green party. these groups demand that the government should opt for the continuation of the swedish exception and plead in its favour until the council has unanimously taken another decision. it is very important to examine the policy of the eu itself in this area. what would the european court of justice say? as the maaten report very carefully points out, this is not just a question of the internal market, but also a question of public health. alcohol and tobacco are the main causes of public health problems in the eu, they represent heavy costs for society, great suffering on an individual scale, etc. articles 152 and 95 (3) of the treaty refer to the need for a high level of protection of individuals, and article 30 does admit the possibility of restrictions on imports, with particular reference to public health. sweden has chosen another method, which has proved successful in the field of alcohol policy. alcohol-related harm is less severe in sweden than in most of the rest of the world, and alcohol consumption is very low compared to eu countries. i do not know whether to laugh or cry, because it should be noted that the eu member states adopted a second action plan against alcohol last year, drawn up by the world health organization. the plan calls for higher excise duties on alcohol to be applied by the member states and for the minimum taxation within the eu to be raised. these are among the recommendations which have been formulated as part of a strategy for public health. why should we be sweeping away swedish alcohol policy when the european public health strategy sets out the same guidelines for reducing alcohol-related harm?
- (sv) madam president, the maaten report establishes that alcohol is the second most important risk factor for health in the eu after tobacco, that alcohol abuse is on the increase and that it is responsible for a growing number of pathologies and social and economic problems in a number of eu countries. the report also states that sweden, largely thanks to its responsible alcohol policy, has a lower level of alcohol consumption and a lower number of alcohol-related pathological cases than other member states. the eu should therefore benefit from sweden's positive experience. it would be desirable to deal with alcohol in the same way as we are beginning to deal with tobacco, nicotine, etc. instead of forcing sweden to dismantle its health policy to adapt to the european union, we should instead see the eu adapt to sweden and try to deal with the growing problems caused by alcohol. in this area, i would like to see a convergence policy where countries with large and growing alcohol problems align themselves with the effective policy in sweden. i shall therefore vote against the commission's proposal, which mr maaten is advocating in a manner which is totally contradictory to the content of an otherwise remarkable report. i would like to conclude by saying that the swedish government has just given in to the demands of the market economy, which excludes a public health perspective. i have heard that many of my colleagues in this parliament share this view: that sweden should bow, for example, even though public health reasons argue the complete opposite. i don't agree with that. i believe that there are objective reasons for the eu to rethink its alcohol policy, and the experience gained in sweden could play an important role in this. the swedish approach to alcohol should not be broken, but should be used constructively for the greater good of the eu.
- (sv) madam president, i am concerned about the extension of the deadline that sweden has just negotiated. i think it's time to end this. i think we should have the same rules as the internal market, and i will therefore vote in favour of that. the swedish government knew that the exemption was due to expire at the end of the first half of the year, and it has done far too little to tackle the alcohol abuse that exists in the country. i am particularly concerned about the drinking habits of young people in relation to alcohol, as well as the massive access to it and its illegal distribution. sweden's alcohol policy is now in a state of failure. at the same time, we have a government in sweden which, under popular pressure, is trying to respond to the general wish that access to alcohol should be made easier. the swedish state monopoly which owns the distribution of alcohol now opens its doors on saturdays, and multiplies its points of sale. in many ways, access to alcohol is also increasing because of the increase in the number of people drinking. but it is above all the volume of illegal sales of alcohol, beer and wine that has increased, to the detriment of employment, tax revenues and controls. there are far too few measures to tackle the obvious abuses, while ordinary consumers remain under excessive supervision and have very different rights when it comes to bringing back alcohol from holidays in, for example, denmark or france. i'm worried about this inability to address the real issues. at present, only a little over 30% of alcohol sales in sweden pass through channels controlled by the authorities. the rest is illegal, smuggled in by individuals who take on rations that are too generous in the eyes of politicians, organized gangs, raw food boilers, etc. that is why i say that this swedish policy based on high prices and difficult access to alcohol is a failure. mr. göran färm, the state has had enough time to act! - (sv) madam president, by what right does the eu make things more difficult for sweden in terms of alcohol policy and the fight against drugs? what makes alcohol importation more of a business than a public health issue? in the 1994 referendum on swedish eu membership, swedish citizens were told that the derogation would be extended beyond the year 2000. the temperance movement and many citizens, including myself, felt satisfied with this prospect. on 13 march, in this house, mr guterres, prime minister of the portuguese presidency, spoke of cultural diversity as a wealth. he said that there was not just one common european opinion, but fifteen different arenas of public opinion. the european union, through the commissioner responsible, mr bolkestein, is now seeking to standardize the conditions under which member states can pursue a national policy on alcohol that has been the subject of democratic decisions. mr bolkestein and the commission argue that the way the internal market works requires that each citizen has the alleged right to bring 210 litres of alcohol across the border of his country every day. for a family with three adult children, that's seven tons of alcohol a week. is that what you call a right? is that a moderate level of consumption ? the policy of our country should not be about protecting citizens, but about solidarity. it is a matter of taking the problems of alcohol and drug addiction seriously and in a spirit of solidarity. in this context, and in view of the concern about the euro 2000 because of hooliganism and alcohol abuse, the commission's decision and the attitude of the swedish social democratic government appear to be absurd to say the least.
- (sv) madam president, the special rules that finland applies to beer imports are of course as important for public health as they are for economic life. the problem is exacerbated by the proximity of third countries, in practice russia and estonia. but it is clear that special rules are needed with regard to customs duties vis-à-vis these third countries. in finland, as in sweden and norway, there is, as was noted in the previous debate on swedish alcohol policy, a very specific policy line in this area. it is based, in particular, on high taxation and the principle that high prices lead to a reduction in consumption. in finland too, this policy has proved successful in terms of the fight against alcohol-related harm. despite the traditional patterns of consumption in finland and sweden, and the phenomena of clandestine manufacture and sale, this relatively low total consumption actually leads to less damage and lower costs for society. on this point i think there is enough convincing evidence to show that the rapporteur, mrs lulling, is wrong when she says that high taxation does not reduce consumption, or that restrictive policy is not effective. a great deal of research proves otherwise. moreover, the united nations in particular is now advocating a policy on alcohol based on these experiences of the nordic countries, recommending that other european countries follow the same path. such a policy of restriction will naturally be more difficult to implement without the protection of import restrictions. this is why finland, like sweden, asked for a delay until 2003 before increasing beer imports from the eu. we simply need time to prepare the new alcohol policy, with measures to reduce the risks that the removal of import restrictions may bring. the problems facing sweden are even more acute in the case of finland. they are accentuated by the proximity of non-eu neighbours, russia and estonia, where the price and tax levels are not comparable to those in the union. this proximity between southern finland and estonia and this enormous difference in prices and taxes have led finland and the european commission to want the restrictions on imports from these countries to be maintained for two more years, that is, until 2005, which seems to me quite reasonable. however, the rapporteur, mrs lulling, did not want to accept this proposal. it called for the same deadline to be applied to third countries and the internal market. the economic and monetary affairs committee, to my great satisfaction, has taken the line advocated by the finnish government, the commission and myself, considering that these large differences between price and tax levels do indeed justify an additional two-year adjustment period, unless estonia becomes an eu member by then, in which case it would be natural that the european rules should also apply to estonia. the risks are primarily in the area of public health, but they also affect national production and sales of beer in border regions. mrs lulling wanted to use the report to advocate a reduction in alcohol taxes in finland. i admit that it may be difficult in the long term to maintain such high taxes, as the number of people travelling outside finland's borders is increasing and they have the opportunity to import alcohol privately. but as commissioner bolkestein has said, it is nevertheless an important principle that taxation is a national matter and should not be the subject of formal harmonization in the internal market. however, it may be necessary for the finnish government to initiate a reduction in its taxation to bring it in line with that of the eu and thus avoid the serious problems which may arise when restrictions on private imports of beer are gradually relaxed to the level of the rest of the internal market. however, i find it rather regrettable that the rapporteur, who is known for defending the interests of wine growers, does not take public health problems seriously at all, and that unlike the rapporteur on the swedish restrictions on alcohol imports, mr maaten, she views the matter only from the point of view of taxation and the internal market. it is clear that the importation of cheap alcohol from estonia and russia is already causing undeniable social and medical problems. i therefore welcome the fact that the next presidency, as i understand it, intends to deal in the course of the next year with the question of public health efforts and the role of alcohol in this respect. but it is certain that many new methods will be needed. in this context, i propose that we support the proposal of the committee on economic and monetary affairs to accept the agreement between finland and the eu.
- (sv) mr president, last month we celebrated europe day. the keynote address delivered by robert schuman on may 9, 1950, was the centerpiece of the celebration. much has changed since then, but the basic principle advocated by schuman and monnet, namely that lasting peace is created by the mutual commitment of peoples and nations in a mutually enriching interdependence, remains valid. security is simply built through cooperation, and through integration and enlargement. schuman began his speech by saying: "world peace cannot be safeguarded without constructive action against the dangers which threaten it". this visionary perspective permeates the socialist group's position on foreign and security policy and is also the main theme of mrs lalumière's report for the feira summit, which our group fully endorses. for the social democrats, the challenge for security policy in the 2000s has two dimensions, which form a whole. this is a global dimension on the one hand and a purely european dimension on the other. from a global perspective, it is clear that security is indivisible. we share the same planet and the same destiny. in today's globalised world, after the end of the cold war, poverty and exclusion are the worst enemies of peace and democracy. no stockpile of weapons, no new nuclear arms race can provide guarantees against the bitterness of the poor. that is why generous, fair and farsighted aid and a trade policy that tackles the causes of poverty and insecurity must be an integral part of our foreign and security policy. this is how we envisage conflict prevention and crisis management in our new european security policy. of course, we are loyal to the decisions taken in helsinki. we must constructively develop the military capacity that has been established by force of circumstances, particularly because of the war in kosovo. the objectives set are essential and must be achieved in full without delay or exception. but we must resist those who try to exaggerate our military capability and role. we must take greater responsibility for security on our own continent, but we must not replace nato or create the embryo of a permanent european defence. such ambitions belong to the world of the unreal: they must be left to don quixote. we lack a policy to prevent conflict. that is why we must actively support the proposals put forward by commissioner patten and advocated by members of the council, as well as those contained in mrs lalumière's report.

in writing. -sv) it is illogical to ban snuff altogether in the eu, while cigarettes and other tobacco products are allowed throughout the union. the ban should therefore be lifted, which is in force in all eu countries except sweden. the advantage of snuff is that it does not harm those around you. while tobacco smoke bothers and causes illness in nonsmokers, especially those with allergies, the person who uses snuff is exposing himself to the possible harmful consequences of smoking. chewing tobacco is addictive. the tobacco industry should therefore not introduce this product on the market as a product with beneficial properties. the directive on the sale of tobacco products, which will shortly ban tobacco advertising in the eu, must be preserved and applied to all tobacco products.
in writing. -sv) the swedish christian democrats voted today for stricter rules for the tobacco industry, including the requirement to provide a larger warning space on cigarette packs (40%) than the commission proposal (25%). we also support the banning of any wording that describes cigarettes as 'lighter' and the rapid abolition of tobacco growing aid in the eu. these aids are in direct contradiction to the eu's work and measures to improve public health. we also want the exceptional situation of greece to be abolished. the committee also welcomed the commission's proposal to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'smoking tobacco' and to include the use of the term 'smoking tobacco' in the definition of the term 'smoking tobacco'. sweden must not, in the name of free trade and the internal market, impose products such as snuff on countries and populations that do not want them. the commission should have shown the same respect for swedish alcohol policy by extending and then making permanent the exemption granted to sweden in 1994 on private alcohol imports.
in writing. -sv) finland is experiencing serious problems due to the massive increase in beer imports from third countries, mainly from estonia and russia. the commission's six-year derogation for finland to apply a new limit on these imports is therefore a very positive measure. finland is thus entitled to limit the quantity of beer brought in from a third country to six litres per traveller, a provision which applies from this date until 1 january 2006. this case shows that the commission recognises that in the case of finland, the quasi-freedom of private alcohol imports is not just a matter of trade and taxation but is more of a public health and social alcohol policy to combat the ravages of excessive consumption. the commission should have shown the same foresight in dealing with the swedish exception of 1994 on alcohol imports into sweden. the commission and the member responsible for the matter, mr bolkestein, are thus removing the backbone of the instruments used by swedish alcohol policy. these instruments, which relate to the limitation of imports and high taxation, were the subject of democratic decisions by the swedish parliament and government.

in writing. - (sv) since its accession to the eu, sweden has been allowed to apply special quotas to imports of alcoholic beverages and tobacco for personal use. i was involved as minister of agriculture in the negotiations which led to this exception to article 26 of directive 92/12/eec, with a view to sweden's accession in 1994. this exception has already been extended once. my assessment of this problem can only be that it was a temporary exception for sweden, which was to expire one day and give way to the community directive in force. the swedish government has, by its attitude in the debate in recent years, helped to conceal this fact. consequently, although the exact implications of this exception were known, the necessary steps to adapt both the national market and the swedish alcohol policy mechanism were also avoided. after informally promising the commission that it would accept its proposal, the swedish government must now recognise the need to completely reform its alcohol policy and find other methods of controlling alcohol consumption in the country and limiting the damage it causes. it is time that this evidence reached the doors of the ministries. if i take into account the outcome of the negotiations prior to our accession and the way in which the social democratic government has dealt with this matter, i see no reason why i should not vote in favour of the report.
in writing. - (sv) since sweden's accession to the eu, the swedish christian democrats have been working to make the swedish exception to directive 92/12/eec on the movement of goods, as regards alcohol imports, permanent. we believe that this is the condition for the continuation of the effective policy which has resulted in sweden having a lower level of alcohol-related harm than the rest of the eu, and therefore good public health outcomes. the commission now refutes sweden's argument and focuses on the functioning of the internal market, and we risk seeing sweden's alcohol policy suffer significant damage. it is also inconsistent to ban the export of swedish snuff to other eu countries on grounds of public health, while ignoring the health aspects of alcohol imports in the name of the free market. we find it very regrettable that the swedish social democratic government has not chosen to take a more firm stand on this matter. consulting the european court on the legitimacy of the swedish exception on the public health argument was an opportunity which was not used. we consider that the swedish government and the european commission have acted wrongly in this matter, and we therefore decide to express our disapproval by abstaining in the vote on the extension of the swedish exception to the directive on the movement of goods.

in writing. - (da) we shall vote against the resolution as a whole, because it stresses the wish to develop community cooperation by giving it a military dimension. we cannot support the very important passage which recognises the need to militarize the eu's common defence, because such a will shows a lack of respect for the non-aligned countries of the union. we cannot accept the more concrete proposals contained in the resolution: an increase in defence budgets in the various member states, coordination of the purchase of military equipment and coordination and the creation of a real army within the union. we agree with the need to develop a common code of conduct for the trade in arms between the countries of the union and with third countries, but we cannot accept the creation of a single market for the arms industry as proposed in the resolution.
in writing. - (sv) the same text as that which was discussed today in the european parliament is found in its main outlines in a proposal which mrs lalumière presented to the committee on foreign affairs in may and which was voted on in june. the question will therefore be read again in accordance with the ordinary procedure at one of the partsession sessions next autumn. i find it surprising and difficult to understand the interest of this duplicate work. that is why i have decided to abstain completely on all the votes on this matter, which is of fundamental importance for a non-aligned country like sweden, which has been fortunate enough to live in peace since 1814.
in the case of the united states, the european union is not a party to the treaty of rome, but it is a party to the treaty of rome. the european union has the political, economic and diplomatic resources to do so. the resolution does say that it is desirable to use non-military means in the first place, but this does not exclude the use of armed forces. i cannot support the idea that military resources are being used to defend the interests of the european union. i believe that military measures should only be taken by decision of the united nations security council. it is not enough to adhere to the principles set out in the charter of the united nations. i shall therefore not be voting in favour of the resolution.
- (sv) mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, full employment is the objective that the lisbon summit assigned to the work of the eu. that's a big step forward. work is the guarantee of peace and freedom in europe. this should apply to the whole of the eu territory, including regions far from the geographical centre of the union. the commission's report on the employment objective calls for all eu policies to work together in the same direction. we must use regional policy, both on a small and a large scale. in sweden, large-scale efforts include social protection policy in general, with social services, training, research, infrastructure and culture throughout the union. the importance of this large-scale policy cannot be overemphasized. small-scale measures are aid to enterprises and the structural funds, i.e. more targeted support for certain sectors under very specific conditions. these may include, for example, industrial conversion operations, areas with a low standard of living, but also areas such as northern sweden and finland, where population densities are low, distances are long and the climate is harsh. in these regions, sweden has long used reduced social security contributions as a form of aid to enterprises. this is now being called into question by the commission, which i find difficult to understand. the major efforts made by the eu through the structural funds in these regions need to be effective if the other parts of regional policy are to function. the structural funds cannot be effective in isolation. this is a point which we draw attention to in the report as well. i would like to hear the commissioner's comments on this principle of the various sectors of politics working together. the structural funds are therefore of vital importance and must function properly. the criticisms i have heard of them in the region i represent are about the administrative complexity involved in their use. we must help to remedy this at all levels. i support all the ideas which aim at decentralising administration, partnership and using funds according to the needs of the regions. mechanisms for evaluating the results obtained must also be integrated into the system. finally, mr president, i would like to stress that the whole of the union must benefit from european growth. i agree with the person who said that every place on earth where people live is the center of the world.
in writing. - (sv) respect for human rights is an issue that requires international attention. when the rights of individuals are violated, other countries have a duty to offer refuge to those seeking protection from persecution. countries around the world have a shared responsibility to give refugees a humane welcome. no country can escape this responsibility when refugees seek protection from them. asylum law practice varies from one eu member state to another, but in general refugee policy has become more stringent in recent years. harmonization between member states is necessary if countries are to be able to pursue a generous policy towards refugees. we therefore welcome, in principle, the commission working document and the report by mr ingo schmitt on the development of a common policy towards asylum seekers and refugees, but we would like to stress that the least generous countries should not be the ones to set the standards in this area. we want a policy which distributes the burden of refugees more evenly among the various european countries, provided that the policy of europe and sweden is more humane in the future than it is today. it should also be borne in mind that refugees and immigrants have a positive impact on member states. they often have experience and know-how that they bring to our societies. immigration is also playing a particularly important role at the present time because of the labour shortages which are becoming more and more acute in the member states. with regard to the report by mr ingo schmitt, we would like to stress that asylum applications must always be examined individually, on the basis of the merits of each man and woman concerned, and that this must be the norm, even in so-called mass migration situations. it is also important that refugees should, in principle, have the same social benefits as other persons resident in the country which is dealing with the asylum application. this fundamental principle, enshrined in the geneva convention, must be guaranteed and respected. we also believe that it is essential to review and broaden the current definition of refugee and asylum seeker as set out in the geneva convention to include persons seeking asylum on the basis of their gender or sexual orientation.

- (sv) mr president, the fight against drugs is one of our most important tasks as citizens and politicians. this applies equally to the citizens sitting in this house and those sitting in the stands. drugs are the root of crime, social misery, and the ruin of many lives. it is therefore positive that the candidate countries and norway are now considering taking part in this joint work which already exists. in order to make a real assessment and to obtain reliable statistics, however, we need to define a strategy to determine what information the member states should provide to the observatory. without this, the foundation is rather thin and serves no greater purpose. however, it is not only the responsibility of the observatory, but also the responsibility of the respective member states to ensure that the information sent is as relevant and useful as possible. as far as measures to combat drug addiction are concerned, responsibility now lies primarily with the member states. this should not, however, prevent us from seeking to develop common missions and strategies for prevention work, which is arguably the most important cornerstone in the fight against drugs. the union and the observatory should also be able to play the role of monitor of national drug production, as one of the previous speakers said. we cannot lose sight of the fact that a large proportion of the drugs that exist today and that young people use when they go out are synthetic drugs, produced in the union and nowhere else. the free movement of goods today makes this production problem more important than we would like it to be. i would add that the definition of a drug-free society is a good one. it is an objective, a dream, like all the other dreams of the union in terms of social misery, and it is one more thing we want to fight. it is an offensive attitude which i hope will be maintained.
- (sv) mr president, i should like to begin by thanking the rapporteurs, mr sousa pinto and mr turco, for their excellent reports. the fight against drug addiction and drug trafficking is paramount. these reports are intended to supplement the existing regulations on the observatory. it is also intended to involve the candidate countries in the work. let me comment on this point first. it is essential that the candidate countries are involved! i'm from sweden myself. in recent years we have established cooperation with the baltic states and poland to prevent drug trafficking and drug addiction. the drug trade is cross-border. cooperation must therefore be cross-border in order to prevent the development of this traffic. this also applies to cooperation with norway. norway participates in the internal market. norway is going to become a member of schengen. norway has more or less the same problem as sweden with regard to drug smuggling across the border. it is therefore essential that we cooperate and gather information from these neighbouring countries and learn about the way in which they conduct their policies. the rapporteur also looks at the way in which the observatory is currently operating. i think he makes a crucial distinction. the observatory should not be involved in politics, we must do so here, in the political assemblies and in the member states, and especially in the member states when it comes to drug policy. the observatory, on the other hand, must lay the foundations for political decision-making. there are currently gaps in this area, particularly in the aggregation of statistics. we need to get relevant, reliable and comparable information. the observatory can also analyse and evaluate the objectives we set at policy meetings so that we can review policies at a later stage. in this area, the observatory needs to improve considerably from where it is at present. the policies that are being applied must be analysed - different types of policy are being pursued in the netherlands and sweden, for example. i think it is essential that we analyse how these different policies on drug use among young people and so on actually look.
- (sv) mr president, mr president-in-office of the council, mr commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the conclusions of the feira summit contain some very fine phrases. the progress of the current intergovernmental conference is being talked about in fine phrases, but in reality no solution is yet in sight. the summit reiterated the importance of rapid enlargement. and yet the candidate countries still want the eu to adopt clear positions on the accession negotiations. we must invest more energy in the process of european integration, not start doubting. we must not confine the applicant countries to a waiting room. we need a new political will, which will lead to more concrete results than those which emerged from the feira summit. at the same time, it is important to be clear about how institutional issues are to be resolved. i am thinking of enlargement, the qualified majority, the number of commissioners and members. the eu must be able to function effectively even after enlargement, and internal eu issues must not delay it. this is why the different processes must be carried out in parallel. the need for a european charter of fundamental rights is also compounded by enlargement. it is imperative to establish the conditions for the rule of law: this is a matter that is not in dispute. however, the eu charter puts european development in a new light. we must build the kind of europe we want - a europe that offers new opportunities and increased competitiveness, innovation and entrepreneurship, the creation of more small and mediumsized enterprises? or do we prefer an increase in social protection supported by public finances and a weak currency? perhaps the mandate of the committee responsible for drawing up this charter should be extended so that it can make proposals for the transformation of the treaty into a constitution, since these issues are so closely linked. there is undoubtedly a direct link between the preamble of a new treaty and the content of the treaty itself. parallel development should create new policy opportunities and move forward, since the different objectives would be directly interdependent. to achieve this parallel implementation of enlargement and a deepening process, a decision in principle could be taken, concerning a charter which is binding for the rule of law part, but not binding for the non-principles part. the committee would then certainly know what status to give to the charter, and could consider it as opening the way to the work of drafting the new treaty. those who give priority to enlargement, as well as those who give priority to deepening, would then have satisfaction. such a development would allow the rapid integration of new member states and, at the same time, create the conditions for a better functioning of the union in the long term. we have a historic responsibility to build bridges across our borders - not to intensify the differences that separate us, as was the case with austria. we must replace short-term measures with deep and long-term cooperation. i think that our fundamental values, which are the undisputed part of the charter, should be able to act as a lever to get the enlargement process moving.

- mr president, commissioners, i will speak in english, not because i reject french or swedish, two languages which i like very much, but because english was the working language of the observer mission. i thank you very much for your kind words to me. it is a pleasure for me to be presenting the final report of the eu election observation mission to zimbabwe for the first time before the european parliament, an institution which has taught me a great deal and in which i have the great pleasure of working. "it was not a perfect election", said opposition leader morgan tsvangirai bluntly. in fact, according to the 190 members of the observation mission, who have an average of seven similar missions, while the pre-election period was among the worst they have ever been to, the actual voting days, with the ballot papers being deposited and the votes counted, were among the best they have ever witnessed. what can we conclude from such a schizophrenic picture, with acts of violence and political intimidation that have claimed the lives of more than 30 people, while many others have been threatened, persecuted, injured, kidnapped and tortured, and voters who still turn out en masse to vote, giving the opposition party almost half of their votes? throughout our mission i have refused to use the term 'free and fair elections' because it is not appropriate, it is too sharp for a process as complex as the one we have witnessed in zimbabwe. that is why our mission from the outset insisted that we were not only there for the electoral process and that our final report would also give a prominent place to the post-electoral phase. the role and sense of responsibility of president mugabe, who has two years left in office, will be decisive in this phase. this is a point i wanted to stress in a conversation i had with him, and in my public statements there, saying that after the revolution, we must build the nation, that after the elections, we must move on to reconciliation. we hoped that despite the widespread campaign of violence and intimidation for which the ruling party, zanu-pf, bore by far the greatest responsibility, citizens, supported by the strong presence of international and national observation missions, would be able to vote in relatively free, fair and non-violent conditions when the day came. in this way, the post-election period would be crucial, as would the role of the president and the zanu-pf leadership, and they would help to create a climate conducive to reconciliation and a return to calm. to some extent, our strategy has proved to be the right one. the citizens voted in large numbers. violence and intimidation did not seem to keep them from voting. many also openly expressed sympathy for the opposition parties. it was not that we were flaunting our ideas; we had a code of conduct dictated by our professionalism and strict neutrality. but we wanted people to know that their vote was secret; that on election day, the president and the peasants are all on the same page and that the people exercise their power through democratic elections. there was a lot of violence and terror during the run-up to the elections and we condemn it. that said, i would like to emphasize that there has been much more nonviolence in those millions of people who have been keen to exercise their democratic right to vote. the main opposition party, while contesting the results in 20 constituencies, also accepted the election results. i believe that the people of zimbabwe, who are crying out for peace, law and order, progress and a decent life, deserve better. let me now sum up our conclusions and recommendations in four points. it is imperative that the government act swiftly to restore the rule of law and to enable the police and the prosecution service to prosecute those who have been guilty of or have encouraged acts of political violence or other human rights violations. it is necessary to set up a new independent electoral commission, free from partisan interests and with sufficient human and financial resources to function effectively. the government must also leave the courts free to rule on any appeal against the electoral results registered in any constituency. he also has to respect their decisions. the european union should ensure that international observers are deployed in time for the presidential elections scheduled for 2002. the european union should make a particular effort in the coming weeks and months to follow developments in zimbabwe closely and to provide assistance and support where needed. i am glad to hear that the presidency and the commission agree with this. to this end, the central unit of our observer mission in harare will remain for some time, as will 25 eu observers deployed throughout the country. the 14 heads of mission will also be on standby. let me take this opportunity to pay tribute to the elections unit, to the eu observers, including our friends from norway and kenya who were brought on board along with five meps. the commission reacted swiftly and appropriately to the council's decision; in addition, their continuous cooperation and dialogue throughout the process has been invaluable. let me conclude by saying that the european union mission was a high-level mission, which was generally very well received, a welcome which several member states, including my own, mr patten, could envy. this type of mission can also be considered as an effective conflict prevention exercise. she was quickly found on the ground and we were many to spread calm and peace; it took very little time to get it up and running, with good national coverage and with a solid strategy, which extended into the post-election period. our mission has been a sign of commitment to the people of africa and to democracy around the world. i regard the mission to zimbabwe as a natural consequence of our support for the struggle for african independence and our desire to build a strong partnership with a peaceful and democratic zimbabwe. - (sv) mr president, it is a good thing that france and belgium have ratified the treaty on the international criminal court, but i am nevertheless very concerned about its development. it is now two years since the treaty of rome was drawn up, and only one fifth of the required states have ratified the establishment of a permanent international criminal court. at this rate, it is unlikely that we will be able to meet the december deadline. i think instead that the eu should lead the way in creating an international legal system and that when the national legal system is not working, the international community should be prepared to take action against the worst criminals. this is particularly true of the attacks on women and girls and the shortcomings that, as we know, have emerged in the constitution and the establishment of war crimes tribunals in the 1990s for rwanda and yugoslavia. it is clear that the current system is not enough to deal with crimes such as forced pregnancy, sexual slavery and systematic rape. the international criminal court needs broad international support to be established and to function effectively. i would also like to ask the council whether it plans to adopt specific initiatives to encourage ratification, including in non-eu states. russia and the united states are essential in this case.
- (sv) mr president, three countries of the union are not part of emu. in these countries, referendums will be held before governments decide on the possible introduction of the euro. two of them, england and denmark, have been wise not to participate in the campaign on the euro. they refused, considering that the discussion on this subject was not yet completed, and in view of the future referendum. the third country, sweden, decided to participate in the campaign, although it did so before the referendum and before a decision was made on whether sweden would adopt the euro. this means that the campaign on the euro will not be, as in other countries, a simple information campaign, but a kind of persuasion campaign which will initiate the electoral battle between the 'yes' and the 'no' campaigners. it is difficult to imagine that the campaign on the euro could be translated into information of a particularly critical nature, since its objective is to strengthen confidence in the euro and the corresponding reform. this means, in practice, that the campaign will enable the swedish government to achieve its political objective, which is to convince the swedish people to vote "yes" in the referendum. the campaign on the euro is 50% funded by european funds and will take place at all levels of society, including in schools, a place where it is not usual to see partisan political campaigns, as will be the case this time. i find it shameful that the swedish government should consider taking advantage of this campaign in this way. if it is carried out as planned, it will be a compromising and embarrassing affair for the eu. i therefore call on the commission to ensure that the agreement with sweden on this campaign is cancelled or amended so that it takes place only after the popular opinion has been expressed in the referendum. i would also ask my colleagues in the house to support amendments nos 7 and 12 when they vote later. the wording of these amendments is intended to ensure that the campaign is not carried out in countries which have not yet decided to participate in emu.
the rapporteur. - (sv) madam president, on a proposal from the commission, the committee on budgets is now recommending, in connection with this matter which was so quickly taken up, that parliament should grant a grant of eur 22 million for the creation of a fund within the framework of the danube committee. the reason is that the danube is blocked, near novi sad, by the remains of bridges destroyed last year by nato bombing. this, together with the fact that the riverbed is lined with mines and explosive devices, is a threat to the development of the balkans, both from an economic and humanitarian point of view. the task is to clean up the river for a five-kilometer stretch in serbian territory, to remove the ruins of the three bridges that were razed, as well as the mines and shells that are still active. eu assistance is essential for this. it is important that this clean-up work can begin during the summer. we would have preferred to have included this item in the supplementary and amending budget just discussed, but this was not possible because of lack of time. that is why the committee on budgets is nevertheless offering its support, so that funds are released and the danube committee can start its work as soon as possible. one of the problems is that part of the work will be carried out on serbian territory, once we have ensured that the sanctions against yugoslavia are respected. we have had contact with the commission on the implementation of the project, its legal basis and financing, and our questions have been answered. the commission proposes that the phare programme should provide eur 7.5 million, ispa eur 7.5 million and obnova eur 7 million for the financing of the eur 22 million. i would simply like the commission to confirm that it will contact the committee on budgets in the event of problems arising from this financing.
in writing. -v) the current trend in transport in europe cannot be seen as a long-term trend. the mass transport of goods by road leads to problems of overloading the road network, noise pollution and environmentally harmful discharges. to stop this trend, goods transport must be converted from road to rail. the railway system we have in europe today, which is actually made up of 15 separate networks, does not allow the train to compete on equal terms with the road. the railways should therefore be liberalised and the freight market opened up. the swedish social democrats, pierre schori, anneli hulthén, jan andersson, maj-britt theorin, göran färm and ewa hedkvist petersen, would like to stress the following points in the debate on the proposal by mr jarzembowski and mr swoboda for recommendations at second reading. one of the most important measures to reduce the railway's competitive disadvantage is to separate the companies owning the infrastructure from those providing rail transport. this distinction should be made as soon as possible, as this is a condition for the different transport companies to be able to compete on the same basis as other modes of transport. fair competition also requires that train fares be based on the marginal cost principle. these rates must be determined by transport costs, such as noise, environmental impact, accident risk, etc. the aim is to establish a tariff system which does not affect competition and does not discriminate against any mode of transport.
in writing. -sv) parliament has the responsibility for giving discharge and for monitoring the way in which the commission has used the union's resources. in order to be able to take an informed decision, parliament must have access to the information it considers it needs. the wording of the framework agreement, and more specifically of annex iii, gives the commission, in the event of a dispute, the task of deciding in the last instance whether a document should be communicated to parliament or not. it is totally unacceptable that the body which is itself being audited should be able to decide what information should be provided to the body conducting the investigation. we consider that this agreement is in contradiction with article 276 of the treaty, which clearly states that parliament must have access to the information it needs. this agreement also covers other aspects of our cooperation with the commission, and in these areas too it represents in some respects a retreat from the promises that were made previously. as regards information on international agreements, a step forward was made before the member states' governments intervened to change the text in the negative direction. the nordic governments also supported such a retreat, which meant that the member states did not even have to justify their possible refusal to see the commission disclose a document which they themselves would have forwarded to it.
in writing. - (sv) on 8 june 2000, the conference of presidents of parliament adopted a framework agreement between parliament and the commission. the vote on this question will take place during the july part-session, and members will not have the opportunity to debate it or table amendments. the chairmen of the parliamentary groups have opted, without any prior inquiry or discussion within their groups, for this framework agreement which deprives the assembly of the right to decide which documents the commission must give it access to. in other words, the elected representatives of the people will not have a real say in the legislative procedure, but will be left in the background, on the sidelines. the group chairmen unanimously encouraged the commission to give members of parliament a legal right of access to documents which are disclosed in other contexts. on 8 june, however, they accepted that mr prodi had not fulfilled all the promises he had made when he became president of the commission. legislation should guarantee every member's right to full information. there may of course be justified exceptions which must be respected. it is undeniable that the commission, as an executive body, has the right to examine different points of view and to present projects in the form of internal working documents, without being obliged to publish them. on the other hand, there can be no doubt that as soon as a document is sent to a body outside the commission, parliament is entitled to receive the same information. when a newspaper publishes material extracted from leaked documents, members of parliament cannot access the documents in question. trainees in the council or the permanent representations of the member states have access to documents which neither members of parliament, nor committee chairmen, nor parliamentary group chairmen, nor rapporteurs have the opportunity to consult. when mr romano prodi came to parliament last spring to present his programme before the establishment of the present commission, he promised that members would have access to documents which were to be given to authorities or bodies outside the commission, or which were to leak to the media. the framework agreement represents, in some respects, improvements compared with the practice which has been in force up to now, and it should not be rejected in its entirety. however, it must at least contain the content of the promises made by mr prodi on behalf of the commission. the closed-door policy is unacceptable to public opinion, elected officials and journalists. members of parliament must fight for their rights as elected representatives.
in writing. - (sv) we abstained on amendments nos 8, 16, 26 and 45 because we believe that they do not reflect clearly enough the necessary balance between, on the one hand, the obligation to communicate information in order to combat money laundering effectively, and, on the other hand, the relationship of trust that must be established between the lawyer or the lawyer and his client, and all the obligations that this entails. it is important that a lawyer is not subject to this obligation to provide information when representing a client in a case. it is more difficult to define the situations in which this obligation will have to apply, however, where the lawyer will have access to important information in the context of what is known as legal advice. we believe that lawyers should be exempt from this obligation to a certain extent, but neither the commission's proposal nor that of parliament seems to us to be sufficiently balanced and precise in this respect to be able to support them. finally, we would like to draw attention to the fact that the proposal contains certain shortcomings in terms of the definition of the terms used, shortcomings which could lead to ambiguity. the words 'lawyer', 'legal practitioner' and 'independent lawyer' are used in the text without any clear indication of the purpose of this distinction. it is important to emphasise that lawyers must be treated as lawyers in countries without a ministry of lawyers.
in writing. -sv) the eu's strict visa policy is a major obstacle for people wishing to travel to europe. in this area, the tightening of requirements in recent years has inevitably resulted in an increase in human trafficking and so-called illegal immigration to eu member states. people who have been smuggled into the territory of the european union, or who have managed to do so in some other way, have often endured tragic trials during this journey. once they arrive in the eu, they have no rights and no social protection. the list of countries whose nationals must have a visa to enter the eu includes more than 130 countries. the number of persons exempt from this requirement is not even 50. this practice of making the requirement to obtain a visa the rule rather than the exception is not worthy of a democratic europe. the basic rule should be that people have the right to enter the eu freely, not the other way around. europe must change its attitude to immigration. in the next few years, the union will be faced with a severe shortage of labour. it is therefore important that we accept and welcome people who seek a better future in the eu member states. a more generous visa policy is also a necessary condition for us to be able to pursue a common policy of a humane nature towards refugees and asylum seekers. a policy of accepting immigration would help to combat organised crime and the human tragedies caused by trafficking in human beings.
in writing. - we voted against paragraph 9 of the haug report, concerning the transfer of funds from the agricultural policy budget to the reconstruction programme for the western balkan countries. estimates show that a significant surplus will be available under heading 1a of the agricultural budget. since a large part of the reconstruction programme for the western balkans is still unfunded, we think it would be wise to transfer part of this surplus to heading 4 of the budget, should a revision of the financial perspective prove necessary. the reconstruction of the western balkan countries is one of the priorities on our political agenda. this is also one of the most important tasks of the european union for the years to come. we agree with the haug report that the financing problems cannot be solved by redefining the union's priorities in foreign policy alone. the funds must be found elsewhere, but this need not necessarily generate a need for 'fresh' money from the member states. since a surplus will be available in the agricultural budget, a reasonable solution would be to lower the ceiling of heading 1a in order to increase the budget for foreign policy.
the rapporteur. - (sv) mr president, the report on phthalates in toys was drawn up for two reasons: firstly, phthalates are a problem for the health of young children, and secondly, several member states have already adopted laws in this field, and the report is therefore aimed at re-establishing the single market for toys. when we deal with issues that involve the health of young children, our commitment is usually strong, whole and sincere. according to the world health organization experts, it would not be possible to prove that phthalates are carcinogenic. however, the who believes that they can cause hormonal and reproductive disorders, especially in those who have been exposed to large amounts of the substance during early childhood. threshold values have been set for phthalates on the basis of scientific data, as for other toxic substances. these values determine a recommended maximum exposure level, based on a calculation of the risk levels. i would like to emphasize that this case is not outside the scope of the routine work carried out in the field of toxicology. the scientific committee on toxicity, ecotoxicity and the environment of the commission has assessed the risks presented by a certain amount of phthalates. in doing so, it found that there was a risk of exceeding the threshold values for the two types of phthalates predominantly present in the composition of toys and childcare articles, which could cause health problems in young children. this scientific assessment raises concerns about the health of young children. this is the case with children whose teeth are forming and who bite or suck on various objects in their hands. i would like to warn the house against any drift which would lead it to take a decision which goes far beyond the scientific data on which this view is based. i am in favour of the desire to inform and enlighten consumers so that they can make their own choices, but the labelling proposed by the commission is not information - it is a stern warning. anyone reading this warning will be reluctant to buy the toys that are for their children or grandchildren. so it will be an indirect ban. in addition, the labelling procedure for toys is difficult and expensive. the traders and the traders' organisations explained to me that the salesmen would not have the time or the expertise to answer all the questions that the customers would ask about this. the application of this warning system to toys intended for older children may in practice result in an effective and widespread ban. the scientific committee identified two types of phthalates in the materials used in the toys which could pose health risks to young children. a decision to partially ban would be based primarily, not on the precautionary principle, but on a routine approach applicable to all toxic chemicals. to ban the 350 or so types of phthalates known today would be to admit that all of them are associated with more or less the same risks. such a supposition would be without scientific foundation. since these phthalates are not used in the manufacture of toys, the proposal for additional risk studies and a revision of this directive within two years should be sufficient. i wanted to emphasize in this brief presentation of the document that the decision must be based on scientific reasons and apply to the risk group identified by the studies, that is to say young children. i also wanted to emphasize the difficulty of implementing this labelling measure. there is as yet no scientifically recognised method of carrying out the migration test which would partially replace the proposed legislation. the legislative text should therefore not contain any wording expressly referring to such a method. the various speakers will discuss their own estimates of the documentation presented by scientific research. let me preface the content of their speeches by saying that the political class must be attentive to scientific studies and their conclusions, and not attempt to make a personal analysis of scientific data. i hope that this report and the decision that will be taken this morning will add to the safety that young children should enjoy in their daily environment.
- (sv) mr president, for the members of the gue/ngl group, this is a good example, very clear and concrete, to which the precautionary principle must be applied. there are clear warning signs about the dangers of phthalates. tests on animals show that phthalates cause localized damage to the liver, kidneys and testicles. in such a context it would be totally unreasonable to allow these substances in the materials used to make childcare articles which children can put in their mouths, as they often do. these substances, phthalates, are a very important component of the materials from which toys are made - they can account for a fairly high percentage of their composition. it is obvious that particles escape from the objects in question, and that children can ingest them. however, it is totally absurd to think that a warning text should have the desired effect when it comes to young children. obviously, young children cannot understand it. once the toy is in the child's hands, it is almost too late. the reasonable solution in this particular case is therefore to impose a ban which is as broadly applicable as possible. the commission proposal is therefore completely inadequate. as several speakers have already said, a ban is already in force in several member states. if the legislation we adopt on this is not strict enough, then member states that have come forward may be forced to adopt a regulation that represents a regression from the one they apply today - commission. - mr president, mrs cederschiöld, ladies and gentlemen, since december 1999 the commission has been holding informal consultations with representatives of the public authorities and the data protection authorities of the member states as well as with representatives of industry, mainly internet service providers and telecommunications operators. the aim of these consultations is to develop the communication on the possible options for the european union to continue its fight against computer crime, which it announced in its annual work programme. the commission considers that a balanced response to the complex issue of the retention of traffic data must take full account of the needs and interests of the various persons involved and at the same time ensure the fundamental respect of human rights in terms of the protection of privacy and personal data. several very different but equally important aspects have to be taken into account. on the one hand, data protection authorities have found that the most effective way to reduce the unacceptable risks to privacy is not to keep traffic data for law enforcement purposes alone, while at the same time recognising the need to maintain law and order. on the other hand, the same authorities consider that it is necessary to keep a minimum traffic data stream for a certain period of time in order to facilitate criminal investigations. in this respect, care will be taken to ensure adequate security of any retained traffic data. the industry must be supported in the fight against crimes such as hacking or computer fraud, but not be required to take measures at unaffordable costs. in addition, industry will play an important role, on a voluntary basis, in creating a safer information society. users must have confidence in the security of the information society and feel protected against crime and infringements of their privacy. the commission's e-europe action plan, which was adopted at the european summit in santa maria da feira, includes a coordinated and coherent approach to cybercrime by the end of 2002. the aim is to improve coordination in the fight against these abuses. in the context of the e-europe initiative, as well as in the multiannual community action plan to promote safe use of the internet, the commission intends to support a series of actions to stimulate the use of the internet and to promote security and gain user confidence.

the committee on the environment, public health and consumer policy has adopted a number of amendments to the proposal. - (sv) mr president, as rapporteur for the opinion, responsible for presenting the document drawn up by the committee on the environment, public health and consumer policy, in accordance with the hughes procedure, i can say on behalf of the whole committee that we welcome the danish initiative. we adopted it unanimously, with our amendments. we believe that this initiative will be a tool that can be used in the fight against environmental crime and the usual cross-border effects of this type of crime, which is becoming increasingly frequent and which of course directly threatens the environment, and therefore people, fauna and flora. the most serious environmental offences are a problem common to all member states, since they do not stop at borders. we must therefore be able to take joint measures to protect the environment as much as possible through criminal provisions. i would like to draw attention to the fact that this framework decision will have to be taken unanimously in the council, and that it has been agreed that it will be binding on the member states. at the same time, it should be remembered that it is for the member states to decide how the result should be obtained, i.e. what forms and processes to use. how is this gonna work? well, in particular, through actual studies, which will have to be carried out quickly. the information thus collected should be disseminated and it should be ensured that these studies are carried out by environmentally competent staff. this is a very important point. administrative cooperation between member states should also make it possible to obtain all information useful in combating environmental offences. however, i would also like to mention a point which was also stressed in the committee on the environment, namely the need to focus also on preventive measures, in other words to intervene before offences are committed and ecological damage occurs. it is therefore important that legislation addresses the issue of environmental liability, that it defines the distribution of this liability, and that legislation is equally effective in this respect in the various member states. naturally, there is a need to increase knowledge in general, both in the field of the environment itself and in information on know-how, on how to detect environmental offences, on their consequences and the penalties to which they are subject. it is important to try to cooperate as much as possible in this area. in this draft framework decision, serious environmental offences are defined as acts or omissions which, by non-compliance with the law and in the presence of aggravating circumstances, cause significant damage to the environment. there is of course some debate about how to interpret the terms 'serious environmental offences' and 'significant damage', but this definition seems to reflect the current state of thinking, even if it is not found in national legislation. in the amendments tabled by the committee on the environment, we have dealt with this question of the definition of serious environmental crime. since this definition is very vague, we consider it important that the proposed framework decision should have as wide a scope as possible. in addition, the fight against environmental crime must not be confined to private enterprises but must also apply to the administrative sector, that is to say, to cases where the administration of our various countries, in fact our governments, have been guilty of serious environmental crime, through negligence or irresponsibility. that is what we wanted to argue in amendment no 16. another important aspect is that of the so-called 'aggravating circumstances', which arise if the activity which caused significant environmental damage was subject to very inadequate safety measures, depending on the risks posed by the activity. this is a question of responsibility, the principle that the polluter pays and that the financial burden, the effects and the risks are not attributed to another actor. these points are contained in amendment no 25. in conclusion, i would simply say that we are on the way to achieving common action to protect the environment. this framework decision is a step forward, as is the council of europe convention on the protection of the environment through criminal law. the framework decision even states that this convention, which is much more detailed, should be ratified as soon as possible. these steps are, in my view, absolutely necessary to prevent criminal or irresponsible actions which cause environmental damage. i am in favour of the draft framework decision and the amendments which have been proposed.

subjecting this type of crime to effective criminal sanctions. in my country, sweden, it is now quite common for complaints to be lodged about environmental crimes. but the proportion of these complaints that result in verdicts is remarkably low. when we consider the follow-up to sentences, it should be noted that in many cases they are quite weak. i don't think these facts are unique to as binding as other laws, must be tackled. if that is the case, then of course it is totally unacceptable. considerable economic interests are frequently at stake. at present, environmental crime is
