or at least most people - although it is an environmental crime that goes back more than twenty years. the company had not wanted to store its toxic waste, so it had buried it, endangering the lives of an entire village. unfortunately, the teckomatorp case is not the only one of its kind. many members of this house have testified to similar events since then, not to mention the situation of our seas - which could be described as western-style. it is clear that we must take environmental crime as seriously as we take other criminal acts. that is why we need common definitions on this subject. i think, like the chair of the committee on the environment, mrs schörling, that to prevent environmental crime we also need to have effective environmental liability legislation. preventive measures are the best way to ensure that our environment does not suffer further criminal acts. in order to be able to investigate suspected cases effectively and efficiently, the police and prosecutors must also have special knowledge, otherwise these crimes cannot be seriously investigated. if such crimes are not detected quickly, they may be left unpunished or even unaddressed. in the few seconds i have left, i would like to address the commission directly and reiterate the request made by, among others, mrs thors and mrs mckenna, concerning the transport of animals which takes place on a daily basis in the territory of the union, and which is perhaps the most obvious environmental crime we know today. i would like the commission to take this matter seriously and to take action to combat the current situation. i am convinced that we have the support of all the citizens of the union when it comes to doing something about these abominable practices.
mr president, i would like to thank you. for 150 years, missionaries and aid workers have created an international knowledge and commitment to broad communities in my own country. it is important to preserve and develop the capacity of each individual member state to assert, through its government and its embassies, the voice and involvement of its own people in international affairs. the embassies are one of the most central and visible expressions of national sovereignty. history shows how a country like norway fought for its own diplomacy in its struggle for independence against sweden. the embassy of one's own country also provides an important service to citizens when they visit another country. at the embassy, they can receive services in their own language. at the same time, embassies are indispensable sources of information and analysis for the governments of the various countries. although galeote quecedo has done a very fine job, i am skeptical about the following points: point e - the establishment of a common professional and permanent diplomatic service. point p - where it is said that in the future there will be eu embassies. what exactly does this mean, galeote quecedo and commissioner patten? point 1 - the establishment of a european school of diplomacy. in what language will it operate, in spanish, english, french or in all the languages of the member states? point 12 - where i think that the embassies of the member states are being told to express their views in a coordinated manner. do they not understand this themselves, already today, in the context of the common foreign and security policy? finally, it is necessary to ensure coordinated representation in the eu and other international bodies. sweden and many other countries have a proud tradition at the united nations. let's keep it that way.
madam president, i would like to thank you. i was in jerusalem as a member of the parliament's delegation for relations with israel. we met sensible representatives of the israeli government, but also among the knesset members a frightening number of uncompromising fanatics who claim that israel has all rights and the palestinians none at all. it was therefore good that the council representatives stated that the eu supports all current un resolutions. what do these decisions mean? in writing. - (sv) we abstained because the report lacks clarity as to its objectives. on the one hand, it contains a number of proposals to improve the work of the eu's existing offices abroad. it also states (in point o) that the aim is not to create a common diplomatic service with the eu, which would replace the external services of the member states. but on the other hand, the whole text is imbued with the desire to achieve this very thing gradually, that is to say, the creation of european embassies, a step in the process of transforming the eu into a superpower. this impression is clearly reinforced by paragraphs p, 13 and 15, which specifically refer to european embassies, the harmonisation of eu representation, particularly at the united nations, and the fact that member states who so wish may merge their embassies with this european representation. we are in favour of practical improvements in the eu's external action as it exists today - but we are absolutely opposed to the eu starting to position itself as a superpower vis-à-vis the rest of the world, and especially to any rash developments in this direction - and we can only abstain.

mr president, i would like to thank you. first of all, i would like to thank both the french presidency and commissioner patten for their excellent speeches this morning. the priorities set out deserve all our support. time permits only a few comments from me. i choose to concentrate on the shortcomings in the implementation of the union's development cooperation. commissioner patten should be commended for the openness with which he describes and has described the problems. my own experience, as a senior official in the undp, of seeking cooperation with the union at national level to coordinate and pool efforts in various areas, has been that it usually did not work, either because almost every decision in the eu's case had to be taken in brussels or because of a lack of competent staff in the area concerned. this made cooperation with the eu difficult and extremely time-consuming. the un has its problems of bureaucracy and slowness - i am the first to admit this - but in terms of development work and development cooperation, at least a few years ago, the eu was far behind. the conclusion i draw is, firstly, that the commission must carry out a very far-reaching decentralisation of decisionmaking at national level. 'brussels' at all its administrative levels must relinquish a large part of its control. secondly, it is important to ensure that the commission has sufficient competent staff to carry out the activities decided upon. i know that you, commissioner patten, have this policy in mind at the moment. i just want to emphasize that it's urgent. if there are no rapid improvements, i fear that the commission's support for development cooperation will gradually disappear. it would be a pity, given that there are many advantages to well-developed multilateral cooperation and that the union needs such an instrument to play a proactive role on the international stage.
madam president, i would like to thank you. in this debate, everyone has stressed the importance of prevention, preventive measures. there is an old saying in english: prevention is better than cure. however, with some 15 years of experience of this epidemic - i myself have worked actively both in the context of the un and in the context of the red cross - i can say that we have not listened very well to that proverb. it was very clear 10 years ago how serious this epidemic could be in some developing countries, but too little was done by both these countries and the international community. one can discuss the reasons why this epidemic has struck poor countries particularly hard, compared with conditions in our part of the world. it has to do, of course, with poverty, it has to do with lack of information and education, it has to do with sexual habits, it has to do specifically with lack of education of young girls, so that they can better resist sexual approaches, and it has to do, of course, with lack of money in health care. i welcome the fact that both commissioner nielson and the french presidency are now considering and designating the fight against the hiv epidemic as a top priority. however, money is not enough, we must also ensure that the commission has sufficient competent staff so that individual staff do not try to push these issues. it is also important to develop new forms of cooperation with the pharmaceutical industry, so that we can produce not only vaccines but also medicines. it's not just hiv. it's malaria. we need to be led by innovative thinking here. finally, when we talk about partnerships, it is very important, as commissioner nielson said, to look for what has worked and to support it. there are many programmes that are crying out for money and where the eu's contribution could be useful. we talk a lot about africa. i fear that in five to ten years' time parts of asia will be in a similar, very serious situation. i welcome commissioner nielson's conference on 28 september 2000 and hope that it will lead to some kind of crash programme to tackle these problems even harder in the future.

(sv) herr president, we have spoken extensively about human rights in the eu, but in practice it is often the case that citizens' rights are synonymous with money. working people who can support themselves are protected by the law, but there are large groups who have no work and are therefore without rights. for example, students who must show that they have a place of study or pensioners who must show that they have a pension of their own resources are included in the pension. they are third-country nationals and certain third-country family members who cannot join them when the breadwinner of the family is working in another country. this means that the economically weak have still been persecuted. the report makes proposals to improve considerably the situation of this group, which in reality is larger than we think. they should have more opportunities to relocate and the right to establish themselves in another country should be extended. in this context, i would like to point out again that the right to freedom of movement in the labour market is not the same as the right of residence. there are currently many workers who work temporarily in another member state without having a right of residence. those who have no fixed income or other source of income have no right of residence either. the free movement of workers is protected by the legislation of the internal market. for most people, however, it is more important that their right of residence, including the right to be able to stay in a place, is protected. you may not be forced to move, even if you cannot support yourself. this situation must be radically changed. it is important to tackle the problem now, when the economies of the member states are getting better than before.
the rapporteur. - (sv) mr president, i am very pleased with the confidence you have placed in me as rapporteur for the second reading of the sme directive, which in my view is an important step towards a new policy in the field of environmental policy. i would like to thank all my colleagues in the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection, the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection and in particular the portuguese presidency for the excellent cooperation. the proposal for a directive concerns strategic environmental impact assessment, or sme, which in short is a systematic process of assessing, analysing and evaluating the impact and potential effects of a programme on the environment. this is one of the earliest possible stages of planning to take place, including the possibility of having to find an alternative solution before proceeding with the decisionmaking process. an environmental report must be written and made accessible to the environmental authorities, the public and the environmental organisations, who must have sufficient time to react to the report. this is a very important directive for the whole of the union's environmental policy. this instrument will facilitate the fulfilment of the obligations of the treaty and the sustainable development agreements. the precautionary principle can also be taken into account. the aim of a modern environmental policy is to avoid environmental damage as much as possible and to minimise its effects. nowadays, this often happens, but the damage already done is probably the first thing that is tried to be repaired. therefore, we must learn in planning how, with the help of policies, plans and programmes, to prevent and reduce harmful effects and impacts on the environment. the sme directive was a very important step towards such an approach. however, the policy measures for this directive were not adopted, although this was the case. the commission's original report of 1991 was a report on the environmental impact of the policy measures adopted by the commission in the field of smes. it has not been possible to get the council through. the environment committee is however of the opinion that a policy should also be drawn up during the five-year review. this issue is dealt with in amendments nos 2 and 21. environmental impact assessment at project level comes late in the decision-making process and was also a decisive argument for the commission when it finally submitted its proposal in 1996. this is in fact one of the main points of the directive: but the decisionmaking process had to be intervened in. during the first reading, the european parliament had a very mixed view of the matter. but the bad is a step in the right direction, but opel areas not to be missed. parliament tabled 29 amendments, 15 of which are included in the common position. for a truly viable sme, the scope of the directives must be wide enough and the environmental reports and evaluations must be of a very high quality. the council has amended the text of the common position in a number of areas, including the scope of the directives and the definition of what they should contain. the commission was also extremely critical of the common position, which was much more restrictive than the original text. the committee on the environment has tried to restore the balance of power, inter alia by means of amendments nos 6 and 7, which widen the scope of the directive. we do not limit ourselves merely to the planning of programmes linked to the eia project. no exceptions may be made to the whole category of plans and programmes. we can also accept that the exception has been made for the financial plan and the entire defence sector as regards the sme assessment. this is dealt with in amendment no 10, which also states that the structural funds should also be allocated to smes in the next round. the other amendments to improve the directive concern more transparency, monitoring and cooperation with countries outside the union. personally, i am very surprised at amendment no 26 by mr nassauer and amendment no 29 by other members who want to reject the common position. in the environment committee's deliberations, this possibility was not taken into consideration at all. of course they have the right to vote, but do they really think that the eu's environmental policy has little to no obligation? should the european parliament ignore the text of the treaty, the cardiff treaty, the fifth environmental programme and so on? i can honestly say that i don't take your proposal seriously. amendments nos 11 and 31 also contained the same concerns. in particular, amendment no 11 states that member states must define the level at which smes are to be implemented as part of a programme's plan and as part of a hierarchy. this is where the whole idea of an sme really comes in. i therefore hope that these amendments have not been adopted.
(sv) mr president, every day we are reminded that the environment has changed. last sunday i heard a swedish radio programme that central sweden is to account for 50% more rainfall in the coming years. in one way or another, environmental issues are increasingly attracting the attention of public opinion. they must therefore be better integrated into the political process. i would also like to congratulate mrs schörling on her excellent report, in which she makes it clear that environmental aspects must be taken into account in good time. environmental issues should in principle be reflected in all activities, especially economic activities. as regards the plan, the building plan and so on, it is of course important, as also in your report is presented, that at the earliest possible stage account be taken of the environmental aspects, so that errors and higher costs can be avoided as quickly as possible. i think it is very important that, as mrs schörling says, the scope of the directive is being extended. economic activities must be subject to environmental self-assessment. as the eu spends a large part of its budget on agriculture, regional policy and the structural funds, it is obvious that these areas have been studied for their environmental impact. this may not be immediately feasible, but we must ensure that the new plan is submitted to it as soon as possible. it is illogical to support eu funds for activities in these sectors, as is currently the case, but then in another context the environmental consequences must be combated. we must try to create a system where we can take environmental measures into account, also when it comes to economic issues. this is of great importance. i am aware that a large part of the house does not agree with me, but in my group i fully support mrs schörling's case.
(sv) mr president, after years of struggle we are now taking a decision on the port recovery system which should make it possible to dispose of the oil residues in the sea instead of dumping them at sea. this is obviously an extremely important step in the right direction. at the same time, it is worrying that people who were up to the last minute against this normal measure have worked. this is illustrated by the fact that a third reading was needed and a dispute committee had to be convened, but also, and above all, by the largely indifferent attitude of the council. this also shows that what we are today achieving is a compromise. in the original proposal, virtually all costs were covered by port money, in accordance with a system that had long existed in the baltic sea. this meant that the ships had a financial incentive to release the oil residues into the ports and not into the sea. only a part of the costs is covered in this way and a very large part of the costs is borne by the shipowner. it is not clear to my mind how this will affect the sorting. we must also be absolutely prepared to take any additional decision that may prove necessary. it is therefore a good thing that the proposal from the dispute committee mentions the evaluation and control of the vessels.
in writing. - (sv) the report deals mainly with two different groups of people, third country nationals and refugees, and their right to family reunification in the countries of the european union. the swedish christian democrats consider that the issues concerning these two groups should be resolved separately. the right of refugees to family reunification must be treated in the same way, in a spirit of equality, but in a separate directive. the family is the most important institution in our society, and we must therefore jealously guard the right to family life. we must above all defend the right of children to join their parents, but in certain cases (for example, for serious health reasons), the right to family reunification should also apply to close relatives and adult children. it would also be reasonable to allow family members, particularly in the event of divorce or death, to obtain an autonomous residence permit in the host country, as well as the right to work or continue their education or training there. we support the report, subject to these few reservations and clarifications.

(sv) mr president, there are various kinds of blackmail. sometimes it's hard to draw the line between black and white. sometimes the blame for tax evasion lies with the individual who is blackmailed. organized black-work can be a serious form of abuse. the number of employees involved is usually high and the cause of this is found in the employers who organise the black-work, maintaining a share of the wage costs and the fiscal burden for themselves. that's what it's all about. in many cases, the organised blackwork is well known, both to the local population and to the authorities. if nothing against black work is called for, it may be because there is a lot of unemployment and poor people are not otherwise able to be exploited by black employers. there is even a fiscal argument which, according to glass, plays an important role. that is all during the discussions on the subject. jöns has rightly observed that in regions of a country where the tax burden is very particularly high, where the labour market is organized in a similar way and where there are strong trade unions, blackwork is almost non-existent, at least not as organized blackwork. when a small fraction of the taxes are intended to make the black market less attractive, it means that the economic calculations of the black market are intended to make the legalization of the latter a general rule. this is approximately the same as the raising of the speed limit to reduce the number of speed-devices, or the legalisation of drug trafficking to reduce the illegal sale of drugs and gangs. i was very shocked when i read the original opinion of the rapporteur, in which she suggested that all employees in european workplaces should be required to carry electronic passports so that they could be continuously monitored by public authorities. i think that employers are the ones who organize production and employ the workers. if everyone is to be required to carry an electronic passport, then employers are responsible for the working conditions. i agree with my group that the fight against unemployment and poverty is the best and actually the only way to achieve this, at least when it comes to organized black work. higher wages are a much better means of combating black-collar work and tax evasion or other abuses by irresponsible employers.

mr president, i would like to thank you. lifelong learning is something we all look forward to. we're hearing that in this discussion. however, when it comes to action, the opposite is often true. i agree with the rapporteur who criticises the commission's efforts in lifelong learning during the 1996 campaign year and the sloppy evaluation that followed. lifelong learning is a phenomenon that requires considerable resources. this requires a new approach to education policy and the prioritisation of financial resources. let me start by commenting on the new thinking. in our education systems, we are experts in the opposite, that is, in time-limited and completed courses of study with a strong emphasis on the final degree. lifelong learning requires the opposite, namely continuity and a long time. it is no longer enough to train for the just-in-time needs of the workplace. now it's time to qualify people for a lifetime course. we need new forms of organization in education. we need new pedagogical ideas and instruments. perhaps the biggest problem is the one that many other speakers have mentioned, namely the availability of money. we need to devote far more resources to education than we have done so far, both in the workplace and in the public education system and elsewhere. there must be money to pay for the cost of education, but also to replace wage income, especially for those who need to study full-time. it is particularly important to be able to finance the relatively long periods of study which may be required for people to move from traditional sectors to new areas of the labour market. as the availability of money is always a problem, i believe that quantitative targets for expenditure on adult education are needed. it might be possible to set aside a certain proportion of the production costs of firms, a certain percentage of the state budget, a certain proportion of normal working time or possibly a certain proportion of individual working time - or to use similar quantitative measures. the swedish presidency, which will begin on 1 january 2001, has said that lifelong learning will be one of its priorities. i think we could ask the swedish government to propose precisely such quantitative targets for lifelong adult education. several speakers in this house have agreed on the need for economic priorities. perhaps we could make a joint statement to the swedish presidency on this matter.
mr president, i would like to thank you. this report deals with issues of major importance for personal privacy and data security. the eu is currently building up extensive personal registers containing highly sensitive personal data. at the same time, it is clear that the supervision and security of these registers are too weak. in the gue/ngl group we are of course in favour of improving data security and supervision. we therefore support the idea of establishing a single independent supervisory authority with adequate powers and resources. we therefore welcome the basic ideas of this report, but we would like to stress the importance of building this supervisory authority on the expertise of the member states in this area. a new supervisory authority must be able to cover all relevant registers, including the eurodac register, which plays a key role in eu asylum policy. refugees are a particularly vulnerable group and data from the register can be a direct basis for expulsion with significant consequences for an asylum seeker. it is therefore particularly important to be able to ensure that the register contains correct information, that asylum seekers are informed of their rights and that they are given the opportunity to appeal. in addition, the sirene structure of the schengen agreement must be covered by a new supervisory authority. in the rather unknown sirene structure, virtually any personal data can be sent at short notice between member states. the commission has also been asked to provide a report on the implementation of the directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens at work. the whole sirene structure suffers from a marked lack of transparency and parliamentary control. we already have important experience in this area in the management of the large schengen register of persons, the sis. unfortunately, those experiences are very discouraging. the jsa, the joint supervisory authority, has had a total lack of resources. in addition, the jsa has been denied access to important information in the sis. this is clear from the first annual reports of the jsa. despite the difficult working conditions, the jsa identifies serious deficiencies in sis, such as incorrect data in the registers, member states illegally copying data and lack of procedures for accessing records. this is completely unacceptable. a functioning data security requires more than a functioning oversight. more fundamental changes are needed in the rules governing the registers. one requirement is to separate criminal records from refugee and national security records. these different areas will not have common structures and registers. transparency and parliamentary control must be strengthened, not least with regard to sirene and schengen. in addition, clear limits must be set on what may be recorded in europol's intelligence registers, based on article 10 (1) of the europol convention. today the powers of the register go far beyond what is necessary. persons who are not suspected of a crime may be registered, and sensitive personal data on political opinion, religious affiliation and sexual orientation may be registered. it doesn't make sense! privacy and the rule of law must be put at the centre.

i would like to express my doubts as to whether this question is appropriate for the european parliament to deal with, in accordance with the principle of subsidiarity or the principle of subsidiarity. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used effectively. before being used, a portable ladder must be prevented from slipping from the ground either by anchoring its lower or upper part, by a device to prevent slipping or by any other equally effective means. first of all, all possible steps must be taken to consolidate the steps by defining them. stairs must be of sufficient height to project sufficiently over the workplace/floor to be reached by the stairs. sliding ladders shall be used in such a way that they are safe. the use of another person to hold the ladder as a safety measure shall not be permitted. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. this is a very generous and well-meaning text. i can assure you that in mora in the dalarna, where i live and work, and in the swedish parliament and also in all the other national parliaments, these things are taken care of. is it right for the european parliament, in accordance with the subsidiarity principle of the maastricht and amsterdam treaties, to legislate at this very high level of detail? the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the regulation we are now discussing concerns the conditions for ensuring that the union's aid policy takes better account of the physical environment and the natural resource base in connection with various development projects. this is an important question. we know that economic growth can often have negative consequences for the natural environment, either in the exploitation of natural resources, or in industrial production itself, or in the use of products. environmental damage has negative effects on health. they risk eroding biodiversity, but can also cause damage that threatens the long-term productive capacity of natural systems. when, and if, that happens, it also affects the health of our economies. by thinking proactively and choosing methods and technologies that are as environmentally friendly as possible, growth can be combined with good environmental care. this is important in our own countries, but we have a long way to go through the european union. it is also important in developing countries, of course, not least in the context of development cooperation based on the european union. the integration of environmental considerations requires, among other things, extensive methodological work, demonstration projects, but also training and education of both commission staff and relevant staff in the beneficiary countries. the regulation we are discussing regulates this in detail and provides a considerable amount of money. mr president, i would like to thank you. this matter has been dealt with twice by parliament. at the last reading there were significant differences between our views and the proposal adopted by the council. a settlement was reached, the outcome of which we are witnessing today. i think that from the point of view of this parliament we should welcome the outcome of this conciliation, which has achieved what we were aiming for in important respects. let me mention three important areas: firstly, the budget is almost double the original amount. we went from 50 million euros to 93 million euros. secondly, the degree of involvement of the member states in the activities themselves was a key issue. the starting point for parliament was to choose the least possible interference, that is to say to give the commission the main responsibility for the actual activities and then to evaluate them afterwards. the compromise we have reached means that only activities above eur 2.5 million will be subject to co-decision, that is to say, the active participation of the member states is required. this is good, in my opinion. if our aim is, as it should be, to simplify and de-bureaucratize aid and thus reduce the gap between the promises and commitments on the one hand and the activities carried out on the other, then a reduced involvement of the member states in operational activities is an absolute prerequisite. with the settlement we have reached, we have taken a good step in this direction. thirdly, at second reading we demanded that the regulation not be limited in time, that is to say that this type of activity should be planned in the very long term. however, since the activities will now be evaluated after four years and decisions will then be taken on the future, i think we should be satisfied. after the evaluation, we have every opportunity to lay down guidelines, and even more detailed ones than today, for this important activity. my recommendation, mr president, is that parliament should accept the result of the conciliation.
in writing. - (sv) we abstained on the skinner report on the minimum safety and health requirements for the use of work equipment by workers. this is an area where the eu should not have such a large power. our basic positive position towards the european union is positive. as swedish liberals, we see european integration as a means of providing solutions to problems that transcend borders, such as the environment, trade, crossborder mobility, human rights and conflict management. europe's democracies thus have a chance to show the world that cooperation leads to freedom and greater well-being. we also believe in the principle of subsidiarity, which means that decisions should be taken as close as possible to the people concerned. that is why we are actively advocating a constitution for the european union, which would imply a clearer distribution of responsibilities for everyone. it must be clear to all citizens that the eu should only deal with matters that concern it - those that go beyond its borders. all other issues must be dealt with at local, regional or national level. we are convinced that the eu is not the most appropriate level to legislate on how, for example, ladders should be used in work at height. national specific conditions must be able to be authoritative as to how scaffolding is erected and work is carried out. we believe it is essential that the eu should focus instead on the few areas where it can really be useful. the one in question cannot be one of them.
in writing. - (sv) in a common market and in collaboration at european level based on common values which put the well-being of people first, it is reasonable and legitimate to require minimum levels of safety and health for workers. the christian democrats therefore support the principles underlying the skinner report, but cannot accept the settlement of the details at eu level which is being achieved. the directive should be aimed primarily at highlighting the objective, not at regulating the details or describing the way to achieve it. it is right to require employers at european level to take the necessary measures to ensure the safety of workers. it is absurd to settle in detail at eu level how to achieve this security. it is high time that the principle of subsidiarity was taken seriously and that detailed rules were adopted at the lowest competent level. let the member states decide for themselves how regulations should be drawn up in order to best meet the main requirements of the directive. in some countries this may be done through national laws, while in others it may be expected that agreements will be concluded between the labour market partners. in the light of the above, the swedish christian democrats have chosen to vote against the report.

the rapporteur. - mr. speaker, i have to go. today, as i flew from stockholm over germany and then drove on down to the historic alsace, i could not help but feel grateful for my europe. i was born in a time and in a country where there is no immediate reason to think about disaster and war. before i was born, freedom and democracy had triumphed over oppression and the madness of war, and a european union was born. today's young people take peace for granted, and mistrust has been replaced by mutuality. there have been tensions, but on the whole peace and trade have established a closer union between citizens, now further consolidated by monetary union. today, as we celebrate the unification of germany and discuss parliament's position on the enlargement process, it is important to have a historical perspective in order to look far ahead and to understand that today's generation of politicians may also have at least as important decisions to make for freedom, peace and democracy. few believed it was possible to reunite germany. everyone was surprised by the dynamic of the process so skillfully handled by chancellor kohl. without his anchoring of peace, europe would look very different today. what began as the courage and desire for freedom of individuals led to the tearing down of the wall of hatred, stone by stone. so now, ten years later, we have a responsibility to history to pursue together the reunification of europe and to strengthen the process of integration. it will change the whole of europe. however, as i see it, there are no other options and nothing else is more desirable. if the european union of today cannot cope with further enlargement, it is not living up to its own ideals. if so, then in my view it has failed its mission the liberation process. our support and commitment continues, much for our own sake. we need stronger cooperation on our common sea, the baltic sea. we have much to gain from more trade and more exchange. the baltic states are small coastal states bordering a powerful neighbour. they have a rich source of culture, diversity and opportunities, without which a united europe cannot exist. each of the countries is judged on its own merits, not in lumps and not on the basis of a particular geopolitical situation. it must be the results of the negotiations that count. the timetables must not be a reason to postpone enlargement. i am, as the report shows, enthusiastic about what a country can achieve in ten years of reform. i am impressed by the political consensus that exists and by the leadership that prime minister mart laar is exercising. the eu has much to learn from estonia's free trade choices, its path towards the new economy and its efforts to integrate the russian-speaking population. it is clear that the foundations of the eu must not be watered down and that the applicant countries must comply with community legislation. however, many eu politicians seem more concerned with further building the union and raising standards. the desire for detailed regulation does not seem to be diminishing. this creates new obstacles for the applicant countries. as i see it, the enlargement process must not be unduly delayed. it is now that we can secure common values and benefits. as a moderate, i'm extremely frugal with the taxpayers' money. by increasing of mismanagement by a planned economy and the inherent irresponsibility of communism are not estonia's fault and should not be a reason to delay membership. estonia has a good chance of becoming a new report go beyond the current eu level and can be seen as new thresholds. i would like to pay tribute to the rapporteur, mr brok, who has tried, but has not succeeded, to concentrate the report. the task of parliament now, across all frontiers, is to reduce, not increase, the obstacles to successful enlargement.
- mr. speaker, i have to go. commissioner, what is it? many people imagine that enlargement of the european union is a project that will cost a lot of money. this is not a completely accurate description of what reality looks like. this was already noted at the berlin summit in march 1999, when the budgetary guidelines were drawn up. it was then clear that we could actually do this within the ceiling of the eu budget. the opinion on the brok report, for which i was responsible and which the committee on budgets supported, reaches the same conclusion. the vast majority of international studies point to macroeconomic gains associated with enlargement. this applies to the candidate countries, but it also applies to all the current member states. to this must also be added all the other benefits which cannot be measured in numbers or assessed in economic terms. through enlargement, we will participate in the largest single market in the world, with almost 500 million people and 25% of world trade. we will also have more competition and a faster reform process in western europe. better conditions for economic growth will be created throughout europe. we shall have, albeit in a somewhat longer perspective, a single currency for the whole continent. we will be able to solve europe's transboundary environmental problems together. we will have an expanded and stable democracy and thus the possibility of lasting peace on a continent constantly ravaged by war. the overall picture of enlargement, therefore, shows not insurmountable costs but gains, regardless of how the calculations are made. they are of such a magnitude that enlargement should be implemented as soon as possible.

the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. today we are going to discuss and perhaps vote on a total of three reports dealing with this subject, two of which are mine. they are the first steps on the long, long road towards creating food security for all 370 million people in europe. we can well hope that before this whole package is in place, we will be many more citizens affected by these rules. this is the first step, and it is now also a matter of establishing extremely important principles for the whole process. the first of my reports concerns extraneous substances in animal feed. the main changes proposed by the commission, which i support, are that the directive should cover absolutely all feedingstuffs, including products for feeding small animals, i.e. cats, dogs, guinea pigs, etc. however, the rules are primarily intended to apply to feed for the animals we eat ourselves. after what has happened on the european market, it should be crystal clear to all of us today that we humans are allowed to eat what we feed our animals. the second important principle is the so-called dilution ban. this means that if a batch of feed material has too high limit values, it cannot be mixed into undamaged batches to avoid the limit values. the commission proposal deletes the rules which give local, national authorities the right to local derogations. this is supported in my report. we know that the foreign substances we are talking about are very rarely acutely toxic substances, but are long-lived, long-lived and accumulative substances, such as dioxins, aflatoxins and heavy metals such as cadmium, lead and mercury. we know that levels of these substances are constantly increasing in our life cycle. the levels increase steadily and slowly in the soil, in the feed, in the food. it is therefore important that we start the preventive process of taking some of these products off the chain. the levels must not increase continuously in the body throughout life. for example, we know that cadmium blocks kidneys in the long run, both in animals and in humans. we know that dioxin and aflatoxins are carcinogenic, etc. i fully agree with all the proposals put forward by the commission in this respect. in addition, and this is important because it will be on the ballot, the commission wants to introduce something called the action limit. all these subjects have a natural background value, so to speak, an eternal noise. we'll never get out of this completely. that's why we set a limit that must not be exceeded. the action limit is a vigilance limit, an opportunity to initiate an investigation when a substance is found in a product or in an area to be above the background value but still below the prohibited value, that is, the limit at which we must intervene. this is a central and important point in any prevention work. in the long term, it is very important that we can calmly start an investigation and try to stop a particular substance before it reaches this acute limit value. in fact, everyone in the food chain would benefit. if we leave it until the limit is reached, the measures will be very drastic. then there will be catastrophic interventions of the type of 'dioxin in belgium'. it would be important for us to implement the precautionary principle in such a way that we can get through this. i have tried in both of these revised proposals to use language that is readable. a legal text must be so precise that the law can actually be followed. but i also believe that it is the duty of parliament to try to produce laws, rules and regulations in the european union which are understandable to those of our citizens who must follow them and need to read them. so i've been making a lot of the language more straightforward, trying to simplify, trying to make it crystal clear. as regards the limit values, which must be set very strictly in the future to prevent a bad development, i ask you to take account of the problems that low limit values, for example for dioxin, will cause for fishermen in the baltic sea, parts of the north sea, probably in areas along the mediterranean coast. i would also ask you to take into account farmers who have their fields next to busy roads or next to industries that have contaminated large areas. many of these poisonings or heavy metal releases occurred before there was sufficient environmental awareness and before the effects were known. we can't find the culprits. i ask that we take these individual traders into account in future, because they are without fault and are suffering from economic disaster. as regards the directive on official controls in the field of animal feed, it is important that we really have controls that are carried out and that are the same throughout the union. if the controls are not observed and we have the means to control the controllers, then the entire security of our citizens will be undermined. however, the citizen cannot really trust that a check is actually being carried out if there are large differences between member states and regions. the second thing we must bear in mind is that differences in the application of these control rules will cause serious disruption to the internal market. safe food will cost you the whole chain. it is dangerous that certain countries, regions or industries could get away with competing on price with poor security. this is an extremely unwelcome situation.
mr president, i would like to thank you. we can only obtain healthy food by taking the whole chain into account, i.e. how we grow our crops, in what soil and with what seeds, and under what conditions we raise our animals, what feed and what water we give them. toxic waste, pollution, poor animal husbandry, poor hygiene will sooner or later result in sick animals, and consequently also in increased disease in humans. unfortunately, much of the inherited knowledge, common sense, has been lost in industrial farming, where animals have been reduced to units of production and commodities. i am convinced that with organic farming we would not have had the major problems we have today. the situation now looks like it does. the two proposals we are discussing today on contaminants and products in animal feed and on official controls are therefore necessary and welcome. these also represent a step towards a whole package of measures in favour of food safety. i would like to congratulate rapporteur paulsen on the consistent way in which she has worked to improve the commission's text and the insightful knowledge she has shown. i am glad that the ppe-de group has withdrawn the amendments on the dilution problem. this saves me a lot of talking time. on the other hand, it would be unfortunate if, for example, amendments nos 39 and 40 were adopted, as this would mean that the possibilities for member states to intervene quickly against newly discovered dangers would disappear. experience has shown that this is precisely what we need to do, and we need to act very quickly, because we are talking about human health. we must also keep the precautionary principle in mind at all times. i support the rapporteur's proposal but will vote against the ppe-de amendment.
in writing. (sv) we would like to make the following five comments: the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'candidate country' in the context of the accession of the candidate countries to the european union. this date should be based on negotiations with each country, which should take account of the major difficulties encountered by the candidate countries in their transition. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. 2. enlargement will cause significantly higher expenditure for the eu, which will have to be covered by savings in the agricultural, structural funds and defence sectors. the eu should also be able to provide the necessary financial support to the candidate countries in the event of their accession to the eu. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. 4. we regret that the position of women and the conditions under which the transition process is taking place have not been taken into account in the report. the fifth. contrary to the usual practice, the report opted to include several annexes as part 2. the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area, and i am sure that the commission will be able to support them. in our intervention we have referred exclusively to the main part of the report.
in writing. - (sv) the enlargement of the eu is synonymous with the reunification of a europe that has been divided for a long time. the eu member states have a historic mission to welcome the countries of central and eastern europe into european cooperation. however, enlargement should not be seen as a problem but as an opportunity to deepen cooperation on issues that transcend national borders and that cannot be solved by states in isolation. eu governments, like the european parliament, have a major responsibility to bring about the necessary reforms as quickly as possible, so that enlargement becomes a reality. preparations are slowly progressing. that is why european leaders must begin to show the political will to meet the challenge of enlargement. a clear timetable must be established for the completion of the negotiations. to stop the enlargement process now would be to go back ten years. this would be a fatal blow to european integration. one of the main reforms to be carried out is a complete overhaul of the common agricultural policy. the current system of aid, subsidies and quotas must be replaced by free trade and market adjustment. the reform of the agricultural policy is necessary not only for economic reasons but also so that european agriculture can take the path of efficiency and quality. the enlarged eu must not become a two-speed europe. for europe to be a whole, the aim must be that all member states participate in all areas of cooperation. of course, free movement will also have to apply to the countries of central and eastern europe from the first day of their accession to the union.
mr president, commissioner, i would like to thank you for your reply. what we are doing now is translating article 13 of the treaty into practical policy. we did this first with the proposal on discrimination on grounds of racial or ethnic origin and now we are continuing with the action plan on discrimination in the workplace. i should like to begin by thanking the two rapporteurs, mr mann and mr cashman, for their excellent reports. i shall be staying with a special group, namely the disabled. i have been following this issue since i was a member of the swedish parliament and we did a large study, which culminated in a proposal against discrimination in working life. we then looked at unemployment, which at that time was generally very low in sweden, and compared it to the unemployment among the disabled. at that time, we had a two percent unemployment rate overall among the population. among the most severely disabled, unemployment was 70 percent. the situation is still such that the most severely disabled have little access to the labour market. they are discriminated against today. that is why this type of legislation is needed, and often only small measures are needed. we often talk about the single market and the opportunities for young people to study and work throughout europe. but what about youths who have some disability? what are their chances of being able to operate in the internal market, to study in different countries and to develop? this is where we discriminate today. that is why it is important that we address this discrimination. this will be followed by more areas. disabled people are also discriminated against in access to cultural institutions and businesses. that is why we must follow up. one last point: in the field of organizational life, the disabled are of vital importance. since the life of organisations is important in this process, i hope that the amendment i tabled and which was adopted in the committee on civil liberties and justice, which states that more than one organisation can benefit from the grants, will also be the one finally adopted.
mr president, i would like to thank you. as regards mr titley's report in general, i agree in principle with the views expressed by mrs morgantini and others. i shall only raise a rather specific question relating to the motion for a resolution. it refers in recital r to 'the development of a common defence policy within the cfsp and the establishment of a european security and defence identity within nato' and in paragraph 1 (e) it states that the eu's arms policy must 'contribute to the development of a common defence policy'. mr president, these are words which must be totally unacceptable to those member states which are non-aligned and wish to remain so. this applies to my own country, sweden, and i assume it also applies to other non-aligned states within the eu. sweden does not consider that the activities of the cfsp can be described as defence policy, as it does not imply a common defence obligation equivalent to article 5 of nato. the cfsp covers crisis management activities outside the eu's borders and not something that can be described as a common defence policy. sweden is actively involved, almost too actively, i would say, in the development of a common arms policy, but that is something else. the fact that mr titley is writing about a common defence identity within nato does not make things any better. as you know, sweden is not a member of nato and, as far as i know, has no intention of becoming one either. all swedish members of parliament must of course vote against the two formulations mentioned and i also expect parliament as a whole to reject them. if that does not happen, the swedish government will have to protest against the fact that parliament is openly denouncing sweden's non-aligned status.

in writing. - (sv) our position on the recommendation of the european parliament and the council on free movement within the union is in principle positive, but we would like to mention the following points: we believe that it is very important from the point of view of equal opportunities and justice that third country nationals resident in the eu have the right to make use of the provisions contained in community legislation on the same basis as union citizens. we also believe that the member states should be able to decide to a large extent themselves what taxes they wish to levy. healthy competition will reduce the tax burden on economic activities, which will encourage business and thus job creation. the revenue shortfall suffered by the states as a result of this tax competition will thus be offset by the fact that income tax will be paid by a greater number of people. that is why we are opposed to amendment no 38 on the introduction of common tax rules for young volunteers.
in writing. - (sv) the christian democrats generally welcome the proposal for a directive on the establishment of general frameworks for non-discrimination in working life. however, we consider that the amendment tabled by the committee on employment and social affairs on statistics broken down by target groups, i.e. based on existing grounds of discrimination such as race, ethnic origin, age, religion or belief and sexual orientation, is far too likely to undermine the integrity of individuals. we therefore vote against this amendment, but we support the report as such.
in writing. - (sv) we welcome the initiative to draw up codes of good conduct for arms exports. many of the proposals and comments made by mr titley in the report represent improvements on the original text presented by the council. we are voting against the titley report, however, for the following reasons: this report presupposes a greater recognition of the importance attached to a strong european military capability, rather than emphasising the need to work for disarmament and peace. the report presents arms export control essentially as a method of avoiding uncomfortable situations for the european military industry, not as a way of achieving disarmament and peace. it also mentions european arms exports as a means of resolving future local conflicts in europe's neighbouring geographical areas. moreover, despite a wish expressed by the green group, the rapporteur systematically avoids mentioning turkey, which, as a result of its membership of nato, is in practice exempt from the obligation to respect these codes of good conduct. the report also has the effect of considering the european war industry as an important economic sector, a perspective which we consider irresponsible, since it is an industry whose main implicit mission is to kill or even destroy human beings. as long as the european parliament is not able to make the logical link between the production and sale of arms on the one hand and the continuing destruction of the environment and human life on a global scale, we cannot support reports of this kind.
in writing. - (sv) the fact that the european union wants to put in place a code of good conduct to restrict arms exports is a good thing. i would not, however, accept the need for a common defence policy and a european security and defence policy. sweden is neutral and has no intention of joining any military alliance. i cannot therefore support such views, whether in recital r or in points 1c, 1d and 1e.
mr president, i would like to thank you. there is a real need for a social agenda in europe after many years of neoliberal economic policies which have led to unemployment and social exclusion in all countries, including my own rich home country of sweden. in our big cities, begging has returned after having disappeared since the first postwar years. even the private charities and their soup kitchens seem to be needed again. i must therefore welcome the purpose of the commission's work and the van lancker report. many of her proposals are very good and deserve our full support. real social action costs a lot, which i think is discussed far too little in the report. these funds must come mainly from the member states. the social authorities of each country must therefore be the first to be called upon. governments must allocate more funds, more social workers must be hired and more resources must be given to voluntary, social organisations, otherwise the result will be mere talk without practical content. the van lancker report presents the commission and its legislation as the real engine of social work. i believe that the power must come from below, and that it is important to put the support at the level where the problems are. the commission can of course make an important contribution, but social problems are local and must be dealt with with great sensitivity. the commission can help to set the bar high, but it is the social workers, the trade unions and the voluntary activists that we must put our hopes in, not the officials in brussels. finally, i would just like to add that i am not a co-author of amendment no 35 as it is stated in the document with the amendments. on the contrary, i am very critical of the proposal. instead of my name, it should be figueiredo. there must have been a mistake in the secretariat and i ask that this be corrected.
the rapporteur. mr president, commissioner, minister, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. i'm very happy to see you all here. the commission's communication states that democracy is a fundamental value in the eu member states, the eea and the candidate countries, a key component of the eu's external relations and development cooperation. it also states that genuine democracy requires that all citizens, women and men, participate and be represented equally in economic life, decision-making, social life, cultural life and civil society. there are major shortcomings in all these areas. the commission therefore intends to tackle the problem by a combination of measures. in parallel with the mainstreaming of gender perspectives in all policy areas, normally known as gender mainstreaming, specific measures for women will also be applied. this is a strategy and an approach which i fully share. i believe that it can be argued that no equality work is of lasting value unless women's rights are also strengthened. this work has gone on rather quickly, although it took some time at the beginning. there are 49 amendments tabled by the committee. we started with 126, which means we're incredibly committed. i'm very happy about that. we believe it is very important that the commission should be able to guarantee maximum transparency in this programme so that all those interested can be informed of the work during the whole implementation phase. it is also very important, if not fundamental, that the various community measures, the framework strategy and the member states' actions, should complement and not contradict each other. this is also the explicit ambition of the commission and the council. the commission has now identified five areas of action which it considers to be of strategic importance. i have nothing to say against this and neither does the committee. we have come up with a new, shocking proposal, which is really a repetition of our opinion on the budget. we believe that 40% of the structural funds should be earmarked and focused on the conditions and needs of women. the commission's action should be complemented by reviews of individual access to health care, social security and pension systems, shortcomings in work organisation and perhaps also a general reduction in working hours. what we have just discussed in this house are social issues, and i would like to emphasize once again that we have repeatedly found that over 50 million of the citizens of the european union are actually classified as poor, and that a large majority of these are women. of course, this must not only be observed, but also dealt with - with force. we have extensively discussed various evaluation procedures a lot. i shall not go into it here, but i just wanted to mention it. we have been thinking that the aim must be to increase accessibility, and also to be able to guarantee the full participation of ngos. there has been discussion on how to include the candidate countries in the programme in a natural way. we have also discussed how we can do something concrete in this area, not just mentioning women in developing countries. during the final phase of the trip, i have been in close contact with people in both the commission and the council. we want the strategy to be included in council decisions. the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection has already adopted a number of amendments to the proposal. i say this so that no mysterious, technical procedures cause this to be misunderstood. there are no less than seven different amendments with the same spirit, namely that the council should also adopt the commission's strategy. it is therefore important to ensure that ngos have a say in the implementation. an amendment means that an annual debate will be held here in parliament, together with the commission. this may be a matter for the commission and parliament only, but i am very, very grateful to you both, commissioner diamantopoulou and minister péry, for being here, and i look forward to hearing your views on all the amendments.
the rapporteur, mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i think it would be a great pity if nicole péry did not also take the opportunity to speak now that she is here, so that we could hear her views too.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i will speak about the riis-jørgensen report. i would like to begin by congratulating mr riis-jørgensen on a very good report. one of the absolute cornerstones of eu cooperation is that competition between different companies, different products, different countries and different regions is well functioning, fair and correct, and that it is beneficial for consumers. let me start with a quote: "people in the same industry rarely meet for fun or entertainment without their conversation ending in a conspiracy against the public, or some contrivance to raise prices". that's what the father of economic liberalism, adam smith, said in 1776. now, certainly, some things have happened in these 200 years and more, but we have the same aspirations today. for the internal market to work, we need active institutions to monitor competition issues. in this respect, commissioner monti deserves a commendation. paragraph 16 of the riis-jørgensen report refers to increased legal certainty in merger applications and more efficient and faster timeframes for appeals before the courts. this is particularly true in view of the commission's somewhat dual role in the merger process. the commission both monitors and enforces competition rules. this dual role requires specific requirements in terms of openness, transparency and predictability. the second point i would like to mention is paragraph 18. it is general in nature but has its origins in the planned merger between the truck manufacturers volvo and scania. the commission, as is well known, opposed this merger, which in turn led to a wide-ranging debate on the functioning of competition rules and on how market shares should be calculated and assessed. a clearer definition and further discussion is needed in this area. as long as the single market is not completed, it is mainly large companies in small member states that may lose competitiveness in relation to companies in the rest of the world. how can large companies with a large domestic market come together and compete in a global market if eu rules make this impossible? i am therefore glad that the committee adopted the amendments i proposed in this context, as i come from a small country. i hope that the commission is now also prepared to broaden the discussion and to shed further light on the competition rules in this context.
mr president, i would like to thank you. there are many important issues to be resolved. the biarritz and nice meetings could, however, be described as a battle between those who want to build the 'eu state' quickly and those who see the eu as intergovernmental cooperation and a unique form of community decision-making. the real concern, however, is that there is what can be called a kind of blackmail against those who do not want to go beyond the reform mandate given to them by the member states. they are often accused in the press of delaying or even preventing the eastward expansion. it is those who are calling for major reforms before enlargement who are guilty of this. act responsibly now and concentrate on solving the tasks left behind in nice after amsterdam: let each country have a commissioner. we must make a re-weighting of the votes in the council, but not on the principle of double majorities. we must not create a permanent core group to lead the way, but respect all the member states. it is also worrying that citizens are not given the opportunity to follow developments and that there are those who oppose a genuine principle of publicity, which is absolutely fundamental to democracy. do not miss the opportunity to give legitimacy to the charter, which is achieved by involving the citizens. i would like to see the swedish presidency contribute to this.
mr president, i would like to thank you. at the biarritz summit there was a clear contrast between the smaller and the larger member states. the demands of the large countries for more power in the council and parliament, if met, would upset the whole balance within the eu and mean that the small countries had to pay the full price of enlargement. such a union would be centralistic and give small countries less influence than the two-chamber system of the united states gives small states. nor would the union become more efficient. instead, all member states must be prepared to give up proportionate influence in the enlargement process. in sweden, supporters of the union have always justified membership in the eu by the fact that we gain influence. now is the moment of truth, when we must be prepared to defend the little influence we have. i would also say that it is remarkable that the french presidency has consistently used this office to promote national interests.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the citizen who is interested in how the eu is governed will hardly read the treaty of amsterdam as a bedtime story. if she should venture to do so, it is not certain that she will become wiser. even the most committed europeanist cannot easily get through the treaties which actually regulate a great deal of our joint activities here in europe. the treaties are hard to obtain, sometimes contradictory, difficult to read and hardly inspiring. the european parliament has been a great help to the commission in this matter. we can't have it like this! if we want to increase awareness and commitment to the european project, it is a minimum requirement that it should be easy to find out how the union is run and why. that is not the case today, as duhamel's report makes clear. after the nice meeting, which we all hope will be a success, it is important to take this next step towards creating a more democratic union. it is time to review the treaties. for florence shows a way forward, but the next step is to create a genuine european constitution. european citizens need, value and want, according to surveys, a common constitution that clearly expresses the values and objectives of the eu, explains the division of powers and responsibilities between the institutions, but also between the institutions and the member states. the rights of citizens should be regulated by incorporating the conclusions of the convention. this is an exciting process. it is certainly not simple, but it is extremely important. of course, citizens must be involved in the eu. there is a need for a clearer division of powers and a clearer principle of subsidiarity. we learned a great deal from the work of the convention. it is a working method which, with certain modifications, should be used to give citizens an even more important place in the process. a future european constitution must not be a product of brussels' desk. vaclav havel said in this house, and decision to start work must be made today. it was hopeful to hear that the french presidency was positive about this. i shall now do my utmost to persuade my countrymen to continue this relay under mr president, i would like to thank you. we are going to decide on the eu budget at first reading. however, this means that we are only at the beginning of the budgetary process. this is important to note, because we are today at a level which means an excessive increase in the eu budget. if implemented, the increase would impose additional costs on all eu households. for sweden, this would bring in some eur 65 million. i think most people are prepared to accept that, provided the money is used properly. however, i am not sure that this proposal is considered to be such. so it is a matter of making good use of time. this is of course the case for the committee on budgets and the european parliament, which must set the necessary priorities, but above all the council of ministers has a clear responsibility. the increase is unreasonably high in category 1, the eu's comprehensive agricultural policy, on which the council of ministers has the final say. it is over 6% for both payments and commitments. the council (budget) meeting at the end of november should, in my view, take the initiative of a much lower rate of increase for category 1. if the council does so, it can also come back to parliament with greater credibility and demand corresponding action. priorities are important in all budgetary work. these are the enlargement as decided at the berlin summit last year and our commitment to live up to our commitments in serbia. by making the necessary budgetary priorities, we will be able to meet these two historic commitments. we will, as i see it, be able to do so within the framework of the budgetary plan which the european parliament has endorsed.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the protection of personal privacy is a fundamental human right. every day, vast amounts of personal data are recorded and circulated, for example in connection with telephone calls and payments on the internet. personal data circulate not least within the eu. the present proposal for a regulation provides a good basis for the protection of privacy. freedom of movement requires effective law enforcement. effective law enforcement is not as successful without effective privacy protection. the proposal would prohibit the eu from handling data on ethnic origin, political views, religion, health and sexuality. data on criminal background and security measures may be processed only if specific conditions are met. the data may only be processed if there is a legitimate reason and the persons concerned are informed of the data. i have long advocated an independent european privacy watchdog, without new bureaucratic structures. i think that mr paciotti has succeeded in his proposal for a european supervisor, who can guarantee the rights of individuals vis-à-vis the institutions, carry out monitoring and advise the institutions. at the same time, we will of course maintain and develop the cooperation that exists between the member states' own data protection authorities. it is important to establish a functioning system and to earn the trust of the people. therefore, the protection of privacy must be developed. the new eu charter of fundamental rights includes the protection of personal privacy. in the future, we may be able to go to court for further protection in this area. we who stand for european values in an eu-sceptic country now have another argument for the eu, namely a more effective protection of privacy, which is important for citizens in modern society. although the handling of this matter has been a little difficult, i would like to congratulate mr paciotti on a very good report. i hope, however, that the model we have used here, which has somehow prevented members from participating on a broad basis, will not be a guide for our future handling of these issues. paciotti has succeeded in uniting all the institutions. i therefore congratulate her and hope that the house will support the report.
- mr. speaker, i have to go. bowis has been a great speaker on this great and complex issue. in fact, there are a number of equally important and seemingly contradictory objectives to be achieved in this food safety issue. one of the issues is security. in this context, i would like to speak in favour of the cheap food that families with children buy and that children and young people eat. it is also about freedom to develop food and the food sector, but also about the preservation of europe's cultural heritage. a key part of this involves ensuring that the rich heritage of traditions can be preserved. i therefore support amendment no 15. however, i believe that one issue has not been properly discussed, namely the need for a uniform set of rules to be followed by a uniform and thorough control. the food sector as a whole is the largest employer in the european union! we must ensure that the rules are applied in the same way throughout the union! otherwise we will undermine both security and the free competition of the internal market! i would like to repeat this: make sure that the regulations are properly enforced, from south to north!
- mr. speaker, i have to go. i am happy and grateful today, both to the commission for having started work on better food safety and to mr bowis and the whole committee on the environment, public health and consumer affairs. i am grateful for the good cooperation we have had and for the willingness to compromise in order to produce a good report. we now hope for the support of parliament and we hope that the council and the commission will accept our proposals. the authority, together with the food and veterinary office, other scientific bodies and national authorities, must create a comprehensive system. as mr paulsen said, all member states and all companies, many of which are multinational, must comply with the legislation. if not, there must be a system of sanctions at eu level. that is why i would like to raise amendment no 16. when food safety is threatened, both human health and the common market are at stake. we must therefore make such changes to the eu treaty that the commission can be sanctioned in the food sector, just as it is today in the competition sector. the authority should be able to propose to the commission appropriate measures in this context.
