mr president, i would like to thank you. some time ago, i suffered a serious injury to my meniscus. i then turned to the medical staff and was told that i would have to wait three weeks for an examination and then at least another month for the much-needed surgery. i said, "i'm not going to. i can barely walk! i need help now! can't i come as an emergency patient? and i was told, "everyone in our queue is the same emergency". then it turned out that there was still a chance to come. suddenly, i was informed that if i had private health insurance or could pay the full cost myself, i could be examined immediately and treated by the same doctor who would otherwise have to wait two months for treatment. i'm a little ashamed to admit it, but i chose that solution. i had the surgery right away, and that's why i'm able to be here today. otherwise i'd be home with my leg in a bag. but it doesn't feel good, because every time a paying patient like me gets a head start, other people have to wait longer with their pains. the same doctors, the same nurses, the same health facilities and the same resources in the health sector are to be used, whether for regular patients or for patients who pay. this means that the larger the health insurance sector becomes, the less resources are left for public health care. i think it is completely wrong that money should determine who will be first in the queues. when we have a comprehensive system of supplementary insurance for those who can pay, or have employers who can pay, then we have what is referred to in the report, namely a distinction between basic care for 'ordinary people' and high-quality care for those who can pay. my group thinks that we should turn against this class-based organisation. we want common solidarity schemes that are equal for all. it is of course good to try to control the systems that exist today, as rocard proposes, but most importantly, the private systems must not grow at the expense of the common systems.
madam president, i would like to thank you. the french ban on meat meal in animal feed came too late. at this stage, the only way to break the cycle of bse infection is to completely ban the use of meat meal as feed. as long as it cannot be guaranteed that carcass meal will not be mixed with uncontaminated meat meal, all meat meal must be banned as feed. i am not a late bloomer, but it is very difficult for me in this debate not to remember what it was like in the early 1990s when, as the swedish negotiator for agriculture, i had to negotiate an exception because we in sweden had a ban on the use of cadaver flour. it was a near-miss to get such an exemption then, but we got it, even though we were laughed at a little and told that it was harmless. today, people don't laugh anymore. that is what happened, mr president. you mentioned that this was due to a wrong production method and that this is why meat meal has been banned in france. that's why we banned it a long time ago back home in sweden. it turned out that wrong production methods are wrong and they must be fought from the start. i would like to stress a couple of other things: the resolution which i hope will be voted on tomorrow contains some very important points. the first is that every raw material in every kind of food should be traceable back in the chain. in a situation such as that which has now arisen in france, and also in the past in many other countries, it is urgent to be able to tell consumers where the products they are getting come from and how these products can be traced. this is a very important requirement, not least in the context of the bse issue. secondly, it is important - and this is not new - to stress that anyone who deliberately allows a sick or suspected sick animal into the food or feed chain is committing a criminal offence. this is something that every food producer must be aware of. of course, many people are affected by this situation, not least many farmers. in some cases, entire herds of animals have to be slaughtered, which is a severe blow. it is also important to remember that national systems for eradicating this terrible disease must include measures which adequately compensate farmers for the economic damage they suffer in this context. i would like to point out that we in the liberal group have tabled an amendment which i hope will be voted on. in conclusion, i would just like to thank commissioner byrne, who i think is doing a good job on this issue.
mr president, i would like to thank you. we live in a paradoxical society. in economic policy we are doing everything we can to increase efficiency, speed and dynamism. in the harsh climate that ensues, more and more people are left out, marginalized and rejected. so the more the economy flourishes, the more people seem to be pushed into poverty. it can make you pessimistic. despite the money, and despite the fact that europe has never been richer, it seems to be harder and harder to protect the weak and give the marginalised a real chance. that is why the figueiredo report is very important and welcome. i would like to congratulate her warmly on her report. it is about encouraging member states to do something about poverty and exclusion. it is particularly good that she emphasizes so strongly that beautiful words are not enough and that solidarity costs money. of course, the best thing is to provide good jobs for as many people as possible, but in a highly competitive economy, many people are not doing well. the report suggests that this may be the case for women who work both outside and inside the home. this may be the case for older people whose skills are no longer needed. it can apply to people with mental difficulties. this may be the case for large groups who do not have sufficient language skills to get a job. this is especially true of the many people who work but whose wages are too low to live on. all these groups are threatened with social exclusion unless there is very extensive support. the member states must therefore allocate much more money than at present to social measures aimed at combating exclusion, poverty and social misery. i mentioned the paradox of society. the prevailing ideology is that people should take responsibility for their own lives. however, i believe that exclusion and poverty are created by social conditions and that the responsibility is therefore shared. in a good society, there must be a place for everyone, which is a shared responsibility.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i should like to begin by thanking the rapporteur for a very excellent report. we have all spoken about the very good economic development we have, with good growth, declining unemployment and increasing employment. at the same time, i think we are seeing in all the eu countries increasing gaps and lasting gaps from the recession we had. it tells us that labour market policy and traditional employment policy are not enough, but we must include more policies to combat exclusion and poverty. this is a multi-dimensional problem. of course, labour market policy is part of this. it is important that unemployment should decline and employment should increase, but this is not enough; education policy must also be included, not least in view of the new society; where knowledge is important, it must be available to everyone. social security policy is also an important area. in various situations of life, such as when one is a parent of small children, unemployed, sick or when one is getting old, one should not have to become poor or marginalized but be able to participate in society. housing policy is also an important area, for example the right to housing is important for inclusion in society. moreover, all political levels must be involved. we are used to the local level, we are used to the regional level and the national level. however, we also know that, given the economic situation we have today, with the crossborder economy, it is necessary that we act at european level to create added value, not to take over something else. that is why the process of this programme is important, while at the same time it is important to link up different policy areas, as was discussed in lisbon, a discussion which will continue at the stockholm meeting, and which means linking up social security policy, labour market policy and education policy with economic policy. a programme like this, the fight against exclusion and poverty, is a perfect fit.
mr president, let there be no doubt a combination of investments in renewables and energy efficiency is the only realistic way for the european union to meet the requirements of the kyoto protocol and reduce our heavy dependence on fossil fuels. i congratulate the commissioner for her initiative, as well as mrs rothe for her report. i only wish that this directive were part of a coherent long-term energy strategy of the union. but it is not. instead, proposals related to energy are submitted one at a time and without much consistency. this is not your fault, commissioner. it is primarily caused by member states. we will not be successful in our efforts to transform our energy system in the union unless we make energy policy – including taxation – part of a common policy. the problem we have with different support systems in the member states, referred to by mr chichester, is but one example of the need for a more common approach in this field. finally a word on costs. all new technologies are expensive in the beginning. but experience tells us from almost every possible field that every time demand increases by 100% costs come down by an estimated 10 to 15%. renewable energy is no exception. i could show you figures and curves here demonstrating that for both wind energy and photovoltaics this mathematical formula applies. hence, i am optimistic about the future potential for renewables provided the right incentives are put in place. i wish you all possible success, commissioner.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the battlefield has shifted since the end of the cold war to villages, streets and homes. conflicts arise within rather than between states. war and conflict have always been a male domain, but most of the victims, 90 percent, are civilians. women and girls are also the victims of special forms of violence. they are raped, kidnapped and held as sex slaves. widows in conflict are forced to raise children and care for the elderly on their own. yet women are excluded from the peace negotiations, and their needs and priorities are ignored. in the commission for the reconstruction of tajikistan, with 25 000 widows, only 1 out of 26 participants was a woman. at the first peace negotiations to end the conflict in burundi, 2 of 126 delegates were women. no bosnian women participated in the peace negotiations that ended the war in bosnia. this was despite the fact that the whole world was aware of the systematic rape of women and the responsibility they would be forced to take in order to rebuild society. in the negotiations that preceded the nato bombing of kosovo four years later, there was only one woman in the delegation. when the stability pact for the reconstruction of south-eastern europe was created, women were not mentioned at all, despite their unique experience of peacebuilding at grassroots level. even less were they part of the committee responsible for the reconstruction. you can't build a house on half a foundation! if we exclude 50 percent of the population, we will not have lasting peace. women must be fully involved in all peace negotiations, in all reconstruction work and in peacekeeping operations. while formal peace processes are almost exclusively a male domain, international experiences and declarations show the importance of women's participation in conflict resolution and peacebuilding. however, in the few cases where civil society and women have participated in formal peace negotiations, they have been successful, for example in south africa, guatemala and cambodia. this is also reflected in a series of un documents, most recently the resolution adopted by the security council on women, peace and security two weeks ago. these include protecting the civilian population, refugees and asylum seekers, combating impunity for war rape, and promoting the active participation of women in conflict resolution and peacebuilding at all levels. that is the subject of my report, which has received wide support in the committee. in order to enable the ppe-de group to vote in favour of the resolution, i have agreed to compromise amendment no 19 to paragraph 12, which is supported by both the pse group and the ppe-de group. i propose an amendment to the fourth geneva protocol, so that rape, forced pregnancy and sexual slavery are defined as war crimes on the same footing as terrorism and torture. i call on the member states to ratify the treaty of rome for a permanent international criminal court. such a court is a must if the trend of systematically using rape as a strategy of war is to be broken. furthermore, all peacekeepers, both men and women, should receive gender equality training. this is particularly true of peacekeeping forces. the target should be at least 40 per cent women in all peace and development work. local participation in peace and reconciliation processes is important. i therefore call on the member states and the commission to provide technical, financial and political support to individual organisations working in conflict prevention and peacebuilding. although the proportion of women has been limited, studies show that women's participation in un peacekeeping forces improves relations with the local population and general morale. the presence of male peacekeepers, however, leads to an increase in sexual violence and prostitution around the camps. un soldiers, including from eu countries, have been sent home after allegations of sexual violence in somalia and mozambique. peace is too heavy a burden to be placed on just one institution, one government, or one gender. sustainable peace cannot be achieved if half of humanity is ignored. peace can only be achieved if women are fully involved in the peace process.
mr president, i would like to thank you. it is customary to begin a public talk by thanking the person for a job well done. in my case, that is impossible, because i think that mr theorin's report should be rejected. i also think that the chairman of the committee on women's rights is balancing on a thin line when she claims that there is broad support for a report which was adopted by eight votes to five. it is disrespectful to parliament to use an own-initiative report to push through matters which are not covered by the mandate. theorin was to write an own-initiative report on women's participation in peaceful conflict resolution. instead, it covers everything from how women are in refugee camps to how women develop strength and power. in its zeal to oppose all evil, it condemns systematic rape and sexual slavery. i personally condemn all rape and all slavery! the commission is not prepared to accept this. i would like to begin by thanking the rapporteur. let me state that housework is important; it should be respected, and it needs to be valued. we liberals can therefore support most of the conclusions of the report. we will nevertheless vote against the report as a whole, on the basis of the principle of subsidiarity. this is a matter for the member states. some of the proposals are too detailed. however, some important points for member states to consider are: the dual work of women needs to be addressed, domestic work is often heavy with many jobs, wages are too low, undeclared work is increasing, and there is often exploitation of women, often immigrants. many are excluded from social security systems. the member states must ensure that migrant women, often from the candidate countries, have decent working conditions, and this is a vital duty for a social europe. an expanded approach to domestic work is a good way for many to enter the labour market, including young people and immigrants. today, "white" domestic services are often too expensive, since vat and employer's contributions must be paid. tax deductions and simplifications should be tried. it must be easier to start and run businesses in the domestic services sector. in my home country, sweden, you often hear two main arguments against facilitating paid domestic work: firstly, it would be against equality between men and women. it's wrong! all those who work in the black today would have a job with decent social conditions. both economic and moral reasons therefore support the report's intentions, but it must be solved at national level, not at eu level.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. it is a very interesting report on the regulation of home help, which has been put forward by rapporteur smet, but it does give me a bit of mixed feelings. it is of course absolutely necessary to try to deal with undeclared work in various sectors of society, not least in the field of domestic services. it is also time that we give more status to homework and recognize domestic work as a full-fledged profession. it is also important to ensure that migrant women are not exploited, from low pay to lack of social security to outright oppression. however, requiring member states to adapt both prices and tax deductions for household services by means of a directive or other legislation, as proposed in the report, sends the wrong signal. it's too far and it's the wrong way to go. it contradicts the desire to see domestic work as a full-fledged profession. for what is it that says you should have tax deductions for having your floors repaired but not your garbage collected? to see domestic services as something separate from other services is something quite different from raising the status of domestic work. finally, i would just like to add that i do not have a report which recognises that in the near future we will be in great need of the labour that our immigrants provide.
the committee on foreign affairs, human rights, common security and defence policy has tabled an opinion. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. transparency and openness are the foundations of democratic governance and are essential for citizens to follow and participate in the public decision-making process. the possibility of access to public documents creates greater trust and legitimacy, which the eu unfortunately does not have in abundance today. international research also shows a clear link between open government and low levels of corruption. the cashman report is therefore not just any report. this report lays the foundation for what will become a european principle of publicity, that is, the principle that all documents should be public. the exceptions shall be few and clearly defined. all documents shall be recorded. the examination shall be carried out on an individual basis. reasons for refusal shall be given. the committee on foreign affairs and security rejects the idea that documents relating to foreign and security affairs should be automatically and as a group excluded from public scrutiny. this would be unacceptable. in this area too, each document must be examined individually. we have therefore proposed, as a democratic vent, that documents which after examination cannot be made public could be shared with a small group of members of parliament. it is important, however, that this should in no way replace the ambition to make as many documents as possible available to the general public: journalists, interest groups and individual citizens. article 255 of the ec treaty is about citizens' access to documents. the rights of the institutions should be further developed in specific provisions. the cashman report is the first step on a long road towards greater openness, democracy and public confidence. a common directive is necessary and important. it will also require a lot of work to change the attitudes and culture within the various institutions towards a realisation that openness is necessary, natural, effective and democratic. now we wait for the council's move.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the cashman report can be summed up in three words: citizens' power over bureaucracy. the right of public access to the documents of the eu institutions is one of the cornerstones of building an open and democratic europe. access to information also stimulates greater efficiency. this is a significant step forward towards a more open and democratic europe. it is gratifying that parliament has managed to create a broad majority for more openness. i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr cashman, and mrs maij-weggen for the very good cooperation which has actually provided a good starting point for the continuation of the negotiations. there are no block exemptions in the area of foreign affairs and security, and there should be no block exemptions in the future. the principle of access to documents should naturally apply to all eu institutions and bodies. it is therefore very important that we have a diary, a register, so that citizens have a reasonable opportunity to see what documents are in existence. the principle of public access to documents must be universal and fundamental, but it must of course be accompanied by certain well-defined exceptions. the citizens must be protected. the commission proposed 16 exemptions, which we thought was too many. so we've reduced them to six. in each case, the justification for classifying a document shall be required. nor may secrecy be issued for all time. as soon as the need for secrecy is no longer justified, the document must of course be made public. there may be negotiating strategies, which mean that a document may have to be kept secret before a meeting, but once decisions have been taken, there may no longer be a need to keep it secret. the document should then be made available. in addition, any confidentiality decision shall be subject to appeal. the court of justice, the ombudsman or the responsible eu authority will be able to be contacted and parliament will always be able to challenge the confidentiality of the information. another important principle, which has been one of my strongholds since the beginning of the transparency debate, is that documents must be made available in electronic form on the internet to the greatest possible extent. this is an important point which parliament has taken up and will no doubt reiterate today. i think this is a very good example of swedish and nordic influence on the eu system.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to begin by thanking the rapporteurs for a very good job. increased transparency will reduce bureaucracy, reduce the scope for fraud and increase the legitimacy of the institutions among citizens. i shall not go into the details of the report - of course one can always ask for more details - but i shall stick to the broad principles. the proposal on our table now has as its principle that all documents should be public. the exceptions are much less and more explicit than in the commission proposal. the public shall be guaranteed the widest possible access to documents and in the simplest possible manner. the same applies to third country nationals. a register of all documents shall be kept. the processing of an access request will be relatively quick. the countries with better systems are not adversely affected, but can maintain them. in addition, a report will be submitted to parliament every year so that we can monitor this activity on an ongoing basis. i would also like to stress that all citizens should be guaranteed access to documents in as many forms as possible. in our group we have also discussed the possibility of giving documents to those who are visually impaired or have other types of disability in a form other than written. i would like to draw attention to the internet and other possibilities which may facilitate access to documents in the future. i would like to conclude by saying that we in parliament must support this comprehensive proposal with great unanimity. the problem is not with us. the point now is that in our negotiations with the council and the commission we must produce a proposal and achieve a good result. this is the most important thing, and it is what we have before us.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank the rapporteur and all those involved in this work. it is a very important report, which forms an important part of what we could call the new eu principle of publicity. in addition to this legislative proposal, which regulates the right of citizens to documents, this includes the framework agreement between the commission and parliament and other agreements such as the solana decision. this is our chance to make the eu more open and more democratic. it would be a great disappointment if we were not to succeed. it is about the rule of law, efficient administration and effective popular government. we must remember that this proposal is to establish the right of citizens to have access to documents, without any need for them to justify why or to show that they are concerned. we should not confuse it with the role of the european parliament or with future interinstitutional agreements. the cashman report is an improvement on the commission proposal, but in my view it is not enough. we still have to vote for many of the amendments tabled by the greens and the liberals, among others. the amendment on a register is particularly important. furthermore, citizens should not have to have their rights interpreted, but should have the right to documents in order to be able to decide for themselves what is important.

sweden about neutrality, a concept that most of us recognize as an outdated relic of the cold war and not applicable today. nevertheless, we in the european parliament, as representatives of the citizens, have a special responsibility to conduct this debate, to critically examine and discuss the emerging defence and security policy. this means that there must be structures for us parliamentarians to be able to be informed about decisions, to be able to debate, to participate and to be held accountable. however, citizens must also have access to information and documents to the greatest possible extent. it is absolutely necessary for the policy to gain confidence. the so-called solana decision of last summer was loudly criticised in this house. this meant that all foreign and security policy documents were automatically excluded from public view. of course, that is unacceptable. that is also why we have asked the court to review this decision. the same ideas of maximum transparency were expressed in the cashman report, which we adopted by a large majority two weeks ago in strasbourg. i therefore hope that the liberal amendments which deal specifically with the question of openness and transparency for citizens will receive broad support. finally, i would like to express my group's deep concern at the worsening situation in afghanistan and call on the commission and the council to do everything in their power to step up efforts to reach a peaceful solution and to assist the civilian population, especially now as we face the winter.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to point out three things: firstly, the lalumière report contains a whole programme for how the eu should become a military superpower in the relatively near future. this may seem incomprehensible when you consider that europe has no enemies and is not threatened by any military power. secondly: the report deals mainly with offensive military operations outside the eu's own territory. the great power of the eu must be able to intervene anywhere in the world where its interests or values are threatened. the eu thus becomes the equivalent of the united states in this area too. i am convinced that this policy would be met with distrust by the people of europe if they only knew about it. thirdly: the report, which is very detailed, does not say a word about the non-aligned states or about non-alignment as such. the closest one comes to this is a mocking statement in paragraph 5 of the explanatory statement about 'countries claiming to be 'neutral'. the non-aligned countries must protest loudly and clearly against such phrases and against the report as a whole.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to congratulate mr brok on his pan-european approach in both the speech and the report. i agree with his reasoning for enlargement. he noted that the accession negotiations with the 12 new member states had proved to be the most effective instrument for stabilising the mediterranean region and central and eastern europe. what we are doing now is strengthening in every way the conditions for freedom and peace in europe. the brok report also mentions the post of high representative for the common foreign and security policy. in the long run, i think the current relationship is an untenable situation. it's an exciting debate tonight. i would like to welcome the unanimity and the boldness with which we are now discussing the lalumière report on the security and defence policy of the union in this parliament. in order to achieve credibility, i think it is important that the members of the council say the same thing in brussels and the eu as they do in their own countries. in my home country, sweden, there is a foreign minister who requires a un mandate for eu action. the debate there is very much about whether sweden as an eu member is still a neutral country. you're gonna be pissed, but that's the way it is. i also believe that it is very important that the ambitions which the eu is now expressing are accompanied by concrete resources, otherwise there is a risk that this will become mere talk. finally, i would point out that it is important for the political leaders of the union to make clear the strong ambition that now exists in order to involve the citizens in these important and far-reaching plans.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the brok report on the common foreign and security policy highlights the following message in paragraph 31: the european parliament considers that, as russia is united with belarus in a political union, it is important for the democratic legitimacy of russia that its president and government make clear and persistent the need for comprehensive democratic reforms in belarus, not least in the light of the report presented by the parliamentary troika of the european union, the council of europe and the osce in minsk on 16 october 2000. i am the author of this amendment, which has been supported by a large majority in the committee on foreign affairs. does russia want to be in political union with a non-democratic regime in the long term? i would also like to mention four people whose names have disappeared in belarus between 1998 and 2000 and a politician who died in unclear circumstances. these persons are yuri zaharenko, former minister of the interior and deputy chairman of the 13th supreme soviet of belarus; victor gonchar, deputy chairman of the 13th supreme soviet of belarus and chairman of a criminal investigation against president lukashenko; mr krasovsky, a friend of deputy chairman gonchar and disappeared with him; dmitry zavadsky, a russian tv cameraman who disappeared under mysterious circumstances from the airport in minsk on 7 july this year and finally yuri karpenko, leader of the civil party and former mayor of molodechno, who died suddenly and in unclear circumstances in august 1999. i would like to say to patten, solana and the incoming swedish presidency: act strongly and unitedly against the anti-democratic regime in belarus!
mr president, i would like to thank you. let me first express my gratitude to both the commission and the three rapporteurs for their important work on this issue. the erika disaster in the autumn of 1999 has caused a lot of reactions, which is good. at the same time, it would have been even better if we had acted in advance rather than reacting afterwards. then we might have avoided a disaster. there is much good in what we are going to decide today. this applies both to the stricter requirements concerning the design of oil tankers, to better rules on port inspection and to the resulting tightening of the rules for classification societies. at the same time, i have many questions. what is the point, for example, of preventing ships from calling at an eu port if we do not prevent them from operating in our waters? realistic estimates suggest that we will only be able to stop another 10-12 ships a year. will that be enough? the last major accident with oil spilling as a result just a few weeks ago was with a double-hull vessel. this means that the compulsory introduction of this type of hull will not provide a safe solution either. let me mention one more thing, namely all the cases of oil spills which are not accidents but purely intentional spills. oil transport in the baltic sea has doubled since 1995 and will triple in a few years. every day, we're hit with an average of two new emissions. a short time ago a maltese flagged vessel dumped oil in swedish waters off gotland. then the ship entered the port of tallinn in estonia. according to the marpol convention, sweden has no right to act, as the vessel is no longer in swedish waters. estonia cannot act either, as the ship has not caused any damage there. malta, for its part, probably does not want to act, as it has great economic benefits from this type of vessel, registered under the flag of malta. that way, another environmental nightmare is set free. the proposals we have put forward so far will not solve these problems in the future either. i therefore believe that there is still a great deal to be done in this area.
in writing. -the 'package' of proposals for legislation on safety at sea is welcome and responds to a need. this was illustrated by the disaster of the oil tanker erika. i voted in favour of the following provisions: a) safety on board ships, measures to prevent pollution and those relating to living and working conditions on board ships in community ports; (b) the requirement to equip oil tankers with double hulls; the establishment and enforcement of vessel control; (d) provisions on accidental or intentional marine pollution. i have also voted in favour of all the amendments which improve and strengthen the commission's proposals. it is particularly important to stress that the principle of 'the polluter pays' must be fully applied. in recent years there has been a marked increase in the number of oil tankers operating in the atlantic, the english channel and the baltic sea, which means that there is an increased risk of accidents with negative consequences for the environment. however, tanker traffic in the baltic sea will be able to increase dramatically, as russia plans to build several oil ports along the coast in the gulf of finland, in what appears to be a large area of concentration of the russian oil industry. in the city of primorski, not far from vyborg, construction has already begun on a major port for exporting russian oil, and a second one is planned in the same area. finland, with the support of the commission's directorate-general for the environment, has attempted to get the russian authorities to carry out an environmental impact assessment in accordance with the esbo convention. unfortunately, russia did not accede to this request. an oil accident would have disastrous consequences for the gulf of finland and the baltic sea. the baltic is a very fragile inland sea, already heavily polluted by industrial, wastewater and agricultural waste. the commission and the council must now apply the requirement to carry out an environmental impact assessment (eia) and inform the russian authorities that any future oil trade with russia will be subject to this condition.
in writing. -{j) we vote in favour of the resolution, but would like to make the following comments: we are in favour of extending the qualified majority in the council. however, we believe that this method of decision-making should not cover the questions under the second pillar. in cases where qualified majority voting is used, we think it should be combined with the right of co-decision of parliament. we would like to support the reference to the charter of fundamental rights in article 6.2 of the treaty. however, we are opposed to the charter being incorporated into the treaty and to the treaty being transformed into a european constitution. on the common foreign and security policy, we agree with the priority given to crisis management. we are also prepared to support the principle of enhanced cooperation, provided that its development is subject to control by the union institutions.
in writing. -{v) in its resolution, the european parliament advocates (as is customary) the promotion of codecision for its own benefit. it would be good if the european parliament were to devote as much enthusiasm to the debate on how to increase participation in the european elections. in the 1999 elections, this participation would have been in the order of 40% if a number of member states had not applied the compulsory voting system and if national elections had not been combined with this ballot. the european parliament's zeal to get more frequent use of co-decision within the eu has no strong anchorage in the electorate. the democratic debate, as it is taking place in europe today, is based on elections to national parliaments in the various member states. in order to build a more democratic eu, national parliaments must be given more influence over the work of the union.
in writing. -sv) i welcome the ambition of the nice european council meeting, which is to prepare the way for the enlargement process. nevertheless, the joint resolution contains a number of phrases with which i do not agree. i am opposed to the development of a common defence policy and the establishment of a council of defence ministers. i cannot accept the application of qualified majority voting for decisions in the field of trade policy, nor do i support this method of decision-making for the second pillar, that is to say the field of foreign, security and defence policy. the division of the union into a and b teams is a mistake, and i therefore disagree with the idea of allowing one third of the member states to go ahead of the others. i would also consider it a mistake to incorporate the charter into the treaty.
in writing. -{{v) we welcome and support this report, which highlights many of the key trends in the development of the common foreign and security policy. however, we cannot accept the idea of transferring the role of the high representative to the commission. nor can we approve of the transformation of community delegations into eu embassies. the member states have the responsibility for diplomatic matters. we believe that this transformation of the commission delegations into delegations of the european community would risk creating conflicts of interest with the national diplomatic delegations of our various countries.
in writing. -{{v) we voted against the lalumière report, in particular for the following reasons: 1. the we believe that there is no real threat to europe at the moment: on the contrary, it is high time to pursue a policy of active disarmament and the development of civil society. we are convinced that peace in europe cannot be built by military alliances. the conflicts of the future will not be solved by military intervention but by policies aimed at social, economic and environmental justice. in this area of peace policy, our analysis is based on the conflict theory that militarisation does not make the world safer or more peaceful, but on the contrary contributes to increasing insecurity and the risk of military conflict. 2. the we believe that civilian crisis management is a valuable instrument for conflict prevention, and we must note that this is a point which is not sufficiently addressed in the report. 3. the we believe that military action within the framework of the common security and defence policy should only be taken in the case of resolutions issued by the united nations security council and in accordance with those resolutions - a view which the rapporteur does not seem to share. 4. the the report is based on an interventionist philosophy which is not ours. we believe that the rights of peoples and the sovereignty of national states should be respected and that these principles should be the guiding principle of international policy. the report cites the eu's common values and interests as a justification for militarizing the union. we do not believe that these values, which are not defined and whose scope seems to us to be questionable, justify militarization. the fifth. mrs lalumière makes it clear that the eu may one day be forced to defend the borders of all its member states against third countries. this prospect is incompatible with the non-alignment of certain member states. 6. the in addition, the question of non-alignment is not mentioned at all in the report, and there is even a suspicion in the explanatory statement that there is a certain lack of respect for neutral states. point 49 provides for the obligation on member states which are unwilling or unable to take part in a mission to take part and finance it. this is also incompatible with non-alignment. 7. the we believe that promoting this idea of large-scale defence is a mockery of the applicant countries, which are already in a very difficult economic situation and need to spend money on social protection, for example, rather than on arms.
in writing. - i voted against the report. i would like to mention, among other points in the text which call for my criticism, the following example: point 49: 'in any event, requests that a state which does not wish to participate in a mission be obliged, in the name of mutual solidarity between the member states, to pay a financial contribution which will be shared out among the participating states'. point 50: 'finally, the petersberg missions will eventually have to be financed by the general budget of the european communities, which will imply a revision of article 28 teu and the financial perspective.' i think that these two points go so far that it is difficult to speak of the right of non-aligned states, sweden for example, to make independent decisions on defence policy, and their right not to interfere in armed conflict. these are therefore excessive formulations and therefore justify a vote against the report as a whole.
in writing. -{sv) i appreciate the efforts being made to develop a common security policy, but i am opposed to the creation of a common military defence. the formulations concerning, for example, cfsp and nato seem to me to go far too far when it comes to using the 'threat of the use of force' as a means of pressure in conflict prevention actions, which is in direct contravention of the un charter. the same applies to the passages on improving the eu's ability to penetrate enemy air defenses and air-sea interventions, as well as those on enhanced cooperation on collective defence guarantee. i cannot therefore support the report.
in writing. - sv) i agree with the overall approach of the report, but i would like to say that i disagree with point 49. it calls for countries which do not wish to participate in a mission to be obliged, in the name of mutual solidarity between member states, to make a financial contribution which would be shared out among the participating states. i think that is going too far.
in writing. - (da) we voted against the brok and lalumière reports for several reasons. we do not believe that rearmament and, therefore, an increase in military spending is the answer to the challenges facing europe in recent years. ten years after the upheavals in eastern europe, we believe that the 'european peace project' referred to in these reports is a bad signal to the applicant countries. the report also attacks neutral countries which are asked to contribute financially to the common european security and defence policy. the motto 'unity in diversity' would lose all meaning, especially for neutral member states, if only parts of the reports had to be produced. we cannot accept the introduction of a new territorial defence which would fall under the responsibility of a vice-president of the commission. such a break with nato for the simple purpose of closing europe even more to itself is far from the fine speeches that followed the fall of the wall. we certainly do not need the creation of various military structures. the eu would instead benefit from changing the priorities set in the petersberg missions and should focus its efforts on civilian and humanitarian tasks.
in writing. -{\v) we wish to express our overall support for this report, but we find it difficult to accept the detailed rules it contains on certain points, and our vote will reflect this reluctance, because these provisions are contrary to the principle of subsidiarity. furthermore, the text goes beyond the original task assigned to it, as defined in its title. many of the problems mentioned in the report are quite real, but they have no place in this document.
in writing. - s7v) we shall vote against this report on so-called 'domestic services'. the emergence of such models, sometimes referred to as the 'new labour market', particularly in belgium and denmark, clearly heralds the maintenance of a subclass of service providers, consisting mainly of immigrant women but also of longterm unemployed men. the fact that such a job market is being subsidised by taxation is quite shocking to us. the money from taxation must be used in other ways, including giving people the opportunity to develop and offer them life choices. the problems raised in the report can be solved by a humane policy on asylum and the reception of refugees, by a work organisation which takes account of the balance between work and leisure time, and by the collective and solidarity-based empowerment of society towards people with special needs.
in writing. -{\v) we voted against the smet report on the standardization of domestic work. the rapporteur considers that a legal framework is needed at european level to enable people who work in this field to be covered by labour legislation. mrs smet proposes, in particular, that a european definition of domestic work be drawn up. the rapporteur also calls for member states to introduce a tax deduction for domestic services, in order to reduce the difference between the costs of undeclared work and 'legal' work. there is a high demand for domestic work. however, the importance of taxation in sweden, as in the other countries of the union, prevents supply and demand from coinciding in a legal market. income tax, social security contributions and vat raise the hourly rate to levels that virtually no household with a normal income is prepared to pay. it is more or less by force of circumstances that paid domestic work is now done in the dark. this labour market has a large number of women and young people. they have no rights, no insurance, no pension points, no social security, no protection of any kind. for many young people, the first contact with the labour market may be in the form of unskilled work. it is quite unfortunate that absurd tax rules encourage people to work underground in this way. we therefore agree with the rapporteur that it is important to reduce the differences in the costs of undeclared work and legal work, and we believe that member states should introduce tax reductions in the field of domestic services. in this way, persons engaged in such activities will be able to benefit from insurance, pension schemes, security and social protection. we are opposed to the establishment of a detailed legal framework for such jobs at european level. these are matters which must be dealt with at national, regional or local level, and we shall therefore vote against the report.
in writing. -sv) we are in favour of integrating domestic services into the labour market, but we find it difficult to accept the detailed regulations at union level which the report advocates in certain cases, and which are contrary to the principle of subsidiarity. in addition, the report goes beyond its mandate as defined in the title.
mr president, i would like to thank you. members of the house! following the judgment of the court of justice of the european communities on tobacco advertising, this report and the amended proposal for a directive have been called into question from various quarters. there are also amendments to reject the whole of parliament's proposal. however, we believe that there are very big differences between the issue of tobacco advertising and this directive. we think it is natural that article 95 should be the legal basis for this directive, together with article 133, which the committee is now proposing in amendment no 1. the directive on which the proposal is based is based on article 95 and the question is directly related to the content of goods in the internal market. we are positive about the content of the report and support its main points. it is equally important to set clear limit values for the dangerous substances in smoke. amendment no 2 on carbon monoxide is therefore particularly positive. we can also support the proposals on the size and wording of the warning texts. in that case, the differences between the various positions are so small that it should not be difficult to find a compromise in conciliation. we are also in favour of a ban on addictive substances. as regards the export from the european union of cigarettes which are banned in the union for health reasons, we believe that the only reasonable and consistent thing to do is to stop them. there is no need to impose special conditions on exports and to extend the time limits compared to production for use in the european union. the report also mentions special brands of cigarettes marketed as milda, light and the like. this is often misleading marketing, which gives the false impression that these cigarettes do not carry the same risks as other cigarettes. we see no reason to allow this kind of marketing and believe that the committee's current position in this area risks giving rise to too many exceptions. although the subsidies for tobacco production are not regulated by this report, it is impossible not to touch on the subject which is one of the absurdities of the european union. it is unreasonable for the union to invest the enormous sums of money in tobacco growing that are being done today. it's inconsistent, and it's going to be expensive. this money could be of great use elsewhere. the only reasonable thing to do is to phase out the subsidies and help the farmers concerned to switch to other crops. in the past, when this report has been discussed, the swedish snus has been discussed. i do not think there is any reason to change the rules that are currently in force, i.e. an exception for sweden but at the same time a right for those countries that do not want to sell snus to maintain their ban. i am therefore glad that there is no change in that regulatory framework.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the development of victim empowerment is a real sunshine story. at first, most were against it. but now most people agree, and i think that's good. i welcome the proposal from portugal that the position of victims should also be strengthened in the criminal proceedings, although i fully respect the legal views expressed by lehne earlier. people who are victims of crime when they exercise their freedom of movement should not have to suffer twice, so that they are treated worse. this applies irrespective of the type of crime. the aim here is to strengthen the position of the victims, but this cannot be done without also talking about crime prevention measures. the best way to help victims of crime is to ensure that no crime is committed, and the most important way to prevent crime is likely to be to spread information. crime prevention measures must also focus on the fight against organised crime. victims need to know what rights and opportunities for help they actually have. most member states provide some form of first aid to victims of crime. sometimes travellers also need different and perhaps more support than the local population. this may include psychological support and language assistance and a number of other factors. it can be difficult for a foreign victim to follow the judicial proceedings from a distance and it is clear that special measures are needed in this case. the victim must always be the center of attention. since the perpetrator is not the focus, it is worrying that some national tendencies are beginning to introduce prosecution forfeiture just because the perpetrator meets the victim. that is a line of action i cannot support. crime must be solved, and if it is not, the democratic system is threatened in the long run. who wants to participate and choose if your problems are completely set aside? in my country, the number of cases of abuse is increasing, and only over 20 percent of them are resolved. this percentage is too low. crime is not an accident. punishment also means respect for the victim. the basic principle that an individual must always be responsible for his own actions can never be waived, either at the level of the member states or at the level of the european union. the state must ultimately take responsibility for its failure to protect the victim. i am therefore pleased that the rapporteur has drawn up this report, which i warmly endorse. i would like to thank her for her contribution to strengthening the awareness of victims of crime in the member states.
in writing. - (sv) in practice, the summer period has become the normal period. the very concept of the "summer period" is therefore called into question, since it covers seven months out of the twelve, that is, most of the year.
in writing. - (sv) we are very much in favour of strengthening the position of victims of crime and therefore welcome the proposal for a framework decision put forward by portugal in principle. we want, above all, to see the establishment of principles at european level for the treatment of victims of crime, while the responsibility for drawing up the details of the necessary provisions should lie with the member states themselves. however, we believe that a number of issues deserve further analysis and we would therefore like to raise the following points of view: freedom of association is of fundamental importance in any democratic state of law: we are therefore opposed to the idea of requiring non-profit organisations to apply for state approval in order to be able to provide assistance to victims of crime. we also believe that transparency is one of the conditions necessary for the administrative authorities and the courts to inspire confidence in the citizens. we therefore oppose the establishment of general rules limiting the ability of the press to comment on facts revealed during a public trial. it is therefore appropriate to limit the exceptions to the rule of publicity of judicial proceedings to a small number of specific cases (for example, for the confidentiality of cases of a sexual nature and relating to the exploitation of children in pornographic material). finally, we believe that it might be useful to provide for the possibility of so-called parallel testimony (e.g. videoconferencing), if it is considered that a witness or a civil party does not freely report the truth in the presence of the accused, out of fear, or for some other reason.
mr president, i would like to thank you. in george orwell's book animal farm, there is a quote that describes what happened in nice in a way. it says that "all are equal, but some are more equal than others". translated to what happened in nice, it could be 'all eu countries are equal, but it's the big countries that decide'. what happened in nice was not only that the large member states of the union set the agenda, but they also brought about treaty changes that guarantee them the same power in an enlarged eu. the enlarged eu, on the basis of the treaty of nice, will be a more centralised union, with the small countries paying the full price of enlargement. for the swedish negotiators, it seems to have been more important to be the big players in the negotiations than to fight for their own influence. therefore, sweden receives fewer votes than comparable member states. it is difficult to see what in the nice decisions actually facilitates eu enlargement. the decision-making process in the council will become more complex, while decision-making will become more difficult. nice means more supranationality and centralism, which is exactly what the eu did not need. the real problems of the eu are the lack of democracy and popular support, but these issues were not discussed at all at the nice meeting. i can also inform the house that today there was a poll in sweden which showed that only 38 per cent of the population want to remain in the european union.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the most important result of nice was that it became a treaty after all, so that the candidate countries can now be prepared. otherwise, the meeting was neither particularly historic nor successful. i would like to make five points in my short minute on behalf of the liberal group: 1. it is not acceptable for the big countries to use blackmail against the small. it goes against the whole idea of the european union. it is tragic that the question of qualified majority voting was not taken further. the eu is now slowing down, its action is being paralysed and decision-making is becoming more complex. the third is that the method of the igc, which is closed and exclusive, is old-fashioned and undemocratic. citizens must be involved in the future.4. the forthcoming constitutional debate must lead to simplification, division of powers and increased democracy. the eu must have a common constitution. the fifth. a new igc cannot be used as an alibi to delay enlargement. our new colleagues must be allowed to participate in the discussions as new members on equal terms.
mr president, i would like to thank you. now that we have reached the final decision on the eu budget for 2001, we can say that we have had a year of constant struggle to get the budget done within the framework set when the budget plans were adopted in berlin in march 1999. today we can say that this battle has been successful. we will be able to adopt a budget within that framework, which is a great success. i think that both the member states and the taxpayers are grateful that the levies are not going to go up any further. i also see it as a clear success that we can enter a new financial year with a larger margin than before. when we use 1.06 per cent of the gnp of the member states, this gives us a margin that is greater than ever before, and greater than that which was foreseen in berlin less than two years ago. it gives us the opportunity to be an active partner both in enlargement to the countries of central and eastern europe and in helping to build peace and prosperity in serbia and the former yugoslavia. to make all this possible, we need to set out broad priorities for the future. this will be necessary if we are to meet the long-term commitments we have, which is clearly possible if our aim is to work for a strong but at the same time limited europe. i believe that there is a great threat today, namely bse. it is important to note, however, that we must also manage this within the existing budgetary limits. this means that if the solution to the problem requires even more money, it will have to be financed through other category 1 savings or by member states bearing a larger share of the cost.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank oostlander for his meritorious contribution as rapporteur. as a swedish christian democrat, i fully support the two-strategy model that oostlander is presenting, with a strong, consistent and persistent criticism of all crimes, not least crimes against human rights within russia, but which also involves close and confidential cooperation with russia, our great neighbour to the east. the reasons for the report state in paragraph kk: given that russia is in political union with belarus, it is important for russia's democratic legitimacy that president putin and the russian government clearly and consistently highlight the overwhelming need for democratic progress and reform in belarus, not least in the light of the report presented by the parliamentary troika of the eu, the council of europe and the organisation for security and cooperation in europe (osce) in minsk on 16 october 2000. mr president, i am the author of this paragraph, which has been approved by the committee on foreign affairs. i would like to emphasize the value of really highlighting the seriousness of russia's being in a political union with belarus. in connection with the report we presented in minsk, we pointed out first of all that the belarusian parliament today has no powers, cannot pass laws, has no veto power over presidential decrees, cannot adopt a budget. secondly, we pointed out that the electoral law has disqualified a quarter of the candidates in this so-called parliamentary election in october. third, we addressed the access to media that is unfair in a state-controlled system. fourthly, we brought up the administrative penal code which makes it illegal to call for a boycott of elections. belarus is thus in political union with russia, and it is important that we stress the seriousness of this for russia as well.

mr president, i would like to thank you. the efficiency of the business structures of the european market is necessary if europe is to compete in the global market. it must also be possible for companies to restructure within the union. this directive will probably not create the clear regulatory framework that is needed today in the international stock exchange market, but the battle is not yet lost. the problem is that stock exchange law is closely linked to company law, and it is difficult to harmonise one area without harmonising the other. no matter how you frame the rules, someone's gonna get hurt. in a small market like sweden with relatively concentrated ownership, a harmonised bidding obligation level of 30% will create problems, and we are calling for greater flexibility. more flexibility is also needed in the area of the full neutrality obligation which the council advocates. the german companies will face problems because german company law prohibits the diversification of voting rights. there must be a greater understanding of national characteristics, otherwise the impact on the structure of the enterprises of individual member states can be devastating. it would, however, be easier to solve the problem of which supervisory authority should have jurisdiction. the most flexible approach would be to choose the supervisory authority in the country where the target company has its registered office. companies may be listed on different markets, and companies with head offices in sweden are not always listed in sweden. it would be strange to say the least if, for example, us authorities were to be responsible for a takeover between two european companies. anyone who wants to avoid such consequences has the opportunity to vote in favour of amendment no 19, which clarifies that the seat of the target company is to be decisive. if we do not protect the duty of neutrality, the purpose of the directive is lost. moreover, if we open the door to defensive measures, we may make it more difficult to restructure european markets and, above all, to protect small shareholders. i would just like to emphasize that the conciliation committee has a very great responsibility and that success requires community.
madam president, i would like to thank you. i come from the country with the most liberalized postal sector in the world. it is also the country where the highest number of letters are delivered the day after they are placed in a mailbox. no other country in the european union has such a rapid distribution of letters abroad. there's a connection between all of this. although the formerly dominant swedish state-owned company is still by far the largest, the increasing competition from today's 50 other companies has forced it to become much more efficient than before. this is the future i wish for the other countries of the european union. let me say a few words, very briefly, about the obvious hypocrisy that is also present in this sector. the british royal mail and the french la poste, to name two examples, oppose all forms of competition in the uk and france respectively. this does not prevent both companies from wanting to participate and gain market shares in countries where deregulation has been implemented. indeed, it would be reasonable for the commission to examine the legality of those companies which deny other companies access to their markets but are willing to participate and take market shares where deregulation is implemented. let me end by thanking rapporteur ferber, who has made several attempts to reach a compromise. no shadow shall fall on him, but the will of others has been lacking. i do not consider the proposal before us today to be a compromise. it had become a compromise with a final date for when liberalisation should be completed. if that date is later than, say, 2007, i think it is completely pointless. by then, however, the market will have solved this problem. there is therefore no reason to support such an amendment today, otherwise i see no other option than to vote against the report.

the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection has tabled an opinion. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. as rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on the environment, i would like to say that i welcome the reaction of the committee on economic and monetary affairs to the taxation of aviation fuel. the arguments i have put forward have been largely accepted there. aviation is now the fastest growing source of greenhouse gases. it is estimated that about 5 percent comes from the air. air travel is also expected to double in about 15 years. if we are to meet the requirements for reducing greenhouse gas emissions, we must take all possible measures to limit them. one such measure is the increased use of economic instruments. as mr van dam also said, aviation fuel should therefore not be exempted from taxation. the commission's communication says that the impact is so small that nothing can be done. i'm going to challenge that line of reasoning. aviation is a fast-growing sector. if the effect is small today, it will certainly be greater tomorrow. such reasoning is dangerous, for it easily leads to a situation where no one is doing anything anywhere. we have to start somewhere. we cannot maintain the current structure, the current technology and achieve the environmental objectives. we have to change either the structure or the technology or both. i believe that the best thing for the various transport sectors, from the point of view of fairness and competition, is for us to go ahead and introduce a tax on aviation fuel in the eu. at the same time, however, we must push for the revision of the 1944 chicago convention, so that we can establish a different order in the international arena.

mr president, i would like to thank you. since mr sjöstedt cannot be present in the chamber, i would like to say a few words on his behalf on the report on the taxation of aviation fuel. air traffic is increasing rapidly. although its emissions are a small fraction of total greenhouse gas emissions, they are increasing very rapidly. finding effective methods for taxing aviation fuel is therefore one of the keys to climate policy. it would also create greater equity between modes of transport. the chicago convention has become completely outdated on this point. we need international agreements on environmental taxes, but not the ban on environmental taxes that the convention implies. therefore, paragraph 16 of the report on the renegotiation of the convention is particularly welcome. in the meantime, a system of environmental charges on flights within the eu would be a positive thing. here in the eu, we have an opportunity to show that we are serious about climate policy. we support the report and the amendments of the greens, but we are completely opposed to the amendments of the ppe.

mr president, i would like to thank you. i can fully support the common position. the argument for further restrictions on food additives is more about a political assessment of consumer needs for these additives. we should, however, try to avoid this type of assessment. i think we should instead rely on scientifically based risk assessments. the commission has decided to withdraw the authorisation for ethyl hydroxyethyl cellulose pending the council's examination of the matter. in sweden and finland, ethylhydroxyethylcellulose is used almost exclusively by people with gluten allergy. these allergy sufferers have long-standing knowledge of cellulose as a binder in bread in particular. if they fail to use it, they risk a poorly functioning diet. poor diet increases the risk of stomach upset and diarrhea. there is also an increased risk of short stature in children. in addition, chronic dietary problems increase the risk of cancer. last weekend i met with a number of nutritionists who were concerned that the withdrawal of the permit for ethyl hydroxyethyl cellulose would lead to problems of nutritional absorption in gluten-allergic people. my question to the commission is therefore: what are the chances of swedes with gluten allergy finding bread with this additive even after a withdrawal of the authorization, and does the commission intend to speed up the consultation in the scientific committee for food which will allow the matter to be reviewed and the decision to be changed? mr president, i would like to thank you. i have a christmas card from a 14-year-old boy in sweden. the kid's gluten-allergic and he's really struggling with his diet. he has now been informed that you in the european parliament want to ban cellulose in his diet and also in his christmas dinner. his christmas greeting to you all is: what are you doing in the eu?
mr president, i would like to thank you. as patrie has already said, 80 per cent of europe's population lives in cities. the prospects of these people for a good life depend entirely on the environment in which they live. unfortunately, cities today are almost automatically associated with a bad environment - high emissions, high traffic, and polluted waterways - but it doesn't have to be that way. better knowledge and new technologies are creating opportunities for a better environment in europe's cities. clear legislation and close monitoring are further tools. cities themselves have a large number of instruments at their disposal to help improve the environment for their own citizens. often it is in the towns and cities that people are best acquainted with their own problems. through sound urban planning, well-functioning public transport and cooperation with other municipal stakeholders, citizens can be provided with a better environment. in addition, in practice it is at the local level that the environmental decisions we take in this parliament are to be implemented. the climate issue is a good example of this. reduced transport and a shift away from fossil fuels at the local level are necessary to bring about a change in the climate threats we are experiencing. there must be a clear dialogue and good cooperation between the institutions of the union and the local level, that is to say the municipalities or cities. i think lienemann's report will help that. however, it is also necessary to integrate citizen involvement at local level. this can be done through cooperation with universities, ngos, businesses and other actors. for this reason, i believe that in the definition of those who will be able to apply for funds from the framework programme, there must be room for formations other than those which we traditionally regard as municipal organisations. there are many good examples where municipalities are included as one party among others. the breadth of responsibility is what matters. i think that should be a criterion. i therefore support amendment no 31.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to begin by congratulating the rapporteur on a very good report. i also welcome the commission's proposal, which provides important guidance for the eu and the member states in understanding and applying the precautionary principle. the proposal is of course an invaluable decision-making tool internationally where there is uncertainty about risks, products or manufacturing methods. i also fully share the commission's view that the precautionary principle is a general rule without a limited scope. like many colleagues, i was pleasantly surprised by the council resolution of the nice european council, which states that the precautionary principle should be applied as soon as possible after the harmful effects on health and the environment have been identified and when no firm conclusions can be drawn about the level of risk on the basis of a preliminary scientific study based on the available data. this is very far-reaching and of course very good. i would also appeal to my colleagues, before tomorrow's vote, not to weaken the text of the committee and the rapporteur by adopting the edd and ppe amendments. instead, we should try to strengthen the text even further to show that we are at least as progressive as the council. amendment no 9 is very important. it proposes that the reverse burden of proof should apply to products without prior authorization, i.e. that the producer should prove that the product is safe and not pass the risk and cost on to the consumer. of course, it is also important to increase the legal burden in order to make the precautionary principle a norm of international law. this is dealt with in amendment no 5 by the greens.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank patrie for her excellent report on the precautionary principle. it is no easy task to define the precautionary principle. i believe that it is impossible to create a general precautionary principle that applies in all situations. every situation requires its own precautionary principle. this is hard for us to accept, but food and chemicals are not the same thing. they cannot therefore be treated in the same way. our task is to create the framework needed to protect both the environment and human and animal health. the precautionary principle is not the same in our respective countries. what we must do now, however, is to create a common precautionary principle at eu level. the principle must be clear. we must be able to assert it internationally, and it must also have legal weight. several people in this house have previously mentioned the mad cow scandal as one of the many scandals we have seen in recent times. this scandal shows something extremely important, namely the importance of having the courage to make decisions even when we do not yet have all the evidence on the table. but there are other examples of mad cow disease, examples that may not be as spectacular, but where it's just as important that we make decisions. for example, we have the ethyl phthalates in children's toys. we have this strange idea of stuffing toys into candy, ice cream, and other edibles. in the first case we say that we do not have sufficient evidence, and in the second that there have not been enough serious accidents to dare to act. it is only to regret that it is not science but the internal market that determines what we are going to put on the market. this is an untenable situation. we can't have it this way. i support the whole of the report by mr patrie, but i would also like to point out what mr schörling has already mentioned in his speech, namely that we need a stricter approach to the burden of proof, particularly with regard to products without prior authorization. producers and manufacturers must be able to provide evidence that a product is actually safe. we must avoid consumers acting as some kind of paying guinea pigs when new products are brought to the market.
